C. SDCL Chapter 22-42 — Controlled Substances and Marijuana; Chapter 34-20B — Drug Control; Chapter 22-42A — Paraphernalia

Chapter index: sdlegislature.gov/api/Statutes/22-42.html, accessed 30 Oct 2026 (§§ 22-42-1 to -24).

§ 22-42-1 — “Marijuana” defined. Verbatim, as reproduced in the 2021 Marijuana Interim Study Committee presentation:

“‘[M]arijuana’ [is] all parts of any plant of the genus cannabis, whether growing or not, in its natural and unaltered state, except for drying or curing and crushing or crumbling. The term includes an altered state of marijuana absorbed into the human body. The term does not include fiber produced from the mature stalks of such plant, or oil or cake made from the seeds of such plant. The term does not include the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent on a dry weight basis.”

Source: mylrc 220552, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Note the tension: § 22-42-1 still says “delta-9” (not “total delta-9”) while ch. 38-35 uses “total delta-9”. Whether § 22-42-1 has since been conformed is Verify.

Schedules — SDCL ch. 34-20B (corrected 31 Oct 2026). Tetrahydrocannabinol is Schedule I under § 34-20B-14(20), except that occurring in industrial hemp as defined in § 38-35-1, in an FDA-approved drug product, or marijuana in its natural and unaltered state; hashish and hash oil are separately listed at § 34-20B-14(10). That express natural-marijuana exception is why AG Opinion 26-06 states South Dakota does not schedule marijuana under ch. 34-20B. Do not state flatly that “marijuana is Schedule I under South Dakota law.” The current schedule list, and whether the state schedule has been conformed to the federal Schedule III medical carve-out, is Verify — no conforming state rescheduling bill was located in this run, and the given federal baseline does not by itself amend SDCL ch. 34-20B. Do not state that South Dakota has rescheduled marijuana to Schedule III under state law.

§ 22-42-5 — Unauthorised possession of a controlled drug/substance as felony. Possession of a Schedule I/II substance (including concentrated marijuana/hash as a Schedule I substance in older fiscal notes) is a Class 5 felony (up to 5 years / $10,000). This is the provision behind the secondary claim “possession of one edible is a Class 5 felony” — that claim conflates an edible with plant marijuana under § 22-42-6; the charging theory for a non-patient edible is Verify on current practice, and the claim must not be stated as a freestanding rule without that qualification.

Other provisions (2021 interim presentation, Verify current text): § 22-42-5.1 ingestion (see §1.3); § 22-42-6 possession by amount (see §1.3); § 22-42-7 distribution / possession with intent (graduated by amount, roughly tracking § 22-42-6 tiers; distribution of ≤½ oz without consideration as Class 1 misdemeanor in older texts; distribution to a minor charged higher; mandatory sentences and school/youth-zone enhancement Verify); § 22-42-15 ingesting a substance (except alcohol) to become intoxicated as Class 1 misdemeanor; § 22-42-19 drug-free-zone / school-zone enhancement (violation within 1,000 feet of a school or 500 feet of a youth centre, swimming pool or video arcade as an additional Class 4 felony, per 2021 presentation — Verify); § 22-42-24 use in a motor vehicle as Class 2 misdemeanor (if enacted — Verify). § 22-42-10 keeping a place for use/sale — Class 5 felony; § 22-42-11 inhabiting a room where controlled substances illegally stored/used — Class 1 misdemeanor.

Chapter 22-42A — Paraphernalia. § 22-42A-3: knowing use/possession with intent to use drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyse, pack, repack, store, contain, conceal, inject, ingest, inhale or otherwise introduce a controlled substance or marijuana in violation of ch. 22-42 is a Class 2 misdemeanor (up to 30 days / $500). § 22-42A-4: delivery / possession with intent to deliver / manufacture with intent to deliver paraphernalia is a Class 6 felony (up to 2 years / $4,000) per 2021 interim presentation. § 34-20G-7(1) protects providing/selling paraphernalia to a cardholder, nonresident cardholder or establishment — paraphernalia for non-patients is not protected.

D. Hemp statutes — SDCL Chapter 38-35

See §1.6 for full treatment. Inventory: § 38-35-1 definitions (hemp, industrial hemp product, licensed hemp producer, chemically derived cannabinoid, total delta-9 THC); §§ 38-35-2 to -22 as amended/repealed by 2026 SB 39 (state grower/processor licences repealed eff. 1 Jan 2027; transport documentation retained); § 38-35-21 smoking prohibition, Class 1 misdemeanor, SL 2020 ch. 176 § 21, eff. 27 Mar 2020. Origin: 2020 HB 1008, signed 27 Mar 2020 (after a 2019 veto of an earlier hemp bill — secondary context, Verify on sdlegislature.gov). DOH hemp-product rules formerly under ch. 38-35 — current rule location after SB 39 is Verify for Run 2, Parts 7/11.

E. Administrative Rules of South Dakota (ARSD) Article 44:90 — Medical Cannabis

DOH index: doh.sd.gov/news-statutes/doh-statutes-rules — lists “SDCL 34-20G — Medical Cannabis” and “ARSD 44:90 — Medical Cannabis”, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Chapters, from 2021 Medical Marijuana Study Subcommittee minutes, 4 Aug 2021 (mylrc 220924): 44:90:01 definitions; 44:90:02 registry cards including caregivers; 44:90:03 registration certificates for establishments; 44:90:04 facilities and transport; 44:90:05 cultivation including pesticides/chemicals; 44:90:07 manufacturing facilities; 44:90:09 testing (mandatory tests; from 1 July 2023 heavy metals, pesticides, mycotoxins, solvents in addition to prior tests — per DOH “MC Minute” 16 June 2023, MC Minute 06-16-23); 44:90:10 packaging, labelling and advertising (see §1.7); 44:90:11 electronic inventory tracking (seed-to-sale) prescribed by DOH (see §1.8); 44:90:12 enforcement. Verify current ARSD 44:90 chapter list and any 2024–2026 amendments; the 2021 list may have gained/lost chapters (e.g. 44:90:06, 44:90:08). ARSD 44:90:10:12.01 is cited as “48 SDR 054, effective 11/15/2021” in Cornell LII — i.e. the original ARSD 44:90 package took effect in late 2021 (DOH briefing said “effective by October 29, 2021” as of April 2021 — the November date is the later, specific citation; Verify). August–October 2026: DOH filed 38 proposed amendments to ARSD art. 44:90 (hearing 23 Sept 2026, comments due 3 Oct, Interim Rules Review Committee 14 Oct 2026) — secondary, CannBus Q3 2026, CannBus. Verify amendment outcomes / digital-card status in current ARSD and DOH pages; the Oct 2026 committee outcome was not retrieved.

1.3 Possession penalties for non-patients, ingestion, cultivation, paraphernalia, public consumption, medical product forms and THC limits

General penalty scale (SDCL §§ 22-6-1, -6-2, as reproduced in secondary compilations of § 22-42-6 — Verify against current §§ 22-6-1/-2): Class 1 misdemeanor: up to 1 year county jail / $2,000. Class 2 misdemeanor: up to 30 days / $500. Class 6 felony: up to 2 years / $4,000. Class 5 felony: up to 5 years / $10,000. Class 4 felony: up to 10 years / $20,000. Class 3 felony: up to 15 years / $30,000.

Plant marijuana possession — § 22-42-6. Verbatim:

“No person may knowingly possess marijuana. It is a Class 1 misdemeanor to possess two ounces of marijuana or less. It is a Class 6 felony to possess more than two ounces of marijuana but less than one-half pound of marijuana. It is a Class 5 felony to possess one-half pound but less than one pound of marijuana. It is a Class 4 felony to possess one to ten pounds of marijuana. It is a Class 3 felony to possess more than ten pounds of marijuana. A civil penalty may be imposed, in addition to any criminal penalty, upon a conviction of a violation of this section not to exceed ten thousand dollars.”

Source: SDCL 22-42-6 via LawServer compilation of the official text, LawServer 22-42-6 — Verify against sdlegislature.gov § 22-42-6 page. Note: the medical allowable amount is 3 ounces, which is above the § 22-42-6 Class 1 / Class 6 threshold (2 oz) — a cardholder’s protection comes from ch. 34-20G, not from § 22-42-6. A non-patient with 3 oz is in Class 6 felony territory under § 22-42-6.

Amount (plant marijuana, non-patient)Classification
≤ 2 ozClass 1 misdemeanor
> 2 oz and < ½ lbClass 6 felony
½ lb to < 1 lbClass 5 felony
1 lb to 10 lbsClass 4 felony
> 10 lbsClass 3 felony

Concentrates / cannabis products — § 22-42-5. Unauthorised possession of a Schedule I substance (the category under which hash/concentrate has historically been charged) is a Class 5 felony. The precise charging treatment of a non-patient edible, tincture, vape cartridge or other manufactured product — whether charged under § 22-42-5, § 22-42-6, or both — is Verify; do not repeat the unqualified secondary shorthand “one edible = Class 5 felony” without that qualification.

Ingestion / internal possession — § 22-42-5.1 (current, post-SB 83) and § 22-42-15. Verbatim § 22-42-5.1, as amended by 2025 SB 83:

“No person may knowingly ingest a controlled drug or substance or have a controlled drug or substance in an altered state in the body unless the substance was obtained directly or pursuant to a valid prescription or order from a practitioner, acting in the course of the practitioner’s professional practice, or except as otherwise authorized by chapter 34-20B. [Penalties:] (1) A first violation is a Class 1 misdemeanor, and the court [shall order drug/alcohol evaluation and recommended treatment, with probation as the court deems best]; (2) A second violation is a Class 1 misdemeanor, [with supervised probation, evaluation and recommended treatment; the court may impose up to one year in jail]; and (3) A third or subsequent violation, occurring within [five years of the first conviction in the enrolled text; secondary summaries say ten years — Verify which window is in the enrolled SB 83], is a Class 6 felony.”

Source: mylrc Amendment 278751; South Dakota State Cannabis, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Critical nuance: pre-1 July 2025 conduct can still be prosecuted under the prior felony version; SB 83 is not retroactive and does not expunge. The older secondary claim that ingestion was “a Class 5 felony” is therefore correct only for pre-July 2025 Schedule I/II ingestion. § 22-42-15 (ingesting any substance except alcohol to become intoxicated) remains a separate Class 1 misdemeanor; its interaction with § 22-42-5.1 for marijuana specifically is Verify. Case law cited in secondary sources for metabolite-only convictions — State v. Schroeder (2004), State v. Whistler (2014) — is Verify (not opened in this run).

Non-patient cultivation. There is no non-patient home-grow right. Cultivation by a non-patient is prosecuted as possession/manufacture/distribution under ch. 22-42 / ch. 34-20B depending on amount and intent; no freestanding “cultivation” penalty tier separate from §§ 22-42-5/-6/-7 was located — Verify whether a manufacture-specific charge (SDCL 22-42-2) is the usual vehicle. Patient home cultivation is §1.4, not this paragraph.

Paraphernalia. See §1.2(C): § 22-42A-3 Class 2 misdemeanor (use/possession with intent to use); § 22-42A-4 Class 6 felony (delivery/manufacture with intent to deliver). Protected only for cardholders/nonresident cardholders/establishments under § 34-20G-7(1).

Public consumption / vehicles. § 34-20G-18 (below) denies protection for smoking/vaping on public transportation, in any public place or place open to the public, and (for under-21 cardholders) smoking/vaping at all. § 22-42-24 (if enacted) criminalises smoking in a vehicle on a public highway. Operating/navigating/being in actual physical control of a motor vehicle, aircraft, train or motorboat while under the influence is unprotected under § 34-20G-18(4), with the metabolite proviso below. Consumption on establishment property is prohibited by § 34-20G-68.

§ 34-20G-18 — Unauthorized conduct. Verbatim, as amended by 2022 SB 5:

“This chapter does not authorize any person to engage in, and does not prevent the imposition of any civil, criminal, or other penalty for engaging in, the following conduct: (1) Undertaking any task under the influence of cannabis, when doing so would constitute negligence or professional malpractice; (2) Possessing cannabis or otherwise engaging in the medical use of cannabis in any correctional facility; Note: no school-property / school-event / recreation-facility addition is in the current § 34-20G-18 as opened 31 Oct 2026 — the 2022 HB 1134 youth-protection bill that would have added such restrictions failed in the Senate (see audit). Current (2) is correctional facilities only.; (3) Smoking or vaping cannabis: (a) On any form of public transportation; or (b) In any public place or any place that is open to the public; or (c) If under the age of twenty-one; (4) Operating, navigating, or being in actual physical control of any motor vehicle, aircraft, train, or motorboat while under the influence of cannabis; or (5) Performing any safety-sensitive job under the influence of cannabis.”
Proviso (in § 34-20G-18 and related texts): a registered qualifying patient or nonresident cardholder “is not considered to be under the influence of cannabis solely because of the presence of metabolites or components of cannabis that appear in insufficient concentration to cause impairment.”

Source: mylrc 232289, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Allowed medical product forms and THC limits — current law:

  • Forms allowed: “Cannabis products” expressly include “edible cannabis products, beverages, topical products, ointments, oils, and tinctures” plus concentrated cannabis and extracts (§ 34-20G-1(3)). Flower, pre-rolls, concentrates, edibles, beverages, tinctures/oils and topicals are therefore all within the medical programme’s product definition; no form is excluded by statute. The failed 2022 HB 1058 (edibles ban) confirms edibles were not removed.
  • THC limits — statute: No statutory THC potency cap was located in ch. 34-20G. The proposed caps in 2026 SB 194 — 30% flower, 60% liquid concentrate, 5 mg/serving and 100 mg/container edibles, 5% cannabis oil — did not pass and must be labelled PROPOSED/DEAD, never as current limits. Source for proposed text: mylrc Bill 299491, accessed 30 Oct 2026.
  • THC limits — rules: ARSD 44:90:10:03 (edible packaging/limits), :04 (tinctures/edible oils), :05 (beverages) exist per DOH inspection reports, but their mg/serving/package numbers were not extracted in this run — Verify before stating any rule-level edible limit. DOH inspection reports confirm the labelling/packaging framework — see §1.7.
  • Purchase/possession amount for products: the quantity of cannabis products is “as established by rules promulgated by the department under § 34-20G-72” (§ 34-20G-1(1)(b)) — i.e. the statute delegates the product amount to DOH rules, and the DOH rolling 14-day 3-oz flower limit (§1.4) is the operational limit. The product-equivalency table (how many mg of concentrate/edible THC equals 1 oz of flower) is Verify from ARSD 44:90 / DOH bulletin — secondary trackers assert equivalencies but no official table was opened in this run.

1.4 Medical programme

Qualifying conditions — § 34-20G-1(8). Two routes: (1) Symptom route: “A chronic or debilitating disease or medical condition or its treatment that produces one or more of the following: cachexia or wasting syndrome; severe, debilitating pain; severe nausea, except nausea associated with pregnancy; seizures; or severe and persistent muscle spasms” — deliberately broad, not limited to a named-disease list. (2) Named conditions (post-2023 SB 1 text, lettering Verify): acquired immune deficiency syndrome / HIV-positive status; amyotrophic lateral sclerosis (ALS); multiple sclerosis; cancer or its treatment if associated with severe/chronic pain, nausea/severe vomiting, or cachexia/severe wasting; Crohn’s disease; epilepsy and seizures; and post-traumatic stress disorder (PTSD) — glaucoma is NOT in § 34-20G-1(8) as opened on 31 Oct 2026; do not list it as a named condition. Older secondary lists that omit PTSD, or that still describe a DOH petition process under repealed § 34-20G-26, are outdated.

Practitioner certification: Who: physician, physician assistant, or advanced practice registered nurse, licensed with authority to prescribe drugs to humans (§ 34-20G-1, “Practitioner”). How: bona fide practitioner-patient relationship (in-person physical examination at initial visit; patient under practitioner’s care for the qualifying condition or referred to the certifying practitioner by the caring practitioner; reasonable expectation of continuing care — Verify (c)). Written certification: dated/signed document stating the patient has a qualifying debilitating condition/symptom, the benefit/risk assessment, and the certification expiration date per § 34-20G-43. Process: practitioner creates the certification in the DOH portal (medcannabisapplication.sd.gov); the patient cannot start an application until the practitioner has certified — the certification email creates the patient account. DOH does not maintain a public list of certifying practitioners (DOH FAQ, via search-indexed DOH FAQ). Restrictions Verify: § 34-20G-5.1, § 34-20G-5.2, § 34-20G-78.2 (clinic financial relationship / referral prohibition), and the pregnancy/breastfeeding prohibition (2023 ch. 120).

Patient and caregiver cards and fees (DOH Step-by-Step Guide for Patients, last revised 17 Dec 2024, and DOH FAQ): Standard registry card fee: $75, non-refundable, for initial and renewal. Low-income fee: $20 where gross monthly household income is <130% of the federal poverty level, with income documentation. Home-cultivation 2-part card: additional $20 (required if cultivation is elected). Additional designated caregiver after the primary: $20 per additional caregiver card; the $75 patient fee covers the primary caregiver designation at initial/renewal application. Payment via the portal (PayPal in the 2024 guide, with a 3.49% convenience fee + $0.49 transaction fee stated in that guide — Verify current payment methods/fees, as the guide is dated Dec 2024). Application requires: unexpired photo ID (driver’s licence, passport, US government ID, tribal ID, student ID per DOH FAQ), passport-quality photo, and the practitioner’s certification already on file. DOH verification/issuance under § 34-20G-31; secure verification system under § 34-20G-45. Card validity/expiration under § 34-20G-43 — Verify current term (secondary sources commonly state 1 year; the statute/rule term was not confirmed in this run — do not assert “annual” without that check, although the fee is described as initial/renewal). Caregiver: at least 21, background check under § 34-20G-32, assists no more than 5 patients (facility exception — Verify). Sources: DOH Step-by-Step Guide, doh.sd.gov step-by-step guide, accessed 30 Oct 2026; DOH FAQ via secondary compilation, South Dakota State Cannabis (24 Sept 2026) — Verify fees against current DOH/ARSD 44:90:02.

Purchase and possession limits: Possession: 3 oz of cannabis or less, plus the DOH-rule quantity of cannabis products (§ 34-20G-1(1)(a)–(b)). Purchase (DOH bulletin, 16 Nov 2023, via medcannabis.sd.gov News): rolling 14-day limit of 3 oz of flower. “The 14-day rolling limit is tracked when medical cannabis is physically dispensed to a Patient or Caregiver. The amounts purchased are subtracted from the three-ounce total. Once 14 days have elapsed from the date of a purchase, that specific purchase amount is added back to the three-ounce limit. This same process applies for each purchase made by the Patient or Caregiver.” Source: Medcannabis News, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — Verify whether the rolling limit is still 14 days and how product equivalencies are counted in the current bulletin. Dispensary dispensing conditions: § 34-20G-70 and § 34-20G-71. Nonresident cardholders designate no more than two dispensaries under § 34-20G-72 rulemaking.

Patient home cultivation — plant limits and extra certification: Limit: 2 flowering plants + 2 non-flowering plants, only if the registry identification card allows cultivation (§ 34-20G-1(1)(c)). Plus: the amount produced from those allowable plants, if possessed at the same property where the plants were cultivated (§ 34-20G-1(1)(d)) — i.e. home-grow product above 3 oz is protected only on the grow property. Conditions: plants must be cultivated in an enclosed, locked facility (§ 34-20G-2) or being transported; DOH application requires indicating who will cultivate (patient, caregiver, or both) and submitting a diagram/floor plan, a photo of the lock on the room/area door, and a photo of the room/area; additional $20 fee for the 2-part card; cultivation may be added outside the renewal cycle via the portal (“Add Cultivation” tab) per the Dec 2024 guide. No separate “extra practitioner certification” for cultivation beyond the card allowing cultivation was located — the cultivation election is part of the card application. Verify whether ARSD 44:90:02 imposes any additional practitioner statement for cultivation. The 2022 SB 24 history matters: the legislature considered prohibiting cardholder cultivation entirely and instead enacted the 2+2 maximum. Any claim that patients may grow “3 plants” or “4 flowering plants” or “up to 6 plants” in secondary summaries is wrong for South Dakota — the limit is 2+2.

Minors’ rules: Under 18: DOH may not issue a card unless the custodial parent / approved guardian with responsibility for medical decisions consents in writing (§ 34-20G-33; § 34-20G-30 for the decision-maker’s information submission). A designated caregiver is required in practice for minor patients. Under 21: may not smoke or vape cannabis (§ 34-20G-18(3)(c)), even if cardholding. Other forms are not barred by that paragraph — Verify whether ARSD imposes additional under-21 product restrictions. Schools: § 34-20G-95; § 34-20G-19; § 34-20G-18(2) school-property provisions (Verify current lettering). Full school rules are Part 12 (Run 2).

Out-of-state card recognition: Yes, with DOH confirmation — not by mere possession of another state’s card. Definition, from 2024 SB 141 text quoting the then-current definition:

“‘Nonresident cardholder,’ a person who: (a) Has been diagnosed with a debilitating medical condition, or is the parent, guardian, conservator, or other person with authority to consent to the medical treatment of a person who has been diagnosed with a debilitating medical condition; (b) Is not a resident of this state or who has been a resident of this state for fewer than forty-five days …; (c) Was issued a currently valid registry identification card or its equivalent by another state, district, territory, commonwealth, insular possession of the United States, or country recognized by the United States that allows the person to use cannabis for medical purposes in the jurisdiction of issuance [and, per § 34-20G-72 rulemaking and secondary DOH guidance, has submitted documentation to DOH and received confirmation of registration — Verify the exact DOH nonresident procedure and fee, if any].”

Protection (§ 34-20G-3, verbatim in §1.2): transporting, purchasing, possessing or using medical cannabis in accordance with ch. 34-20G, up to 3 oz + DOH product amount. The protection expressly includes purchasing — i.e. a confirmed nonresident cardholder may purchase from a South Dakota dispensary (subject to designating no more than two dispensaries under DOH rules), contrary to looser secondary summaries that say “possession only, no purchase.” Verify the two-dispensary designation procedure in current ARSD.

Tribal medical cards — including non-members — and the state’s position: Current state law (as of 30 Oct 2026): the nonresident-cardholder definition as quoted above lists “another state, district, territory, commonwealth, insular possession … or country” — it does not list a tribe. SB 141 (2024) would have added: “or resides within the boundaries of an Indian reservation and was issued a currently valid registry identification card or its equivalent by a tribe that allows the person to use cannabis for medical purposes in the jurisdiction of the reservation.” That bill died (deferred to 41st day, 5–1, 29 Jan 2024). No later tribal-card recognition bill was located as enacted in this run. Consequence: a tribal medical card, on its own, is not a state-recognised nonresident card under current SDCL ch. 34-20G as far as this run could confirm. Whether DOH, the Attorney General, or a court has taken a different position by opinion, guidance, or non-enforcement practice is Verify — no AG opinion or court case on tribal cards was located in this run, and none should be invented. Tribal-land practice (secondary, for Part 10): Flandreau’s Native Nations dispensary is reported to accept medical cards from any state/jurisdiction for purchase on tribal land under tribal law — that is a tribal-law acceptance rule, not state recognition off-reservation. A non-member who buys on tribal land and carries product off-reservation into state jurisdiction is outside ch. 34-20G protection unless independently a state cardholder or confirmed nonresident cardholder. Verify tribal ordinances in Part 10. Federal Schedule III tribal question: Verify — see Part 0, H2. No conclusion is offered here.

1.5 Amendment A — 2020 adult-use constitutional amendment and its striking down

Measure: Constitutional Amendment A (2020), in substance “An amendment to the South Dakota Constitution to legalize, regulate, and tax marijuana; and to require the Legislature to pass laws regarding hemp as well as laws ensuring access to marijuana for medical use.” Vote: approved by voters 3 Nov 2020, 54.18% yes / 45.82% no (secondary compilation; Verify against Secretary of State official canvass). It was on the same ballot as IM 26 (medical, ~70% yes).

Challenge: Pennington County Sheriff Kevin Thom and South Dakota Highway Patrol Superintendent (Col.) Rick Miller filed an election contest and a declaratory-judgment action against Secretary of State Steve Barnett. Circuit court (Sixth Circuit, Nos. 32CIV20-186 / 32CIV20-187, decisions 8 Feb 2021) dismissed the election contest as an improper vehicle but held Amendment A unconstitutional (single-subject and revision grounds). Governor Kristi Noem ratified the declaratory-judgment action (executive order, 8 Jan 2021 per secondary reporting), curing the standing defect.

Supreme Court decision — from the Court’s own press release: Case: Thom, Miller v. Barnett and In re Election Contest as to Amendment A, South Dakota Supreme Court, decision issued 24 November 2021, 4–1. Citation: Thom v. Barnett, 2021 S.D. 65 (secondary citation; Verify against UJS opinion). Justia docket reference 29546: law.justia.com 29546.

“In a four-to-one decision, the Court held that Amendment A, as submitted to the voters in the November 2020 general election, violated the single subject requirement in the South Dakota Constitution. As a result of the constitutional violation, the Court has declared the amendment invalid.” The three subjects were: “(1) Recreational Marijuana — by creating a comprehensive plan to constitutionally legalize, regulate, and tax marijuana for all persons at least twenty-one years of age; (2) Hemp — by constitutionally mandating that the Legislature pass laws regarding hemp; and (3) Medical Marijuana — by constitutionally mandating that the Legislature pass laws ensuring access to marijuana for limited medical use for qualifying persons.” The majority reasoned each had “separate objects and purposes, which were not dependent upon or connected with each other,” so voters could not separately vote on each distinct subject, violating S.D. Const. art. XXIII, § 1. The Court unanimously held the election contest was properly dismissed, Thom/Miller lacked standing in their official capacities but the Governor’s ratification allowed the action to proceed as if commenced by the Governor, and the challenge was not required to be brought before the election. A separate opinion concurred in part and dissented in part on initiative history.

Source: UJS press release, 24 Nov 2021, ujs.sd.gov, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Effect: Amendment A never took effect as law; no adult-use licensing, taxation or retail system was created under it; IM 26 (a statute, not a constitutional amendment) was unaffected and proceeded to its 1 July 2021 effective date. Later adult-use attempts — 2022 IM 27 and 2024 IM 29 — both failed at the ballot (IM 29 reported ~44% yes / 55% no — Verify against SOS canvass in Part 9). South Dakota is therefore the only state where voters approved adult-use legalisation and a court invalidated it before sales (secondary characterisation, widely repeated; the factual predicate — approval + invalidation — is established above).

1.6 Hemp and intoxicating hemp

Definition and THC measurement — SDCL 38-35-1 (as amended by 2026 SB 39, eff. 1 Jan 2027; pre-2027 text substantively identical on the THC threshold). Verbatim, 2026 SB 39 enrolled / session law:

“(1) ‘Hemp’ or ‘industrial hemp,’ the plant Cannabis sativa L. and any part of that plant, including the seeds thereof and all derivatives, extracts, cannabinoids, isomers, acids, salts, and salts of isomers, whether growing or not, with a total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent on a dry weight basis;”
“(2) ‘Industrial hemp product,’ a finished manufactured product, or consumer product made from industrial hemp with a total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration of not more than three-tenths of one percent, derived from or made by processing industrial hemp. This term does not include a product containing any of the following chemically derived cannabinoids: (a) Delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol, also known as delta-8-THC; (b) Delta-10 tetrahydrocannabinol, also known as delta-10-THC; (c) Tetrahydrocannabinol acetate, also known as THC-O-acetate or THC-O; (d) Hexahydrocannabinol, also known as HHC; or (e) Tetrahydrocannabiphoral, also known as THCP;”
“‘Total delta-9 THC or total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol,’ the value determined after the process of decarboxylation, or the application of a conversion factor if the testing methodology does not include decarboxylation, that expresses the potential total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol content derived from the sum of the THC and THCA content and reported on a dry weight basis;”

Sources: mylrc Bill 306612; Session Laws 306615; pre-SB 39 text via Justia 2025 compilation, Justia 38-35-1, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

What this means for measurement: South Dakota already uses total delta-9 (THC + THCA, decarboxylated/converted) — a delta-9-only lab result that ignores THCA is insufficient in South Dakota. This predates the federal total-THC redefinition and is independent of it.

Product / cannabinoidSouth Dakota status at 30 Oct 2026Authority
Naturally occurring delta-9 THC in hemp / hemp product at ≤0.3% total delta-9, dry weight, not chemically derived, not for smoking/inhalingLawful as hemp (subject to food/drug/cosmetic and other applicable law)SDCL 38-35-1
THCA in hemp, counted toward total delta-9Counted — if total delta-9 (THC+THCA) exceeds 0.3%, the material is not hemp; it is marijuana under ch. 22-42. There is no separate “THCA loophole” in South Dakota law. High-THCA flower sold as “hemp” that exceeds 0.3% total delta-9 is marijuana, not hemp.SDCL 38-35-1 (“total delta-9”); SDCL 22-42-1
Delta-8 THC, delta-10 THC, THC-O, HHC, THCP when chemically derivedBanned — excluded from “industrial hemp product” and independently prohibited by SDCL 34-20B-118.SDCL 38-35-1; SDCL 34-20B-118
Chemically derived cannabinoids generally (conversion of hemp/CBD into any THC isomer/analog/derivative)Prohibited — see verbatim § 34-20B-118 below.SDCL 34-20B-118 (HB 1125, 2024)
Non-psychoactive cannabinoids (e.g. CBD) in compliant hemp product, non-inhalableLawful within the 0.3% total delta-9 limit; CBD itself is not the banned category. The “chemically derived cannabinoid” definition expressly excludes non-psychoactive cannabinoids and decarboxylation without a chemical catalyst, and cannabinoids in a topical cream product (per SB 61/1125 texts — Verify current exclusion lettering in § 38-35-1 / § 34-20B-1(3)).SDCL 38-35-1; HB 1125 texts
Smokable / inhalable hemp (flower, pre-rolls, vapes), even at ≤0.3%Prohibited — Class 1 misdemeanor, regardless of THC compliance.SDCL 38-35-21
Hemp-derived THC beveragesNo hemp-beverage-specific statute was located. A beverage that is a compliant hemp product (≤0.3% total delta-9, not chemically derived, not inhalable) falls under the general hemp-product rules; a beverage containing chemically derived THC or exceeding the limit does not. Whether DOH/DANR food/beverage rules impose additional labelling, serving-size or sales-channel restrictions is Verify — do not assert a beverage ban or allowance beyond this paragraph. Medical cannabis beverages, by contrast, are expressly within § 34-20G-1(3).Verify — DANR/DOH food rules

SDCL 34-20B-118 — verbatim operative text (current law, HB 1125; as reproduced in the federal-court undisputed-facts filing and in 2026 SB 61’s amendment base):

“No person or entity may: (1) Chemically modify or convert industrial hemp as defined in § 38-35-1, or engage in any process that converts cannabidiol, into delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol, delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol, delta-10 tetrahydrocannabinol, or any other tetrahydrocannabinol isomer, analog, or derivative; or (2) Sell or distribute industrial hemp or an industrial hemp product that contains chemically derived cannabinoids or cannabinoids created by chemically modifying or converting a hemp extract. A violation of this section is a Class 2 misdemeanor.”

Sources: D.S.D. filing (citing SDCL 34-20B-118; the filing also cites the original codification as SDCL 34-20B-1(3) — renumbering history is Verify); mylrc 293027, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Notes on § 34-20B-118 scope — read carefully: The current ban covers chemical modification/conversion and sale/distribution of products containing chemically derived cannabinoids. It does not, on its face, criminalise mere possession/consumption by an end user — that broader possession/consumption ban was proposed in SB 61 (2026) and failed. Secondary summaries that say HB 1125 “prohibits possession” of delta-8 conflate HB 1125 with failed SB 61 and must not be repeated. Penalty: Class 2 misdemeanor (up to 30 days / $500) per violation; Pennington County enforcement guidance (June 2025 letter, secondary reporting) treats each non-compliant package/product as a separate violation and provides for seizure — that enforcement position is not in the statute text and is flagged as secondary. Litigation: Hemp Quarters 605 LLC v. [State], D.S.D. No. 3:24-cv-03016, challenged HB 1125 under the 2018 Farm Bill, Commerce Clause, takings and vagueness theories. Summary-judgment briefing/order dated 14 July 2025 (Docs. 42–43) is located; the final disposition (whether HB 1125 was upheld in full) was not confirmed in this run — Verify the final judgment before stating that HB 1125 “survived challenge.” Do not cite the challenge as invalidating HB 1125. Enforcement data point (secondary): “more than 100 charges” under the hemp laws statewide since July 2024, per UJS data cited in Rapid City Sentinel reporting, including March 2026 Pennington County charges against two Rapid City businesses under § 38-35-21 for pre-rolled hemp. Treat as secondary, dated 2026.

SDCL 38-35-21 — Industrial hemp for smoking prohibited. Verbatim:

“The sale or use of industrial hemp for smoking or inhaling is prohibited. A violation of this section is a Class 1 misdemeanor. Source: SL 2020, ch 176, § 21, eff. Mar. 27, 2020.”

Source: sdlegislature.gov 38-35-21, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Minimum age — hemp: Medical cannabis: cardholders under 21 may not smoke/vape (§ 34-20G-18(3)(c)); no general “21 to buy medical” rule beyond cardholding and the smoking/vaping bar. Hemp-derived THC consumables: reported 2026 SB 45 under-21 sales restriction (see H7) — Verify citation, products covered, and exceptions. Secondary summaries agree the restriction covers specified consumable THC products (delta-8, delta-9 THCA, THC-O, HHC) and does not impose a general 21+ rule on non-intoxicating CBD. Do not apply a single “18 or 21 for all hemp” rule. Smokable hemp: prohibited for everyone (§ 38-35-21), so no age threshold saves it.

Testing and labelling — hemp: Pre-harvest / compliance testing: DANR (pre-2027) / USDA-or-tribal licensee testing thereafter; DEA-registered laboratory testing for hemp is the federal framework, with USDA enforcement delayed to 31 Dec 2026 (given baseline). South Dakota’s own testing rule — “All [DANR] compliance testing shall [be] conducted by a laboratory approved by the Drug Enforcement Administration. The laboratory shall report the total delta-9 [THC] concentration level and the measurement of uncertainty” (2022 SB 201 text amending § 38-35-14 — Verify current section after SB 39 repeals). Non-compliant hemp (total delta-9 >0.3%) is subject to destruction / marijuana prosecution under ch. 22-42 (2022 SB 201 text: material “determined to meet the definition of marijuana is subject to prosecution and penalties for possession or distribution of marijuana under chapter 22-42” — Verify current destruction procedure). No comprehensive South Dakota finished-product testing/labelling statute was located beyond the definitions, the conversion ban, and the smoking ban. Food/drug/cosmetic law may apply, but no hemp-specific label statute is asserted here — Verify in Part 7/11 (DANR/DOH rules, SDCL ch. 34-20B food provisions).

State response to the new federal hemp definition and the 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026 dates: No South Dakota statute or published DANR/DOH guidance responding to PL 119-37 §781 / PL 119-103 §2019 was located in this run — Verify. The interaction must be stated as follows, and no further: (1) On the given federal baseline, after 11 Dec 2026 the federal hemp definition becomes a full total-THC definition with a 0.4 mg total THC per container cap for hemp products, excluding cannabinoids synthesised outside the plant. (2) South Dakota already bans chemically derived/synthesised cannabinoids (SDCL 34-20B-118) and already uses total delta-9 measurement (SDCL 38-35-1), so the federal “synthesised outside the plant” exclusion and total-THC approach are consistent with, but do not replace, South Dakota law. (3) South Dakota has not enacted the 0.4 mg per-container cap as state law (SB 61, which would have done so, failed). Whether the federal cap, once effective, pre-empts or effectively governs South Dakota retail hemp products, and whether South Dakota will conform its § 38-35-1 definition in the 2027 session, is Verify — it depends on federal pre-emption / Farm Bill construction that this run does not decide. (4) Separately, South Dakota’s own hemp-licensing transfer (SB 39, eff. 1 Jan 2027) will already have moved production licensing to USDA/tribal programmes before the federal cap’s 11 Dec 2026 date has fully played out in the 2027 growing season — the two timelines overlap but are legally distinct. (5) The Attorney General’s August 2026 position (secondary reporting): AG Marty Jackley joined a 35-state AG letter (4 Aug 2026) urging Congress to keep the stricter federal redefinition and not delay/weaken/repeal it. That is an advocacy position, not South Dakota law. Source (secondary): Rapid City Sentinel, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

1.7 Packaging, labelling, advertising, delivery, zoning and buffer distances

Packaging — ARSD 44:90:10:01:01 to :06 (framework confirmed by DOH inspection reports): Exit packaging: child-resistant, tamper-proof, resealable, and opaque (DOH inspection item 3A(a), citing ARSD 44:90:10:01:01–06). Flower packaging per 44:90:10:02; edible packaging/limits per :03; tinctures/edible oils per :04; beverages per :05; topicals per :06. Specific gram/mg package limits under :03–:05 were not extracted — Verify. Sources (DOH inspection reports, official DOH PDFs, accessed 30 Oct 2026): 20250624 South Dakota Cannabis Company, Colman; 20250714 Butte Valley Farms, Newell; 20250609 Trailshead, Deadwood.

Labelling — ARSD 44:90:10:07 to :14, :12.01 (verbatim warnings): All products: required labelling or establishment-printed exit labels (44:90:10:07); clear, ≥6-point font, nothing required covered/obstructed (:08); mandated testing results including THC in mg and % (:09); onset time and duration of effects + effects warning (:10); pesticides used in cultivation, all ingredients, solvents used in extraction, allergen warning (:11); all requirements in :12; warning symbols/labels in :12.01; exit-packaging identifying information (:13); prohibited labelling in :14. Verbatim warnings (ARSD 44:90:10:12.01):

“The department shall design a standard symbol that indicates an item contains cannabis or cannabis extract that shall be used by all registered establishments. This standard symbol must appear on the front or most predominantly displayed area of the marketing layer of an edible cannabis product, no smaller than 1/2 inch by 1/2 inch. Labels must contain the following warning statements in no smaller than six-point font: (1) For all cannabis and cannabis products: (a) Contains cannabis. For medical use by qualifying patients only. There may be health risks associated with the use of this product. There may be additional health risks associated with the use of this product for women who are pregnant, breastfeeding, or planning on becoming pregnant. Do not drive a motor vehicle or operate heavy machinery while using this product.; (b) Cannabis has a high potential for abuse. This product has not been approved by the United States Food and Drug Administration for preventing or treating any condition or disease process.; (2) For all cannabis flower and trim, including pre-rolled cannabis cigarettes: Not for retail sale to persons under 21 years of age.; and (3) For all inhalable cannabis products: Possession by persons under 21 years old is illegal.”

Source: Cornell LII 44:90:10:12.01, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Note: the “(b) high potential for abuse / not FDA-approved” warning and the “Not for retail sale to persons under 21” flower warning predate the federal Schedule III medical move; whether DOH has revised them after 28 Apr 2026 is Verify. Do not treat the warning text as proof of current federal scheduling.

Advertising — ARSD 44:90:10:14:01 to :19 (see Part 0 H3): General rule (from 2021 DOH local-government briefing and Cornell text): advertisements must be targeted as directly as possible to other establishments and cardholders over 21. Prohibited forms (ARSD 44:90:10:14.01, verbatim core):

“Unless and until the United States Drug Enforcement Administration removes marijuana or cannabis as a Schedule I controlled substance, no establishment may advertise: (1) On a sign or billboard, except that a dispensary may advertise on signs on its own premises; (2) By distributing handbills in public areas or on publicly owned property; (3) Through direct mail, phone, text, or email without verifying the recipient is a cardholder or medical cannabis establishment and offering a permanent opt-out feature; (4) Through publication, except that a dispensary may advertise on publications within its own premises; (5) Through radio, television, and other media; or (6) Through a practitioner or health care facility, by placing advertising material at a practitioner’s office or health care facility, or by targeting the practitioner’s or health care facility’s patients through direct mail, phone, text, or email.”

Source: Cornell LII 44:90:10:14.01 (48 SDR 054, eff. 15 Nov 2021; General Authority SDCL 34-20G-72(5)(i)) — Verify current text and the Schedule III interaction (Part 0, H3). DOH “MC Minute” (June 2023) also flags sponsored social-media ads under ARSD 44:90:10:16–:18 — Verify current social-media rules.

Delivery: No patient-delivery right was located. Secondary business guides state: “Medical cannabis can be ordered online in South Dakota but delivery of medical cannabis is illegal. Customers must visit the dispensary to pick up the order they made online.” That is secondary only — Verify against ARSD 44:90:04 (facilities/transport) and any DOH delivery guidance. Establishment-to-establishment transport is regulated under ARSD 44:90:04 and SDCL 34-20G-63/-64 (security/transport), which is a different activity from delivery to a patient. Do not state “delivery is banned by [statute]” without a citation. Source (secondary): CannaBusinessPlans, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Zoning and buffers — state level: State school buffer: DOH establishment checklists require “Allowable Location SDCL 34-20G-55 • Not within 1,000 feet of a public or private school.” Sources: DOH Dispensary Checklist, 2025 Dispensary Checklist; Cultivation Checklist, 2025 Cultivation Checklist; Testing Checklist, Testing Checklist, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Local zoning/buffers: See § 34-20G-58 (verbatim below) and Part 3. Local governments may impose time/place/manner restrictions, caps, proximity limits to sensitive land uses, and local licence/permit/registration requirements with reasonable fees — but may not prohibit dispensaries outright (§ 34-20G-59).

§ 34-20G-58 — Local ordinances (verbatim, as indexed). Source: sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-58, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — note: subsection (3) is omitted in the search excerpt (“…”) — Verify full text; it is presumed to concern hours or a related time/place/manner item, but it is not quoted here for that reason:

“The governing body of a municipality may enact an ordinance not in conflict with this chapter, regardless of whether it has enacted a zoning ordinance pursuant to title 11, imposing: (1) Restrictions on a medical cannabis establishment to govern the time, place, and manner of operation; (2) A limit on the number of medical cannabis establishments in the municipality; … (4) Limitations on the proximity of a medical cannabis establishment to: (a) Any sensitive land-use area, including a childcare facility, park, public service facility, recreational facility, religious facility, school, and any location frequented by individuals under the age of twenty-one; or … (5) Requirements for a medical cannabis establishment to obtain a local license, permit, or registration to operate; or (6) Reasonable fees for any local license, permit, or registration. The governing body of a county may enact an ordinance governing all matters set forth in this section. The county ordinance applies throughout its jurisdiction, except within the boundaries of a municipality that has enacted an ordinance in accordance with this section. A county or municipality may impose a civil penalty for the violation of an ordinance enacted in accordance with this section. Source: Initiated Measure No. 26, approved Nov. 3, 2020, eff. Jul. 1, 2021; SL 2024, ch 137, § 2.”

Local example (illustrative only, not state law): City of Brandon, Title 17, requires establishments ≥1,000 feet from any public/private school (radial straight-line measurement) and otherwise 300 feet between cannabis establishments (shared-location exception per ARSD 44:90:04:04). Source: City of Brandon Ch. 17, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Local rules vary by jurisdiction and are Part 3.

1.8 Seed-to-sale tracking system

System: Metrc (Marijuana Enforcement Tracking Reporting and Compliance), RFID-based seed-to-sale tracking, contracted by DOH. Metrc’s own South Dakota page: “Metrc will serve as the seed-to-sale tracking system for the State of South Dakota cannabis program.” / “The South Dakota Medical Cannabis Program welcomes you to the Metrc webpage.” Source: Metrc South Dakota (published 27 Jan 2022, updated 2 June 2026 per that page), accessed 30 Oct 2026. ARSD 44:90:11 “requires the use of electronic inventory tracking (seed to sale) prescribed by DOH” (2021 subcommittee minutes). DOH MC Minute (June 2023) reminds operators that “vape cartridges need to be entered and tracked in milligrams, not grams (ARSD 44:90:11:03).” Secondary compliance summaries add two operational points that are Verify against ARSD 44:90:11 / DOH guidance before republication: (a) South Dakota has “expressly prohibited integration with the state patient registry, so purchase limits are not tracked in any state system” and the point of sale must enforce limits internally; (b) all licensed businesses are required to use Metrc for inventory/reporting. The Metrc contract itself (AP reporting, 2022: “The South Dakota Department of Health has hired a New York-based software company… Metrc will use radio-frequency identification to track cannabis from seed to sale”) corroborates (b) in general terms. Sources (secondary): Dutchie; Distru, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

1.9 Employment and drug-testing protections for patients

Core protection — § 34-20G-22 (verbatim, current text as amended by 2024 SB 12, SL 2024 ch. 135 § 1). Source: sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-22; 2024 SB 12 enrolled/session law, Session Law 264684, accessed 30 Oct 2026:

“Except as otherwise provided in this chapter, a registered qualifying patient who uses cannabis for a medical purpose must be afforded the same rights under state and local law, as the person would be afforded if the person were solely prescribed a pharmaceutical medication, as it pertains to: (1) Any interaction with a person’s employer; (2) Drug testing by a person’s employer; or (3) Drug testing required by any state or local law, agency, or government official. Nothing in this section prohibits adverse employment action, based solely on a positive test result for cannabis metabolites, if the person is employed in a safety-sensitive job. Nothing in this section prohibits an employer from refusing to hire a person, based solely on a positive test result for cannabis metabolites, if the person is seeking employment in a safety-sensitive job.”

Workplace limits — § 34-20G-24 (as amended by 2024 SB 12):

“No employer is required to allow the ingestion, possession, transfer, display, or transportation of cannabis in any workplace or to allow any employee to work while under the influence of cannabis. No employer is prohibited from establishing and enforcing a drug-free workplace policy, which may include a drug testing program that complies with state and federal law, or acting with respect to an applicant or employee under the policy. No cause of action is created for employment discrimination or wrongful termination arising from an employer’s enforcement of a drug-free workplace policy in compliance with this chapter.”

Source: 2024 SB 12 text, mylrc 264681, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Related provisions (titles confirmed from chapter index; content Verify where noted): § 34-20G-23 conflict with employer’s obligations or benefits under federal law (federal-law / federal-contract / federal-benefit conflict preserves employer action — Verify text); § 34-20G-25 schools, landlords, and employers not to be penalised (for complying with ch. 34-20G — Verify text); § 34-20G-25.1 healthcare and accredited prevention/treatment facilities; § 34-20G-27 no requirement to reimburse costs, permit smoking/vaping on property, permit cultivation on rental property, or permit medical-cannabis conduct in public buildings; § 34-20G-28 discipline for ingestion at workplace and working under the influence permitted; § 34-20G-18(5) performing a safety-sensitive job under the influence is unprotected; § 34-20G-61/-62 establishment employment: background checks and hiring restrictions (Part 4/11 detail; Baker Donelson employment guide, Nov 2025, confirms § 34-20G-61 as a mandatory-background-check category: Baker Donelson).

Plain-English summary — what the protection does and does not do: Does: treat a registered patient, for employer-interaction and drug-testing purposes under state/local law, as if prescribed a pharmaceutical — except in safety-sensitive jobs, where an employer may take adverse action or refuse to hire based solely on a positive metabolite test. Does not: require any employer to allow cannabis in the workplace, allow working under the influence, or forgo a compliant drug-free workplace policy; create a discrimination/wrongful-termination claim for enforcement of a compliant policy; or override federal obligations (e.g. federal DOT / federal-contract testing). The pre-2024 version of § 34-20G-22 (without the safety-sensitive carve-outs) is superseded — do not quote the 2022 Justia version (“shall be afforded all the same rights…”) as current law. Federal Schedule III interaction (Verify): whether the federal Schedule III move for state-licensed medical marijuana changes any employer’s federal-law obligations (e.g. DOT, federal contractors) is outside the given baseline and is Verify — the baseline addresses scheduling and §280E, not employment testing.

PART 2 — TAXES

2.2 State sales tax on medical cannabis — 4.2%, no medical exemption, no cannabis excise

Current rate and statute — opened directly. SDCL 10-45-2, opened directly at sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/10-45-2, accessed 30 Oct 2026, reads in its current, reduced-rate version:

“There is hereby imposed a tax upon the privilege of engaging in business as a retailer, a tax of four and two-tenths percent upon the gross receipts of all sales of tangible personal property consisting of goods, wares, or merchandise, except as otherwise provided in this chapter, sold at retail in the state to consumers or users.”

Source history on that page: SL 2023, ch. 32, § 1 — the 2023 reduction. The same page also displays the 4.5% version, reflecting the sunset: SL 2023, ch. 32, §§ 1, 19. Plain English: medical cannabis sold at retail is taxed as tangible personal property at the general rate. There is no cannabis-specific rate in SDCL chapter 34-20G and no cannabis excise section in the current medical programme.

Rate history and sunset — to be diarised, not assumed away. Before 1 July 2023: 4.5%. 1 July 2023 – 30 June 2027: 4.2%, by 2023 HB 1137 / SL 2023, ch. 32. 1 July 2027: reverts to 4.5% unless the Legislature extends the reduction. This is consistently reported in Department of Revenue material and secondary summaries of HB 1137. The reversion is a scheduled sunset, not a new increase. Sources: SDCL 10-45-2, opened directly (above); South Dakota Department of Revenue, July 2026 Sales and Use Tax Guide, as cited in secondary reporting — Verify the July 2026 guide itself; it was not opened in full in this research.

Why patients pay full sales tax — the prescription-drug exemption does not apply. SDCL 10-45-14.10 exempts, in substance, gross receipts from the sale of drugs “prescribed by prescription, dispensed, or administered by a physician” etc. Medical cannabis in South Dakota is dispensed on a practitioner written certification, not a prescription, and dispensaries — not pharmacies — sell it. The Department of Revenue position reported in 2021, and repeated in 2026 secondary summaries, is that medical cannabis is therefore subject to state and local sales tax because the prescription-drug exemption does not apply. Sources, search-result / secondary, Verify the original 2021 Department of Revenue statement: KELOLAND, “S.D. sales taxes apply on medical marijuana, state Revenue official says” (2021), as cited 2026; South Dakota Marijuana Interim Study Committee minutes, 26–27 May 2021, reporting a Department of Revenue explanation: “The tax exemption on prescription drugs does not apply to medical marijuana as marijuana is not a prescription drug.” — mylrc Combined 220568 (search-result excerpt only, not opened in full); SDCL 10-45-14, exemption list, sdlegislature.gov 10-45-14 (search-result excerpt only). All accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Worked example — illustration only, not a price survey. $100.00 product + 4.2% state ($4.20) + 2.0% municipal where levied ($2.00) = $106.20. Where a city levies 1% or no municipal tax, the total is $105.20 or $104.20 respectively. No discount, patient exemption, or low-income sales-tax exemption was found.

Filing. Dispensaries collect and remit through the Department of Revenue like other retailers. Secondary tax guides describe Form ST-1 and mandatory e-filing via Department of Revenue Online Services, with filing frequency set by gross receipts. Verify against the current Department of Revenue sales-tax filing page; no current DOR filing page was opened in full in this research.

Conflicting figure to disregard for 2026. Older secondary pages still show “4.5%” as the current rate. That was correct before 1 July 2023 and will be correct again from 1 July 2027 if the sunset holds, but it is not the rate in force on 30 October 2026. Some non-official calculators that show 6.5% combined (4.5% + 2.0%) are using the pre-2023 / post-sunset state rate, not the 2026 rate.

2.3 Municipal sales taxes — up to 2% general tax; combined 6.2% in full-rate cities

Framework. South Dakota law, SDCL chapters 10-52 and 10-52A, allows municipalities to impose a general sales and use tax. The Department of Revenue administers and collects municipal tax alongside state tax, so a business files a single return covering both. Source: South Dakota Department of Revenue, Municipal Tax Guide, January 2025, dor.sd.gov Municipal Tax Guide Jan 2025 — search-result excerpt, not opened in full; the guide itself states the state rate in the guide as 4.2% and introduces SDCL 10-52 / 10-52A. Accessed 30 Oct 2026.

City — SD, not a same-name city elsewhereMunicipal general sales-tax rate in retrieved DOR materialCombined with 4.2% state rate
Sioux Falls, SD2.00%, code 330-2, DOR Municipal Tax Guide, Jan 20256.2%
Spearfish, SD2.00%, code 334-2, DOR Municipal Tax Guide, Jan 20256.2%
Sturgis, SD2.00%, code 341-2, DOR Municipal Tax Guide, Jan 20256.2%
Aberdeen, SD2.0%, code 001-2, DOR Municipal / Special Jurisdiction Tax Rate Chart, 1 Jan–30 June 20266.2%
Rapid City, SD2.0% in secondary summaries citing the DOR Jan 2026 chart; the Jan 2026 chart excerpt retrieved here does not itself reproduce the Rapid City line in full6.2% — Verify against the Jan 2026 chart line for Rapid City
Brookings, SD; Watertown, SD; Yankton, SDDescribed as full 2% cities in secondary summaries citing the DOR Jan 2026 chart6.2% — Verify city-by-city against the current DOR chart
Mitchell, SD; Pierre, SD; Vermillion, SD; Huron, SD; Box Elder, SDNo city-specific DOR chart line retrieved in this researchVerify — do not assume 2% for these cities from the general “up to 2%” rule

The DOR chart to check for any address is the Department of Revenue Municipal / Special Jurisdiction Tax Rate Chart and rate-lookup tool at dor.sd.gov. A search result for a January 2026 chart was returned via a gateway mirror, not via dor.sd.gov directly — do not cite the mirror as the official source; use dor.sd.gov.

What NOT to add. Municipal gross-receipts tax, 1%: DOR material lists a separate 1% gross-receipts tax, with categories shown as lodging, eating establishments, alcohol and admissions. In the Jan 2025 guide excerpts, Sioux Falls (330-1), Spearfish (334-1) and Sturgis (341-1) all show that 1% code. No source retrieved shows that gross-receipts tax applying to a retail medical-cannabis sale. Do not present a combined rate of 7.2%. Verify with DOR / the city finance office whether any gross-receipts category captures dispensary sales. Tourism tax, 1.5%: DOR material describes a 1.5% tourism tax on many services and goods. No source retrieved shows it applying to medical-cannabis retail. Verify; do not add it. County sales tax: a 2026 newspaper excerpt notes a new county power to impose up to 0.5% for property-tax relief, and a separate 2026 law directing the July 2027 state-rate restoration revenue to school funding / homeowner relief. No source retrieved shows a county sales tax actually levied on medical-cannabis sales in any county in Part 3 as of 30 Oct 2026. Verify before adding any county component. County property tax on business premises is outside this Part and is not a cannabis sales tax.

“Up to 10%” claims. A 2026 secondary summary cites South Dakota Searchlight, “Yes, sales taxes in South Dakota can add up to 10%.” That headline, if it refers to stacked state + municipal + gross-receipts + tourism + special-jurisdiction taxes on a hospitality transaction, does not establish a 10% rate for a dispensary sale. The dispensary general-sales-tax total established here is 4.2% + municipal general tax (0–2%) = 4.2%–6.2%.

2.4 Licence and registration fees that function as programme revenue — not taxes, but included because Part 2 asks for them

State establishment fee — the material change. Original IM 26 / ARSD 44:90:03:17 (48 SDR 40, eff. 5 Oct 2021): $5,000, non-refundable, initial or renewal, increasing annually by an index factor. November 2023 amendment: $5,310. 2024: $9,000. Authority: 2024 SB 43, SL 2024, ch. 138, which raised the statutory cap in SDCL 34-20G-72(10) from $5,000 to $20,000, adjusted annually for inflation, and directed the Department of Health to promulgate rules raising the fee to $9,000 by 30 September 2024. Governor signed 14 March 2024; effective 1 July 2024. Current statutory cap, SDCL 34-20G-72(10), opened directly at sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-72, accessed 30 Oct 2026:

“Establishing application and renewal fees for registration certificates, not to exceed twenty thousand dollars, with this upper limit adjusted annually for inflation, with the total fees collected sufficient to offset all costs related to program implementation and administration”

Confirmation of the $9,000 figure as “current” in 2024–2025: South Dakota Supreme Court, Puffy’s, LLC v. State, 2025 S.D. 10, opinion filed 19 Feb 2025, footnote 1: “In 2021, the Department set a non-refundable fee for an initial or renewal application at $5,000. See ARSD 44:90:03:17. The Department amended the rule in November 2023 to increase the fee to $5,310, and in 2024 raised it to the current amount of $9,000.” — UJS opinion 30554 (search-result excerpt; the opinion itself was not opened in full), accessed 30 Oct 2026. August 2026 secondary reporting (CannaCon, 24 Aug 2026; CannBus Q3 2026) still reports $9,000 annual and “open to some applications” for all four types. Verify the current ARSD 44:90:03:17 text in the 2026 rules package; the Department filed 38 proposed ARSD Article 44:90 amendments in Aug–Oct 2026, and a fee change in that package was not confirmed in this research.

Other state establishment fees — historic, Verify current. From the Department of Health’s June 2021 “Information for Establishments” summary of ARSD 44:90:03:13 (as then numbered): $250 to operate at a different physical location; $250 to transfer an ownership interest to a person not listed on the most recent initial or renewal application; $50 per agent identification badge; all non-refundable, increasing annually by the index factor. These three figures are historic and are NOT confirmed as current in 2026. The rule numbering has since changed (fees are now cited as ARSD 44:90:03:17). Verify against the current ARSD Article 44:90 before budgeting.

Patient / caregiver card fees — for completeness, not establishment tax revenue. $75 standard patient registration, $20 low-income, +$20 for home-cultivation registration and +$20 per additional caregiver after the first — reported consistently in Department of Health patient material and 2023 Oversight Committee minutes. These are programme fees, not sales taxes. They are not the focus of Part 2 and are covered in full in Part 1 / Part 6.

Local licence fees. Local fees are not state taxes, but they are material programme revenue for cities/counties and are listed jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction in Part 3. The state rule is only that a local fee must be “reasonable” — SDCL 34-20G-58(6) as amended in 2024; the former freestanding fee section, SDCL 34-20G-60, was repealed by the same 2024 act (see Part 3.1). No statewide schedule of local fees exists.

2.5 Hemp product tax

Finding: no hemp-specific excise, licence-tax, or product tax was found in current South Dakota law in the sources retrieved. Retail sales of hemp products that are tangible personal property are, in principle, subject to the same general state + municipal sales tax as other retail goods, subject to any generally applicable exemption. No hemp-specific exemption or surcharge was found. No Department of Revenue hemp-product tax guide, hemp excise return, or hemp tax rate was located. Verify with the Department of Revenue; absence in retrieved sources is not proof that DOR has never issued hemp guidance. The state industrial-hemp licensing programme itself is being repealed: 2026 SB 39, signed 18 March 2026 (secondary reporting: SDPB; THC Group deadlines summary, reviewed 27 Oct 2026 — signing date conflicts with 17 March in other secondary reporting, Verify), repeals the state grower/processor/research licences under SDCL chapter 38-35 with effect 1 January 2027, leaving USDA licensing for cultivation. State rules on hemp-derived intoxicants stay in place. Historic state hemp licence fees reported in secondary sources — $50 application + $500 licence, 15-month validity for growers; $2,000 for processors in one secondary summary — are historic, secondary, and Verify; they are Part 7 material and are not relied on here as current tax revenue. Hemp-derived THC products that are unlawful under the hemp definition (e.g. chemically derived/converted cannabinoids excluded by 2024 HB 1125) do not become taxable legal products by virtue of being taxed; taxability does not confer legality. That legal analysis belongs to Part 1.

2.6 IRS §280E and South Dakota decoupling — the short answer is “there is nothing to decouple from”

South Dakota has no general income tax to conform or decouple. South Dakota levies no personal income tax and no general corporate income tax. That is the reason every secondary tax guide for South Dakota reaches the same conclusion: there is no state-level §280E conformity issue for an ordinary cannabis business, because there is no state income-tax return on which a §280E disallowance could bite. Sources, secondary but consistent, accessed 30 Oct 2026: South Dakota Department of Revenue, Taxes pages, as summarised in secondary guides: no corporate income tax return to file for a standard commercial company; Tax Foundation, 2026 State Tax Competitiveness Index, South Dakota profile: “one of only two states to forgo individual income, corporate income, and gross receipts taxes”; Governor’s Office / Department of Revenue public statements: “We don’t have a corporate income tax… We are one of the few remaining states with no personal income tax.”

Caveats — do not overstate “no state income tax at all”. (1) Bank franchise tax, SDCL chapter 10-43, is an income tax on banks and financial corporations. It does not apply to a standard dispensary, cultivator or manufacturer as such. Whether it could touch a cannabis-serving financial institution is Verify and is not a cannabis-operator tax. (2) No South Dakota statute decoupling from, or conforming to, IRC §280E was found. Searching for a South Dakota §280E statute returned no such statute. That is the expected result in a no-income-tax state, but it should be recorded as “no decoupling statute found”, not as a positive assertion that the Legislature considered and rejected one. (3) Pass-through owners resident elsewhere may still face income tax, including §280E issues, in their home state. South Dakota’s lack of an income tax does not shield a non-resident owner from another state’s tax. Verify with a tax adviser for multi-state owners.

Federal §280E — applying the fixed baseline to South Dakota. Under the fixed baseline: South Dakota’s programme is state-licensed — registration certificates issued by the Department of Health under SDCL chapter 34-20G. It therefore falls squarely in the category the baseline describes as moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026. For those state-licensed medical establishments, §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity under the baseline. Adult-use activity, which has no legal South Dakota market, remains fully subject to §280E federally. Tribal medical programmes: per the commission instruction, mark as Verify whether tribal medical programmes count as “state-licensed” under the federal rule. Tribal adult-use activity remains Schedule I federally. This report does not resolve that question. South Dakota Attorney General opinion, 13 Oct 2026: secondary reporting (Cannabis Business Times, Oct 2026) states AG Marty Jackley advised, in response to Sen. John Carley, that federal rescheduling will “likely have a negligible impact” on South Dakota’s medical programme, that “Because federal law explicitly permits state medical marijuana programs, South Dakota’s program may continue to operate as it has without impact from marijuana’s federal rescheduling to a Schedule III controlled substance,” and that future programme shape is a policy choice for the Legislature or voters. The opinion also notes DEA Schedule III registration as a route to ordinary deductions. The full AG opinion text was not opened in this research — Verify the opinion itself before quoting it as law. It is an opinion, not a statute, and it does not change South Dakota sales-tax treatment. DEA registration wrinkle reported in the same secondary coverage: some operators did not use a 60-day early DEA application window, reportedly because the application asked businesses to admit to drug trafficking. That is a federal compliance question for advisers; it does not affect the South Dakota sales-tax analysis.

Practical consequence for a South Dakota operator in 2026. State tax cost = general sales tax collected from patients (4.2% + municipal) + state $9,000 registration fee + local licence fee + ordinary business costs (property tax, employment taxes, federal tax). There is no state income-tax bill to reduce by deducting expenses, and therefore no state “§280E penalty” to remove. The federal §280E benefit from Schedule III, if it applies to the operator, is a federal benefit only.

2.7 Proposed adult-use taxes — all PROPOSED, STRUCK or DEAD; none in force

Measure / billTax proposedStatus — label exactly
Constitutional Amendment A, 202015% excise on gross receipts of all sales of marijuana sold by a person licensed under the article to a consumer. Text, as reproduced in the struck Article XXX: “An excise tax of fifteen percent is imposed upon the gross receipts of all sales of marijuana sold by a person licensed by the department pursuant to this article to a consumer. The Legislature may adjust this rate after November 3, 2024… Fifty percent of the remaining revenue shall be appropriated by the Legislature for the support of South Dakota public schools and the remainder shall be deposited into the state general fund.” Regulator was to be the Department of Revenue.STRUCK — passed by voters 3 Nov 2020 (~54%), then invalidated by the South Dakota Supreme Court on single-subject grounds, 24 Nov 2021. Never in force as a tax. Do not present the 15% as current law. Fiscal estimates attached to it in 2020 ($10.7m first year including fees/taxes, rising to $19.5m / $29.3m; LRC gross sales $49.3m first year) are historic estimates for a struck measure, not revenue.
2022 SB 3 (Sen. Rohl) + SB 150 (tax)SB 3: adult-use legalisation, Department of Revenue regulation. A warning on a recurring secondary error: Some commercial “how to open a dispensary in South Dakota” pages state that cannabis sales are subject to “a 15% excise tax” in addition to sales tax, citing the Department of Revenue. That is the struck Amendment A rate, not current law. It must not be carried into a current tax table.

2.8 Revenue figures — what official data actually exists, and what does not
