5.1.6 Medical Marijuana Oversight Committee (legislative oversight source)

What it is: Committee created by IM 26, meeting at least twice a year to evaluate patient access, dispensary/cultivation effectiveness, prices, complaints/security, and sufficiency of numbers, and to recommend to the Legislature and DOH. Latest final report retrieved: 2025 Final Report, 19 Nov 2025: mylrc 288146 (search-result, accessed 30 Oct 2026). Key data: see §5.1.5 (Aug 2025 counts); rule changes effective 15 July 2025 implementing fines; 7 DOH programme staff; presentations by Cannabis Industry Association of South Dakota, Genesis Farms, Dakota Cannabis Consulting; committee recommendations including that DOH ensure fines are fair/equitable with policies, and that sale of THC products be prohibited in stores and permitted only in licensed medical marijuana facilities. 2026 context (secondary, Verify): a 25 Oct 2026 trade-press transcript reports the Oversight Committee’s 13 Oct 2026 meeting (federal rescheduling discussion, AG’s Office; AG Official Opinion 26-06, 6 Oct 2026, committee document mylrc 308377, agenda mylrc 307903). The transcript states DOH numbers at that meeting of “around 19,800 approved patients… about 19,100 South Dakota residents… only about 600 non-residents.” Search-result / secondary transcript — Verify against the official committee minutes before quoting. Source: WeedPress 25 Oct 2026, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026. Format: PDF via mylrc.sdlegislature.gov. No CSV/download of underlying data.

5.2 Department of Agriculture and Natural Resources (DANR) — Industrial Hemp

Programme page — opened directly, accessed 30 Oct 2026: danr.sd.gov Industrial Hemp Program. Title: “South Dakota Industrial Hemp Program”. It states: any person/legal entity shall obtain a licence from the department to purchase, receive, obtain, store, propagate or process industrial hemp (other than hemp product for planting); a grower licence is not required for possession/distribution of hemp seed; requirements are in the approved State Plan and ARSD 12:82; DANR “has received final approval by USDA for the South Dakota Industrial Hemp Plan” (linked plan on the page — link target not opened in this run, Verify).

FieldFinding
Holder typesGrower licence; Processor licence. (Research licence existed under the pre-2027 framework — see §5.2.1.) Seed sellers need a seed permit via the DANR Seed Program (page linked from the hemp page, not opened — Verify).
Fees as stated on the opened pageGrower: $50 non-refundable application fee + $500 licence fee on approval, valid 15 months. Processor: $50 application fee + $2,000 licence fee on approval, valid 15 months. Grower inspection/sample fee $250 per hemp lot inspected. Background checks by SD Division of Criminal Investigation and FBI for applicant, key participants and landowner. Municipal approval letter required if growing within corporate limits of a municipality. Planting verification inspection within 30 days of planting; pre-harvest sampling not more than 30 days before harvest; non-compliant lots remediated/disposed under DANR/DPS oversight per USDA/DANR guidelines.
Public licence-holder list?NONE FOUND. The DANR hemp page as opened contains programme rules, application guidance, an amendment form link and a paper-form PDF download link (link targets not opened — Verify), but no searchable licence-holder list, no CSV/XLSX, and no published grower/processor roster. Unavailable in this run — do not assert a DANR public hemp register exists.
Format of what IS downloadableApplication / amendment paper forms (PDF) linked from the programme page. Exact file names/URLs not retrieved — Verify by opening the page’s links in a live browser (the text-fetch did not expose their targets).
Update frequencyNot stated on the opened page.
LimitationsDANR page text still describes the state licensing system as current, but that system is repealed effective 1 Jan 2027 (see §5.2.1). Any Part 7 hemp-licence text must carry that sunset prominently.
Contact as shown on the opened pageIndustrial Hemp Program, 523 E. Capitol Ave., Pierre, SD 57501; programme manager/specialist names and DANR phone numbers are shown on the page — omitted here per the no-personal-contact-details rule; take the official programme contact block from the opened page URL above.

5.2.1 The 2027 sunset — SB 39 (2026), Session Laws ch. 167 — critical for Parts 5 and 7

Enacted text (search-result, Legislature document system): Session Laws 306615 — “Chapter 167 (Senate Bill 39) An Act to revise provisions relating to industrial hemp”, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026. It repeals §§38-35-3.1 (research licence), 38-35-4, 38-35-6, 38-35-12, 38-35-15 (state hemp production plan duty), 38-35-18, 38-35-20 and related provisions. Effect and dates (secondary synthesis, corroborated by the session-law repeals): SB 39 signed 17 March 2026 (one secondary source says 18 March 2026 — Verify the exact signing date on sdlegislature.gov), effective 1 January 2027; from that date only a producer licensed under the USDA programme (or a tribal programme, per one secondary summary) may cultivate hemp in South Dakota; state grower/processor/research licences under SDCL ch. 38-35 end; synthetically derived cannabinoids (delta-8 THC, delta-10 THC, THC-O-acetate, HHC, THCP named) are excluded from the industrial hemp product definition; transport documentation is aligned with federal rules. Sources: HomeGrown Group deadlines (reviewed 27 Oct 2026, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026) and MyCannabis (accessed via search 30 Oct 2026). Both secondary — the session-law PDF itself is the authority for the repeals; the 1 Jan 2027 effective date and the USDA-only consequence must be confirmed in the enrolled bill text before final publication. 2025 licence counts given in SB 39 testimony (secondary, dated Feb–Mar 2026): DANR’s Brian Walsh cited 17 growers, 5 processors and about 1,100 harvested acres in 2025, and ~$200,000/year general-fund saving from returning the programme to USDA; DPS retains field sampling/transport enforcement at ports. Source: Citizen Portal summaries of committee/Senate proceedings, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026: Citizen Portal 7591452 and Citizen Portal 7637559 — AI-generated summaries of proceedings, Verify against the official legislative record. Consequence for Part 5: after 1 Jan 2027 the relevant “licence-holder data source” for SD hemp cultivation becomes the USDA source in §5.4, not DANR. DANR remains relevant only for the pre-2027 state licences, transport/sampling enforcement, and hemp-product rules that SB 39 did not repeal.

5.3 South Dakota Secretary of State — Business Entity Search

FieldFinding
Holder typeALL entities registered with the SOS (LLCs, corporations, LLPs, LPs, nonprofits, foreign entities qualified in SD). It is NOT a cannabis licence register: a cannabis establishment’s SOS entity record proves legal existence/standing only, not DOH certification, and sole proprietorships/general partnerships never appear.
FormatInteractive search page only for basic searches. No free download, no cannabis filter, no bulk list. Full database download available for a fee (per secondary guides describing the search page itself — Verify fee/route on the live page).
How to access (search-result URL — Verify live)FilingSearch, SOS Business Services: sosenterprise.sd.gov FilingSearch — cited verbatim in multiple secondary guides accessed via search 30 Oct 2026, e.g. BusinessAnywhere and Commenda. This URL was not opened directly in this run; confirm it live before publishing. Official SOS portal domain also cited as sdsos.gov. Warning from secondary guides: secretaryofstate.com is a private lookalike, not the state tool — use sdsos.gov / sosenterprise.sd.gov only.
Search byBusiness name (“Starts With” or “Contains”), Business ID; registered-agent search also described in some guides (conflict: one guide says no agent search — Verify on the live tool). “Active Entities Only” checkbox.
Result fieldsBusiness ID, Type, Name, Name Type, Filing Date, Status; detail record: formation date, registered agent, status, filed documents (document images mostly for entities formed since 1994; pre-1994 documents $2/page on request, per secondary guides — Verify). No shareholders/ownership percentages.
Cost / accountBasic search free, no account, 24/7 (per secondary guides). Certified copies / Certificate of Good Standing are separate paid documents.
Update frequency as statedNot stated; described as regularly updated as filings are made.
Limitations for cannabis researchCannot filter by “cannabis”; cannot confirm DOH/DANR licence status; cannot show unregistered sole proprietors; a name search for e.g. “Cannabis” will return entity names containing that word only, which is not a licence census. Use only to verify an individual establishment’s entity existence/standing, then cross-check DOH/DANR status separately.

5.4.2 USDA Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP) — Public Search Tool vs Law Enforcement Search Tool

FieldFinding
Public Search Tool URL (search-result, via a state portal citing USDA)hemp.ams.usda.gov PublicSearchTool — cited in Kansas Dept. of Agriculture hemp portal, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026: KDA LicenseSearch. Described by CSBS (May 2026 job aid, CSBS industrial hemp, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026) as the “U.S. Domestic Hemp Production Program’s New Public HeMP Search Tool” usable to verify a borrower’s licence validity. The tool itself was not opened in this run; its SD result fields, export ability and whether it returns state-plan producers vs USDA-direct producers only are Verify.
Law Enforcement Search ToolAt hempsearch.ams.usda.gov; access ONLY on request to FarmBill.Hemp@usda.gov, with eAuthentication; searches city, county, entity name, licence number, holder first name; shows lot maps from FSA data. USDA’s own caveat in that guide: the tool “may not capture all approved hemp growing locations” at first.
HeMP producer platformAMS HeMP platform — login-gated (Login.gov / eAuth); producers apply/renew/amend/print licences and file disposal (AMS-27) and annual (AMS-28) reports. Not a public register.
SD-specific extract?None retrieved. No USDA list of SD hemp producers, with names/acreage, was found in this run. NASS survey data (§6.4) and FSA acreage reports are statistical, not licence registers, and must not be presented as such.

There is no statewide consolidated list of local cannabis licences. Records exist only jurisdiction by jurisdiction, in ordinances, minutes and codes. Do not infer “allows” or “bans” from the absence of a record in this inventory. Note: Sturgis appears in DOH inspection reports as a dispensary location (Northern Hills Alternative Health, Sturgis, inspected Nov 2025 — see §5.1.4), which proves at least one state-certified dispensary site there at that date, not the content of a Sturgis local licence.

Local-record limitations (all jurisdictions): local licences are in addition to, not instead of, DOH certificates; a business can hold a local licence without yet holding/retaining a state certificate and vice versa at a given moment; minutes and pleadings are point-in-time snapshots, not registers.

A Pine Ridge community report (accessed via search 30 Oct 2026: United Resource Connection) states No Worries opened late May (year contextually 2022), was the first tribally-licensed dispensary to open, is privately owned (not tribe-operated, unlike Native Nations), vertically integrated (grow/process/sell), with a purchase limit described at opening as up to a quarter-ounce subject to supply. All tribal operational details here are secondary — tribal ordinance text and any tribal regulator/licence list are Verify / Unavailable in this run. Tribal sales figures: none officially reported and retrieved — Unavailable.
Other seven tribes (Rosebud, Cheyenne River, Standing Rock, Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate, Yankton, Crow Creek, Lower Brule)No cannabis licence register or operation confirmed in this run.One secondary note only: a Lower Brule council member visiting No Worries expressed interest in legalising medical cannabis on Lower Brule (KFF, same URL) — interest is not an operation. Do not list any other tribe as operating without a tribal-government source.

Cross-cutting tribal-data warning: Tribal establishments are licensed by tribal governments, not DOH, and therefore will not appear in DOH counts, DOH inspection PDFs or SOS cannabis filtering. Any combined “South Dakota total” that adds tribal shops to DOH counts without labelling the two systems separately is misleading.

5.7 Other / Negative Results (sources checked, nothing usable found)

  • DOH enforcement-action spreadsheet / disciplinary database: Not found — Unavailable. Use inspection PDFs (§5.1.4) + Oversight Committee reports (§5.1.6) + court filings for enforcement, case by case.
  • Department of Revenue cannabis sales/tax dataset: Not found — DOR does not report cannabis sales tax separately (see §6.2). No DOR licence-holder list for cannabis exists because there is no cannabis-specific excise licence.
  • METRC public portal: METRC is the state’s seed-to-sale system (SFY 2024 report, opened directly), but no public METRC query portal for SD was found — Unavailable / Verify (METRC data is licensee/regulator-facing).
  • DOH “establishments list” as CSV/XLSX: Not found — see §5.1.1.
  • DANR hemp licence-holder list: Not found — see §5.2.
  • Tribal licence registers: Not found — see §5.6.
  • Statewide local-licence list: Not found — see §5.5.

Is there an interactive dashboard or search page with no download option? Yes — the DOH establishments list (§5.1.1, web list, no download button identified), the SOS FilingSearch (§5.3, interactive search only), and the USDA HeMP Public Search Tool (§5.4.2, interactive tool, export ability Verify) are all in that category. The DOH monthly data PDFs and annual-report PDFs are downloads, and their exact links/file names are given above. No other downloadable licence-holder file was verified in this run.

What the counts mean for Part 6 narrative (safe to state): The programme grew from ~1,200 patients in mid-2022 to ~10,000 by May 2023, ~13,300–13,500 in mid-2024/2025, and ~19,800–20,000 by September 2026 — roughly 2% of the state’s population on the May 2023 secondary comparison, higher on an adult-population basis (secondary, Verify denominator). Severe, debilitating pain has been 70–73% of patients at every official snapshot (72.71% June 2024; 70.50% July 2026). PTSD is second (~11.7% June 2024; 13.99% July 2026). No official patient forecast was found.

Establishment certificate raised to $9,000 (SB 43, 2024 session; rule effective Aug/Sept 2024 — CannBus + July 2024 fee article, same URLs as above). SFY 2024 programme revenue $1,915,946; expenditures $1,193,010 (opened directly, SFY 2024 report). CannBus cites SFY 2024 programme revenue as “$1.92 million, mostly cardholder and establishment fees” — consistent. No SFY 2025 or SFY 2026 annual-report revenue total was retrieved — Verify / Unavailable in this run.

6.2 Medical Sales by Year — Official Position: NOT PUBLISHED

Finding: The South Dakota Department of Health and the Department of Revenue do not publish medical cannabis sales (by year or otherwise), and DOR does not report cannabis sales tax separately. Source: CannBus Q3 2026, opened directly, accessed 30 Oct 2026: CannBus — “Cannabis sales: N/A: DOH and the Dept. of Revenue do not publish medical cannabis sales” and “Cannabis tax revenue: N/A: DOR does not report cannabis sales tax separately.” Consequence: any “South Dakota medical sales by year” table in the final report must show Unavailable (official) for every year, with secondary estimates (below) clearly labelled as estimates, never as sales.

Secondary sales/market-size claims (dated, unverified, do NOT present as official): “$66 million in sales this year” (projected medical market, year unstated in snippet, contextually 2025) + “just 14,000 registered patients” (stale patient base vs 2026 counts) + “116 licensed cultivators, manufacturers and dispensaries” (inconsistent with DOH snapshots) — Cannabis Risk Manager article, accessed via search 30 Oct 2026: Cannabis Risk Manager. Treat as an unverified industry projection; its patient and establishment inputs already conflict with official snapshots, so its $66m output cannot be relied on. April 2026 TV report cited card approvals “up 62% year over year” — CannBus expressly declines to use it because the DOH series does not show that rate for total active cards (same CannBus URL). Do not use.

Tax context for market sizing (Part 2’s job, summarised here only): No cannabis-specific excise tax found; general state sales tax 4.2% (July 2026 DOR guide, per CannBus) + municipal 1–2% where levied; medical cannabis subject to sales tax because the prescription-drug exemption does not apply (DOR counsel, 2021, per CannBus). South Dakota levies no corporate or personal income tax, so state-level 280E decoupling is moot at state level (CannBus, same URL). Verify the 4.2% rate and the DOR taxability position in the current DOR guide before final publication.

Part 7 — Hemp Licences

7.1 The headline answer: state plan until 31 December 2026, USDA programme from 1 January 2027

Until 31 December 2026, South Dakota runs its own USDA-approved state plan. DANR administers it under SDCL ch. 38-35 and ARSD art. 12:82, under a State Hemp Plan approved by USDA. Source: DANR Industrial Hemp Program page, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: danr.sd.gov/Agriculture/Inspection/IndustrialHemp — “The South Dakota Department of Agriculture and Natural Resources (DANR) has received final approval by the United States Department of Agriculture (USDA) for the South Dakota Industrial Hemp Plan.” Search-result only in Run 2; Run 1 opened this page directly on 30 Oct 2026 and recorded the same statement.

From 1 January 2027, South Dakota does NOT run its own state-plan licensing system. 2026 SB 39, 2026 Session Laws ch. 167, repeals the state grower, processor and research licences and provides that only a person licensed by USDA or a tribal programme may cultivate hemp in South Dakota. After that date a South Dakota producer outside tribal jurisdiction must hold a USDA hemp producer licence under 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C (the USDA plan), not a DANR licence. Sources: SB 39 enrolled text, search-result excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026: SB 39 enrolled text and 2026 Session Laws ch. 167; secondary confirmation, THC Group, reviewed 27 Oct 2026; SDPB, 18 Mar 2026: SDPB, 18 Mar 2026.

What SB 39 does NOT do — do not overstate it: it does not legalise unlicensed cultivation — its new section states, verbatim in the enrolled text: “Only a licensed hemp producer may cultivate hemp in this state.” It does not repeal the chemically derived cannabinoid ban (SDCL 34-20B-118, HB 1125) or the smoking/inhaling ban (SDCL 38-35-21). It does not take effect during the 2026 growing season — its effective date is expressly 1 January 2027, chosen, per DANR testimony, “to avoid disruption of the 2026 growing season” (Citizen Portal summary of House Agriculture and Natural Resources Committee, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — AI-generated summary of proceedings, Verify against the official legislative record). It does not make DANR disappear from hemp enforcement: DANR testimony and DPS testimony state DPS retains field sampling and transportation enforcement at ports, and South Dakota keeps THC sampling and transportation checks even as USDA assumes licensing and inspection. Verify the post-2027 division of labour in DANR/DPS guidance after 1 Jan 2027; the committee summary is not a statute.

Why the DANR page still describes the old system — reconciliation. At the 30 Oct 2026 research date the DANR page still presents grower/processor licensing, fees and inspections as the current system, with no SB 39 sunset banner in the retrieved excerpt. That is not a contradiction: SB 39 was enacted in March 2026 but is not yet effective. A reader who plans a 2027 crop on the basis of the DANR page alone, without SB 39, will be misled. Rule: every DANR pre-2027 statement in §7.2–§7.5 must carry the tag “pre-2027 system; repealed eff. 1 Jan 2027 by SB 39.” The DANR page must be re-checked after 1 Jan 2027; whether DANR updates it, archives it, or redirects to USDA is Verify.

Sourcing honesty note for Parts 7–8. In this run, four attempted direct page-opens — DANR Industrial Hemp page, SB 39 enrolled text, USDA AMS “Information for Hemp Growers,” and Cornell 7 CFR 990.21 — failed with a network-grant error and, per instruction, were not retried through another tool or endpoint. Those failures do not prove the sources are down. Facts below that Run 1 already opened directly are labelled “Run 1, opened directly 30 Oct 2026.” Facts in this run that come only from search-result excerpts or secondary summaries are labelled “Search-result only / Verify” or “Secondary, dated.” Every verbatim quote below is quoted exactly as it appeared in the retrieved excerpt. Where the excerpt truncates a provision, that is stated.

7.1.1 Check against Run 1 — conflicts found

  1. Hemp grower minimum size — CONFLICT, unresolved. Run 1 secondary summaries and the SB 39 introduced text state at least one-half contiguous outdoor acre with a 300-plant minimum, or a greenhouse with a 50-plant minimum. A Run 2 search excerpt of ARSD 12:82:02:02 states: “No application to plant, grow, or produce industrial hemp may be for less than five contiguous outdoor acres.” Audit resolution (30 Oct 2026): the current statute controls the application size, and the 5-acre figure is stale rule text. Current SDCL 38-35-3, as amended by 2022 SB 201 (SL 2022 ch. 155), provides: an application “must be for at least one-half, contiguous outdoor acre with a three hundred plant minimum, or in a greenhouse with a fifty plant minimum, or combination thereof.” The 5-acre sentence in ARSD 12:82:02:02 is the original 2020 rule text (2020 HB 1008, SL 2020 ch. 176) that was not conformed in the compilation retrieved. A grower must therefore use the 0.5-acre / 300-plant / 50-plant (greenhouse) statute and confirm on the current DANR application form whether DANR has conformed its rule/form — do not advise on the basis of the 5-acre rule text alone. Source: SDCL 38-35-3.
  2. SB 39 signing date — still unresolved, as in Run 1. SDPB published its “signed” story 18 March 2026; MyCannabis states signed 17 March 2026. Verify on sdlegislature.gov. The enrolled act and 2026 Session Laws ch. 167 status are not in dispute.
  3. Grower licence fee — one secondary error rejected. PaymentCloud (undated, secondary) states a grower licence fee of $200. DANR’s own page, corroborated across Run 1 and Run 2 search excerpts, states $500. The $200 figure is rejected; the DANR $500 figure is used, with the pre-2027 caveat.
  4. Municipal approval wording — secondary garble corrected to DANR. One secondary page says approval is needed “if the proposed location is within an unincorporated area.” DANR’s page says approval in letter form is required if growing within the corporate limits of any municipality. The DANR wording is used.
  5. Minnehaha County dispensary address — new licence, not a contradiction. Run 1 recorded an initial county licence to Genesis Farms, LLC at 7605 E. Arrowhead Parkway (Mar 2022, with a later compliance dispute). A Run 2 search result shows a 2026 Minnehaha County licence for Genesis Farms, LLC at 7401 E Arrowhead Parkway, Sioux Falls, SD 57110, issue date 01/07/26, expiration 01/07/27, licence number Medical Cannabis 26-01, expressly “contingent upon the State of South Dakota Department of Health awarding a registration certificate.” A changed address and a new licence year are consistent with a new licence cycle; do not merge the two addresses into one site history without the county file.
  6. Fort Pierre §160.03 is NOT Pierre and NOT Sioux Falls. A search result for §160.03 (3 cultivation / 3 dispensaries / 0 testing, 1,000 ft school, 500 ft residence/church/park/pool/daycare, hours 7 a.m.–7 p.m. MST) is from the Fort Pierre code library. It must never be cited as Pierre’s or Sioux Falls’ rule. See §8.3.
  7. Security-camera retention — jurisdiction-dependent, not a single state figure. Pierre’s city summary states footage maintained for a minimum of 90 days; Minnehaha County’s ordinance states recordings retained for 180 days. Both are reported as local/ordinance figures; the current ARSD 44:90:04:08 figure is Verify — do not publish either as “the state rule.”
  8. All other Run 1 key facts checked and NOT contradicted in this run: medical-only; SDCL ch. 34-20G; 3 oz / 14-day rolling limit; home grow 2+2; Amendment A struck 24 Nov 2021, 2021 S.D. 65; delta-8/chemically derived banned (HB 1125, SDCL 34-20B-118); smokable hemp banned (38-35-21); SB 61 0.4 mg cap FAILED; SB 39 ends the state hemp programme 1 Jan 2027; sales tax 4.2% + up to 2% municipal; establishment fee $9,000; Sioux Falls dispensary cap 5; Rapid City 15 in 2022, current figure disputed; establishments 62/30/14/1 = 107 on 27 Aug 2026 (mixed official/secondary) vs 118 Aug 2025 official; patients 19,821 on 2 Sept 2026 vs 20,040 Sept 2026; no official medical sales series; hemp NASS 2024 3,900 planted / 3,700 harvested, fibre $3.54m.

7.2 Pre-2027 state system — SDCL ch. 38-35 and ARSD art. 12:82 (repealed eff. 1 Jan 2027 where noted)

7.2.1 Origin and plan approval

2020 HB 1008, signed 27 Mar 2020 (after a 2019 veto of an earlier hemp bill — Run 1 context, Verify on sdlegislature.gov), created SDCL ch. 38-35. It legalised cultivation, manufacture and distribution of industrial hemp at or below 0.3% THC and required DANR to regulate and license growers and processors. Source: secondary summary, MyCannabis, accessed 30 Oct 2026: MyCannabis — CBD in South Dakota — secondary; the HB 1008 session law itself was not opened in this run.

USDA approval: DANR “has received final approval by USDA for the South Dakota Industrial Hemp Plan” (DANR page, §7.1). The plan document itself is linked from the DANR page as “SD State Hemp Plan_2021.pdf” (not opened in this run, Verify). The plan, per its search-result summary, contains: USDA 2018 Farm Bill requirements; plan to maintain licensee and land information; plan for crop sampling and testing for total delta-9 THC; plan for disposal or remediation of non-compliant plants; plan for inspection procedures and annual inspection; sampling and testing guidelines; and a State Hemp Disposal Form. Its compliance standard is stated verbatim in the excerpt:

“The acceptable hemp THC level for the purpose of compliance with the requirements of the State hemp plan is when the application of the measurement of uncertainty to the reported total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol content concentration level on a dry weight basis produces a distribution or range that includes 0.3% or less”

USDA approved-plans list, updated 17 Feb 2026: South Dakota is listed as “SD Approved Plan” (pre-2027 status). Source: USDA AMS, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: USDA AMS state and tribal plan review. Recheck after 1 Jan 2027 — the list entry should change or be removed for the state plan once SB 39 takes effect; do not cite the Feb 2026 list as post-2027 authority.

ARSD art. 12:82 structure (pre-2027 rules, 47 SDR 75, eff. 28 Dec 2020, with 49 SDR 47, eff. 22 Nov 2022 amendments to disposal): Ch. 12:82:01 Definitions; 12:82:02 Application and licensure; 12:82:03 Records; 12:82:04 Enforcement; 12:82:05 Handling requirements; 12:82:06 Inspections; 12:82:07 Samples and testing; 12:82:08 Disposal; 12:82:09 Transportation. Source: rules.sd.gov article index, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Whether ARSD art. 12:82 is repealed, amended or left dormant after SB 39 is Verify — SB 39 repeals SDCL sections, but the rulemaking consequence for art. 12:82 was not confirmed in this run. Do not assume the rules vanish automatically on 1 Jan 2027; equally, do not cite them as the post-2027 licensing rules.

7.2.2 Licence types in the pre-2027 system — table

LicenceWhat it authorises (pre-2027)Fee / term (pre-2027)Status from 1 Jan 2027
Grower licencePlant, grow, produce industrial hemp; purchase/receive/obtain hemp (other than hemp product) for planting, storing, propagating or producing. A grower licence is not required for possession or distribution of hemp seed.$50 non-refundable application fee + $500 licence fee on approval; valid 15 months from issueRepealed by SB 39. Replaced by USDA producer licence (§7.7).
Processor licenceObtain hemp plants from licensed growers and process/manufacture hemp products; “process” means to render raw hemp plants or plant parts from their natural or original state to an initial processed form (SB 39 enrolled definition of “process,” retained). Processor must give street address, legal description and latitude/longitude for each processing location, certify the location is under the applicant’s control, and give notice of any ownership/location change within 10 days — failure to amend automatically invalidates the licence and a new licence must be obtained (pre-2027 §38-35-4 text, as reproduced in SB 39 introduced text).$50 non-refundable application fee + $2,000 licence fee on approval; valid 15 months; processor inspection $500 per processing location (DANR Processor Application Guidance, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026)Repealed by SB 39 as a state licence. See §7.6 for the open question: what, if anything, a pure processor (who does not cultivate) must hold after 2027.
Research licenceCultivate hemp for research purposes (secondary summaries; the pre-2027 §38-35-3.1). DANR testimony/fee discussion in SB 39 materials references a “research inspection fee per lot” for research licences.Fee/term not retrieved in this run — Verify. The SB 39 committee-substitute excerpt references a research inspection fee per lot, but no dollar figure was retrieved.Repealed by SB 39 (§38-35-3.1 repealed). Post-2027 research cultivation falls under the USDA producer licence, including the USDA research-producer rule in 7 CFR 990.21(d) / 990.24 (§7.7.4).
Handler licenceNo separate “handler” licence was found in SDCL ch. 38-35 or ARSD art. 12:82 in this run. Handling (storing, propagating, producing, processing) was covered by the grower/processor licences and by the general licensing duty in ARSD 12:82:02:01 (below). Transporters are not separately licensed; they are documentation carriers (§7.5).—No handler licence to repeal. Post-2027, handling by a non-producer is governed, if at all, by the retained transport/documentation sections and by general product law — Verify (§7.6).
Seed producer / seed distributor licence (hemp-specific)No hemp-specific seed producer/distributor licence was found. Two different instruments apply and must not be conflated: (a) DANR’s statement that a grower licence is not required for possession or distribution of hemp seed; (b) the general South Dakota seed permit under SDCL ch. 38-12A (§7.8). A person who sells hemp seed commercially in South Dakota needs the general seed permit analysis, not a DANR hemp licence, for the seed-sale act itself.See §7.8General seed law is not repealed by SB 39. Whether DANR or USDA guidance after 2027 treats seed distribution differently.

General licensing duty — ARSD 12:82:02:01, verbatim (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026):

“Any person or legal business entity in South Dakota shall obtain a license from the department to purchase, receive, or obtain industrial hemp, other than industrial hemp product, for planting, storing, propagating, producing, or processing industrial hemp. Employees of the State of South Dakota are not required to obtain a license when performing official duties.”
Source: 47 SDR 75, effective December 28, 2020. General Authority: SDCL 38-35-11(1). Law Implemented: SDCL 38-35-2.

Pre-2027 §38-35-2 (repealed by SB 39 §5), as reproduced in SB 39 introduced text, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026:

“No person may purchase, receive, or obtain industrial hemp or product in process, other than industrial hemp seed or industrial hemp product, for planting, storing, propagating, producing, or processing unless the person has a license as provided by this chapter or is working under contract with or under the direction of a licensee. The licensee is responsible, either civilly or criminally, for any person working under contract with or under the direction of a licensee for all sections of this chapter.”

That “working under contract/direction” sentence is the pre-2027 answer to employees/contractors: they did not need their own licence if they worked under a licensee, but the licensee bore responsibility. Whether any equivalent contractor rule survives in the USDA plan is covered by 7 CFR 990.21 (authorised employees/agents of the licensee) — see §7.7.

7.2.3 Grower application — what was required (pre-2027)

From ARSD 12:82:02:02 (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026) and the DANR page: apply to the secretary on the prescribed form; applicant name, mailing address, physical address, telephone number and email; if a business entity, full business name and physical address; legal description and geospatial (GPS) coordinates of the area where hemp will be located; authorisation for state and federal background checks by the South Dakota Division of Criminal Investigation (DCI) and the FBI on the applicant, key participants and landowner; municipal approval letter if growing within the corporate limits of any municipality (DANR page wording — see conflict note in §7.1.1); certificate of good standing from the South Dakota Secretary of State (for business-entity applicants; $20 only if applying under Business Entity Section 2, per DANR Processor Guidance — whether the same $20 figure applied to growers); Federal Employer Identification Number (FEIN) (secondary summaries; against the DANR application form); documents confirming proposed facility locations are not within Indian tribe territories (secondary summary of the State Plan — ; this is a plan-level attestation, not a statement that tribal land is state-licensed).

Minimum size / plant count — RESOLVED in favour of the current statute (audit correction). Current SDCL 38-35-3 (SL 2022 ch. 155) requires at least one-half contiguous outdoor acre with a 300-plant minimum, or a greenhouse with a 50-plant minimum, or a combination. The ARSD 12:82:02:02 “five contiguous outdoor acres” sentence is original 2020 rule text (SL 2020 ch. 176) not conformed in the compilation retrieved. Use the statute figure; confirm DANR's current form before filing. Source: SDCL 38-35-3.

Grower application submission period: ARSD 12:82:02:03 exists (chapter index) but its dates were not retrieved — Verify. Do not state a DANR application window. Licence number, non-transferability: ARSD 12:82:02:05 (grower) and 12:82:02:11 (processor) — licences are non-transferable (chapter index titles). Full transfer/amendment mechanics for growers were not retrieved; processor amendment is ARSD 12:82:02:13. Before advising on sale of a licensed business. Renewal: ARSD 12:82:02:08 (grower) and 12:82:02:14 (processor) — renewal exists; the renewal fee is stated in secondary summaries as the same $500 / $2,000 licence fee, and the term remains 15 months. Renewal fee and whether a new background check is required on renewal — the DANR page states the 15-month validity but does not, in the retrieved excerpt, state the renewal background-check rule.

7.2.4 Processor application — what was required (pre-2027)

From DANR Processor Application Guidance (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): Step 1a — Apply: completed application form; online application and payment preferred; map(s) of processing locations and buildings (with aerial/photographic map and GPS pinpoint instructions); SD Secretary of State Certificate of Good Standing ($20 only if applying under Business Entity Section 2); non-refundable $50 application fee (check to DANR). Step 1b — Background check: DANR mails a fingerprint kit (fingerprint cards + authorisation form) for key participants, applicants and landowners (if the applicant is a lessee). Complete the authorisation on the back of the SD DCI fingerprint card; mail cards, authorisation and $50 background-check fee (check to DCI) in the prepaid envelope. Processing may take 20 or more days. DANR reviews applications and completed background checks to determine eligibility, then contacts the applicant. Step 2 — Licence fee: if eligible, pay the $2,000 annual licence fee to DANR to receive the processor licence; licence and memorandum of understanding mailed via US Mail after payment. Step 3 — Annual inspection: DANR or DPS schedules an inspection at which the licensee must be present; inspection fee $500 per processing location, paid to DANR. Dual holding: secondary summaries state individuals/businesses can hold both grower and processor licences at the same time. Against the DANR application form — no statutory bar was found, but no express dual-holding authorisation was opened in this run either.

7.2.5 Background checks — who, what disqualifies (pre-2027)

Who: applicant, key participants and landowner (grower and processor). “Key participants” in the federal sense (and in DANR guidance) means sole proprietors, partners and executive managers / persons with executive managerial control. The SD plan’s exact key-participant definition — the DANR page states the categories but the full definition was not opened. How: fingerprint-based DCI + FBI checks; $50 DCI fee per background-check submission (processor guidance); 20+ days’ processing (processor guidance).

Disqualifier — ARSD 12:82:04:03 (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): DANR may deem ineligible and deny, revoke or suspend a licence if any applicant, licensee, key participant or landowner “(3) Has a conviction of a misdemeanor or felony relating to a controlled substance or marijuana under state or federal law in the last ten years, unless participating in a state industrial hemp pilot program authorized under the Agricultural Act of 2014, P.L 113-79, before December 20, 2018.” The same rule also lists violation of state law or USDA hemp rules, false/misleading application information, and being charged (not only convicted) with a controlled-substance/marijuana misdemeanor or felony since the most recent background check (per the ARSD chapter-index excerpt). The “charged” limb in full text — it would be broader than the federal 10-year felony-conviction bar and must not be quoted as settled without the full rule. Hearing: DANR may deny, revoke or suspend a grower or processor licence “following a contested case hearing pursuant to SDCL chapter 1-26” (ARSD 12:82:04:03, same excerpt).

Federal bar (for comparison, and the post-2027 bar): 7 CFR 990.21 / USDA guidance — no licence if convicted of a felony related to a controlled substance in the last 10 years; criminal history report dated within 60 days of application. See §7.7.2. The state pre-2027 rule is broader on its face (misdemeanor included) — flag the difference; do not assume the federal bar replaces the state bar for pre-2027 licences.

7.3 SB 39 in full — what was repealed, what was amended, what was kept

Citation: 2026 SB 39, 2026 Session Laws ch. 167, “An Act to revise provisions relating to industrial hemp.” House 55–12 (5 Mar 2026), Senate 31–3 (10 Mar 2026), signed March 2026 (17 vs 18 Mar — Verify on sdlegislature.gov; the 18 March SDPB story is a publication date, not proof of the signing date, and the enrolled-act signature block retrieved in Run 2 was blank in the excerpt — do not cite either secondary date as the signing date). Effective 1 January 2027 (SB 39 §22: “This Act is effective beginning January 1, 2027”). Sources: enrolled text search excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026: SB 39 enrolled text; session law.

SB 39 sectionActionEffect
§1Amends §38-35-1New/retained definitions, including: “hemp” / “industrial hemp” at ≤0.3% total delta-9 THC, dry weight; “industrial hemp product” at ≤0.3% total delta-9 THC, excluding products containing chemically derived cannabinoids: (a) delta-8 THC, (b) delta-10 THC, (c) THC-O-acetate, (d) hexahydrocannabinol (HHC), (e) tetrahydrocannabiphoral (THCP); “licensed hemp producer,” a person licensed by USDA or a tribal program to produce hemp, as provided in 7 CFR §§990.20 to 990.32 (1 Jan 2026), and any authorised employee or agent of the person; “process” defined as rendering raw hemp plants/parts from natural/original state to an initial processed form. The pre-2027 “applicant” definition (grower/processor licence applicant) is removed from the amended §38-35-1.
§2Amends §38-35-17Transporter documentation (§7.5). References updated to 7 U.S.C. §§1639o–1639s (1 Jan 2026) and 7 U.S.C. ch. 38.
§3Amends §38-35-17.1Licensed-producer transport documentation (§7.5). Failure to possess required documentation is a Class 2 misdemeanor.
§4New section added to ch. 38-35“Only a licensed hemp producer may cultivate hemp in this state.” This is the operative post-2027 cultivation rule.
§5Repeals §38-35-2Repeals the pre-2027 general licensing duty (purchase/receive/obtain for planting, storing, propagating, producing, processing only under a state licence or under a licensee’s contract/direction).
§6Repeals §38-35-3Repeals the pre-2027 grower-licence application section (including the 0.5-acre / 300-plant / 50-plant provision as introduced — see the acreage conflict in §7.2.3).
§7Repeals §38-35-3.1Repeals the research licence.
§8 (in enrolled numbering; §9 in introduced numbering — section numbering between versions)Repeals §38-35-5 (processor-related section in introduced text) / related licensing sectionsContributes to repeal of the state processor licence framework. The exact section-to-section map between introduced, committee-substitute and enrolled versions was not fully reconciled in the excerpts — Verify against the enrolled act’s repeal list before citing a single section number for “processor repeal.” The secondary consensus (THC Group, SDPB, DANR testimony) is unambiguous that grower, processor and research licences are all repealed; the precise SDCL numbers for each repeal are the only Verify item.
§§9–21 (enrolled)Repeals §§38-35-4, -6, -12, -14, -15, -18, -20, -22 (and related provisions, per Run 1)Includes: §38-35-12 (Department of Health hemp rulemaking — testing procedures, labelling requirements, federal-compliance modifications); §38-35-14 (DANR compliance testing by a DEA-approved laboratory, measurement-of-uncertainty reporting); §38-35-15 (state hemp production plan duty — DANR to develop a plan and submit it to USDA for approval); §38-35-18 (applicant/licensee/key participant/transporter compliance with USDA rules and the USDA-approved state plan); §38-35-20 (related programme provision). Consequence: after 1 Jan 2027 there is no SDCL duty for DANR to maintain a USDA-approved state plan, and no SDCL DEA-lab testing section — testing is governed by the USDA plan (7 CFR Part 990) and by retained state transport/sampling enforcement (§7.5).
§22Effective date1 January 2027.

Sections NOT repealed by SB 39 (retained, as far as the excerpts show): §38-35-1 (as amended); §38-35-17 / §38-35-17.1 (transport, as amended); the new §38-35 cultivation-only-by-licensed-producer section; §38-35-21 (smoking/inhaling ban, Class 1 misdemeanor — not listed in the repeal sections); and the SDCL 34-20B-118 chemically derived ban (different chapter, untouched by SB 39). The full post-SB 39 chapter 38-35 table of contents on sdlegislature.gov after 1 Jan 2027 — the excerpts do not list every retained section, and a retained section that is not listed here must not be assumed repealed.

Legislative history note (secondary, dated): SB 39 was brought by DANR at DANR’s request (introduced by the Chair of the Committee on Agriculture and Natural Resources at DANR’s request). DANR Deputy Secretary Brian Walsh testified the state programme was not financially sustainable for the small licensee base (§7.9) and that returning the programme to USDA would save ~$200,000/year in general funds and reduce state FTE burden. SDPB, 18 Mar 2026. These are reasons given in testimony/reporting, not statutory findings, and must be labelled as such.

7.4 Lot / field registration, records, inspections (pre-2027; post-2027 USDA equivalents in §7.7)

Pre-2027 — lot/field registration: Each growing site is registered by legal description + geospatial coordinates (ARSD 12:82:02:02, §7.2.3) and by lot. A “lot” in the SD plan is a contiguous area in a field, greenhouse or indoor growing structure containing the same variety/strain of hemp — the SD plan’s lot definition was not opened in full in this run; Verify against the State Hemp Plan (the federal lot concept in 7 CFR 990.1 is the post-2027 analogue).

Planting verification: “Within 30 days of planting, each licensee shall file documentation with the department to identify the type and variety of industrial hemp seed planted with the corresponding lot. Upon receipt of this documentation a planting verification inspection will be scheduled. This inspection will verify the GPS coordinates of the growing sites, acreage or square feet planted, and the FSA Farm, Tract, and Field Number where hemp is planted. The licensee must be present at the inspection.” Source: DANR page, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026.

Records — ARSD ch. 12:82:03: Grower records maintained 3 years and available for examination, including: geospatial location(s) of each lot; industrial hemp crop acreage; total acreage planted, harvested and disposed; record of all processors sold to and quantities sold to each processor; variety/strain for each lot; copies of all THC test-result notifications for each lot. Processor records (ARSD 12:82:03:03) include: records of all unprocessed hemp purchased from another processor (processor name, address, registration/licence, quantity, transaction date); records of all unprocessed hemp sold to another processor (same fields); and THC byproduct storage and disposal records. Sources: ARSD chapter-index excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Full record lists are Verify — the excerpts are partial.

Inspections — ARSD ch. 12:82:06 and SB 39 testimony: DANR or DPS conducts grower inspections and sampling; the secretary may contract with DPS to conduct inspections and sampling of lots and processor locations; DANR and DPS may enter any land/property where hemp is grown, produced, stored or processed for inspections, sample collection, testing or investigation; a licensee is deemed to have consented to reasonable warrantless search/seizure of hemp to determine lawful total delta-9 THC and for enforcement (pre-2027 §38-35 provision as reproduced in SB 39 committee-substitute text, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026). Whether that consent/entry provision survives SB 39 — it appears in a pre-repeal section; do not cite it as post-2027 law without checking the enrolled repeal list. Post-2027 inspection is primarily USDA audit/inspection under 7 CFR 990.28, plus retained state transport/sampling enforcement (§7.5).

7.5 Sampling, testing, transport documentation, destruction / remediation, penalties (pre-2027, with post-2027 notes)

Sampling and testing — pre-2027

When: DANR or DPS “shall conduct grower inspections and sampling of each lot not more than 30 days before the hemp is harvested.” The licensee pays a grower inspection and sample fee of $250 per hemp lot inspected. Source: DANR page, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026. Lab: Pre-2027 §38-35-14 (repealed by SB 39) required DANR compliance testing by a laboratory approved by the DEA, reporting total delta-9 THC concentration and measurement of uncertainty. Post-2027 lab rule is the USDA rule: testing by a DEA-registered laboratory, with USDA enforcement of that requirement delayed to 31 Dec 2026 under the fixed federal baseline — i.e. the DEA-lab requirement becomes enforceable the day before SB 39 takes effect. The interaction (a crop sampled in Dec 2026 under the state system but harvested/tested in Jan 2027) is Verify in USDA/DANR transition guidance; no such guidance was found in this run. Acceptable level: measurement-of-uncertainty range that includes 0.3% or less total delta-9 THC, dry weight (SD State Hemp Plan, §7.2.1; ARSD 12:82:08:01, below). This is the same concept as the federal “acceptable hemp THC level” in 7 CFR 990.1. Sampling method (pre-2027 plan): all hemp grown under the SD programme is subject to sampling and compliance testing; tests measure total THC using post-decarboxylation or similarly reliable methods considering potential THC (State Hemp Plan excerpt, search-result, accessed 30 Oct 2026). Full sampling guidelines (cutting pattern, flowering-top requirement, sample size) were not opened — Verify against the plan’s Sampling Guidelines appendix.

Transport documentation — retained and amended by SB 39 (this is the post-2027 state-law core)

§38-35-17.1, as amended by SB 39 §3 (enrolled text, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026):

“A licensed hemp producer transporting hemp shall have in the licensee's possession the following: (1) A copy of the producer's license under which the industrial hemp was grown or produced; and (2) A manifest that includes the name and address of the transporter, the lot from which the industrial hemp being transported was harvested, the destination storage location for the hemp, and the type of vehicle being used. Failure to possess the appropriate documentation pursuant to this section is a Class 2 misdemeanor.”

§38-35-17, as amended by SB 39 §2 (non-producer transporters): If the transporter is not a licensed hemp producer, the transporter must possess documentation including a copy of the applicable producer licence, a laboratory report confirming compliance with federal hemp standards, a signed statement regarding the shipment’s contents, and a bill of lading or manifest detailing the shipment. Sources: BillTrack50 summary of SB 39, accessed 30 Oct 2026; Marijuana Herald, 29 Mar 2026 — both secondary summaries of the enrolled text; the full §38-35-17 text was not opened in this run, Verify.

Pre-2027 transport rule (for the 2026 season): secondary summary of §38-35-17 (Run 1 context): transport within the state without a licence is lawful only if the transporter carries copies of the licences under which the hemp was grown and lab reports proving federal compliance. That summary is consistent with the SB 39 amended structure; the difference after 2027 is that the “licence” carried is a USDA/tribal producer licence, not a DANR licence.

Practical consequence: after 1 Jan 2027, a truck stopped in South Dakota carrying hemp must still be able to produce state-law documentation (licence copy + manifest / lab report + bill of lading, depending on whether the transporter is the producer). A USDA licence alone, without the SD transport papers, does not satisfy §§38-35-17/17.1 as amended. DPS retains port/field sampling and transport enforcement (committee testimony, §7.3).

Destruction / remediation — pre-2027

ARSD 12:82:08:01, verbatim in substance (Cornell LII excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: ARSD 12:82:08:01):

“Disposal or remediation is required for any non-compliant industrial hemp lot. If a grower licensee chooses to remediate a non-compliant lot, the licensee must schedule an additional sampling inspection with the Department to retest the remediated lot. If a remediated lot does not meet the acceptable hemp THC level, it is non-compliant and must be destroyed using the United States Department of Agriculture methods. If a grower licensee determines all or a portion of their industrial hemp crop is unfit for commerce due to pests, insects, weeds, disease, poor stand, or industrial hemp lots destroyed by a natural disaster or weather event such as a flood, wind, or hail, the damaged industrial hemp must be disposed in accordance with the United States Department of Agriculture methods.”

Source line in that excerpt: SDR 075, eff. 12/28/2020; 49 SDR 047, eff. 11/22/2022. General Authority: SDCL 38-35-11(7). Law Implemented: SDCL 38-35-9, 38-35-14. Note: SDCL 38-35-14 is repealed by SB 39, so the “Law Implemented” citation for disposal will need re-checking after 1 Jan 2027; the disposal duty itself under ARSD art. 12:82 ch. 08 is Verify post-2027 (§7.2.1 on art. 12:82’s fate). Earlier formulation (search-result excerpt of the same rule, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “Disposal is required for any industrial hemp lot where the acceptable THC level is non-compliant. The acceptable THC level … is when the application of the measurement of uncertainty to the reported THC content concentration level on a dry weight basis produces a distribution or range of 0.3% or less. These non-compliant lots must be disposed of under the direction of, and documented by, the Department of Public Safety.” The two excerpts are consistent; the Cornell version is the later, fuller text and is preferred.

Post-2027 analogue: 7 CFR 990.27 (§7.7.5) — non-compliant plants are marijuana under the CSA and must be disposed of via a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement, or on-site disposal, with USDA notification and verification, and post-remediation re-sampling/testing.

Penalties — pre-2027 hemp (and what survives)

ViolationPenalty / consequence (pre-2027)Post-2027 note

Sale/use of hemp for smoking/inhaling (§38-35-21)Class 1 misdemeanor (up to 1 year / $2,000)Retained — §38-35-21 not repealed by SB 39.

Do not assume the old §38-35-2 penalty carries over.

7.6 Finished-product licences, CBD/hemp-product manufacturer registration, and the post-2027 processor question

Finding: No South Dakota finished-product licence or CBD/hemp-product manufacturer registration was found, pre- or post-2027.

Retail sale of compliant hemp/CBD products: secondary summaries agree no state licence is required to sell, buy or carry hemp-derived CBD products in South Dakota, provided the product is compliant hemp (≤0.3% total delta-9 THC, not chemically derived, not for smoking/inhaling). Sources: SouthDakotaStateCannabis.org CBD page, accessed 30 Oct 2026; PaymentCloud (secondary, undated) — “Merchants can resale out-of-state CBD products in South Dakota provided they meet all federal and state-of-origin cultivation and production standards.” Secondary only — Verify against DANR/DOH product guidance. The “labelling guidelines established by DANR” mentioned in secondary summaries were not located as a standalone DANR labelling rule in this run — Verify (pre-2027 §38-35-12(3) gave DOH rulemaking for hemp-product labelling; that section is repealed by SB 39, so post-2027 labelling authority is Verify).

Manufacture of a finished product from hemp: pre-2027, “processing” hemp (rendering raw plants to an initial processed form) required a DANR processor licence (§7.2.2). Whether turning processed hemp into a finished consumer product (e.g. a CBD oil, topical or edible) required anything beyond the processor licence — e.g. a food establishment licence, a drug/cosmetic registration, or a DOH licence — was not found in this run. No DANR finished-product registration form, fee or rule was located. With DANR and, for food products, the DOH food programme before advising a manufacturer. Do not state that a processor licence alone authorises a finished food/drug product.

Post-2027 processor question — OPEN, flagged prominently: SB 39 repeals the state processor licence and says only a licensed hemp producer may cultivate. It does not, in the excerpts retrieved, say “only a licensed hemp producer may process.” The USDA programme itself licenses producers, not processors/handlers. Three readings are possible and none is adopted here: (1) A South Dakota processor who buys USDA-licensed hemp and processes it needs no state licence after 2027 (processing is unlicensed, subject only to product, food/drug, tax and transport law). (2) A processor who also cultivates, stores or handles unprocessed hemp must be (or work under) a USDA-licensed producer. (3) DANR/USDA transition guidance imposes a processor registration that was not in the SB 39 excerpts. Do not advise a processor to operate after 1 Jan 2027 on the basis of this report alone. Verify with DANR and USDA AMS before the 2027 season. This is the single largest open licensing question in Part 7.

Medical cannabis manufacturing is a different licence and is NOT affected by SB 39. A medical cannabis product manufacturing facility under SDCL ch. 34-20G (DOH registration certificate, $9,000 — Run 1, Part 4) is not a hemp processor licence. The two must never be conflated. A hemp processor licence never authorised medical cannabis manufacturing, and a medical cannabis manufacturing certificate never authorised hemp processing for the commercial hemp market.

Food-service licence for medical cannabis edibles (for contrast, not hemp): DOH Manufacturing Checklist (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026) states: “If edibles will be manufactured, a food establishment license must be obtained” (ARSD 44:90:07:09(1)) and “Identify food service manager and upload a copy of certified food service manager’s certificate” (44:90:07:09(2)). That is a medical cannabis rule, cited here only to show that finished-product food licensing exists in the medical programme; no equivalent hemp-edible food-licence rule was located — Verify whether hemp edibles fall under general food law.

7.7 USDA-direct licensing procedure after 1 January 2027

When this procedure applies: to any person producing hemp in South Dakota outside tribal jurisdiction on or after 1 Jan 2027, because South Dakota will no longer have a USDA-approved state plan. Until 31 Dec 2026, a South Dakota producer must still be licensed under the state plan (DANR), not directly by USDA — USDA’s own rule is explicit: “If your state or tribe has an approved plan or is in the process of developing a plan, you must apply to and be licensed or authorized under its hemp program” (AMS grower page, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: USDA AMS information for hemp growers). Applying to USDA in 2026 for a South Dakota (non-tribal) site, while the state plan is still approved, is therefore the wrong route — Verify with AMS if a producer wants to pre-apply for a 2027 licence, and whether USDA accepts applications in advance of the state plan’s end.

Authority: 7 CFR Part 990, Subpart C — USDA Hemp Production Plan (86 FR 5680, 19 Jan 2021, eff. 22 Mar 2021). SB 39 incorporates “7 C.F.R. §§990.20 to 990.32, inclusive (January 1, 2026)” into the SD definition of “licensed hemp producer.” Note the incorporation date: SB 39 freezes the CFR version at 1 Jan 2026 for SD purposes. If USDA amends Part 990 after that date, whether SD law follows the amendment automatically is Verify — do not assume it does.

7.7.1 Step-by-step USDA application (from 7 CFR 990.21 and AMS grower pages, search-result excerpts)

  1. Create an account on the Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP) and submit a USDA Hemp Application through HeMP. Source: AMS Hemp Production page, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: USDA AMS hemp production — “To obtain a license to produce hemp under the USDA production program, create an account using the Hemp eManagement Platform (HeMP) and use HeMP to submit a USDA Hemp Application. USDA hemp production applications are accepted on a rolling basis throughout the year.” AMS contact given on the same page: farmbill.hemp@usda.gov (USDA’s published programme contact, not a personal email).
  2. Application timing: “Applicants may submit an application for a license at any time” (7 CFR 990.21(a)(2), via Cornell excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: 7 CFR 990.21). There is no single annual USDA deadline; the rolling basis is the point. Whether AMS imposes a practical cut-off for a given growing season (e.g. background-check processing time) — the regulation does not state one.
  3. Information required (7 CFR 990.21(a)(3), same Cornell excerpt): Contact information: full name, residential address, telephone number and email address. If a business entity: full business name, principal business location address, full name and title of key participants, title, email address (if available) and employer identification number (EIN). Criminal history report: a current report for the individual, or for all key participants if a business entity, dated within 60 days of the application submission date. “A license application will not be considered complete without all required criminal history reports.” Completed forms submitted to USDA (HeMP or, on request, a mailed/PDF version — AMS grower page states a mailed or PDF version may be requested via farmbill.hemp@usda.gov).
  4. Land / lot information: Although 990.21(a)(3) as excerpted lists only contact + criminal history, the USDA programme as a whole requires the producer to report hemp crop acreage to the Farm Service Agency (FSA) (§7.7.3) and to identify lots for sampling. The full HeMP application’s land fields (legal description, geospatial coordinates, acreage/square footage, intended use — fibre, grain, seed, CBD) were not opened in this run — Verify in HeMP / AMS application instructions before filing. Do not file on the basis of the two 990.21(a)(3) limbs alone.
  5. USDA decision: USDA provides written notification whether the application is approved or denied (7 CFR 990.22, via govinfo excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026). If approved, a licence is issued. Licences may not be sold, assigned, transferred, pledged or otherwise disposed of, alienated or encumbered (same excerpt). A licence shall not be issued unless the applicant submitted all required reports as a programme participant, the application contains no materially false statements, and other 990.22 criteria are met — full 990.22 approval criteria are Verify (the excerpt is partial).
  6. USDA fee: Unavailable in this run. No USDA application or licence fee figure was retrieved from 7 CFR 990.21/990.22 excerpts or AMS grower pages in this run. Do not state that USDA licensing is free, and do not import a fee from another state’s programme. In HeMP / AMS fee guidance before budgeting. (For contrast only: the pre-2027 DANR fees were $50 + $500 grower / $50 + $2,000 processor; those fees do not carry over to USDA.)

7.7.2 Eligibility and background checks (USDA)

Felony bar, verbatim in substance (AMS grower page, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “You cannot receive a hemp production license from a state, tribe, or USDA, if you have been convicted of a felony related to a controlled substance in the last 10 years.” Key participants: every key participant of a business entity needs a criminal history report dated within 60 days (§7.7.1). “Key participant” under 7 CFR 990.1 means a sole proprietor, a partner in a partnership, or a person with executive managerial control in a corporation — The exact 990.1 definition in the current eCFR; the secondary summary in this run states “owners, officers, and anyone with decision-making authority,” which is broader and must not be quoted as the regulation. Difference from pre-2027 SD rule: federal bar is felony-only, 10 years; pre-2027 SD ARSD 12:82:04:03 on its face included misdemeanors and a “charged” limb (§7.2.5). A person barred under the old SD rule but not under the federal rule, or vice versa, must get advice on which regime governs the season in question — the regimes change on 1 Jan 2027.

7.7.3 Term, renewal, modification (USDA)

Term, verbatim (7 CFR 990.21(a)(6), Cornell excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “USDA-issued hemp producer licenses shall be valid until December 31 of the year three years after the year in which license was issued.” Example: a licence issued in 2027 is valid until 31 Dec 2030. This is not the DANR 15-month term — do not carry the 15-month figure into the USDA era. Renewal (7 CFR 990.21(b), same excerpt): “USDA hemp producer licenses must be renewed prior to license expiration. Licenses are not automatically renewed. Applications for renewal shall be subject to the same terms, information collection requirements, and approval criteria as provided in this subpart for initial applications unless there has been an amendment to the regulations in this part or the law since approval of the initial or last application.” Modification (7 CFR 990.21(c), same excerpt): “A license modification is required if there is any change to the information submitted in the application including, but not limited to, sale of a business, the production of hemp in a new location, or a change in the key participants under a license.” Research producers (7 CFR 990.21(d), via 990.28 excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “Producers that produce hemp for research must obtain a USDA license. However, the hemp that is produced for research and does not enter the stream of commerce is not subject to the sampling requirements in §§990.24 and 990.26; provided that the producer adopts and carries out a USDA approved alternative sampling method that has the potential to ensure, at a confidence level of 95 percent, that the cannabis plant species Cannabis sativa L. that will be subject to this alternative method will not test above the acceptable hemp THC level.” Only DEA-registered research institutions may keep hemp testing over 0.3% until the end of a study; all other USDA licensees must dispose of non-compliant plants under §990.27.

7.7.4 Reporting acreage, sampling, testing (USDA)

FSA acreage report (7 CFR 990.23, via govinfo excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “All USDA licensees shall report hemp crop acreage with USDA Farm Service Agency.” Secondary summary (consistent with the regulation): report within 30 days of planting, including street address and geospatial location of the growing site, acreage or indoor square footage dedicated to hemp, and the producer’s hemp licence number; intended use (fibre, CBD, grain or seed) is also reported. Source: LegalClarity summary of 990.23, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — secondary; Verify the 30-day deadline in the full 990.23 text (the govinfo excerpt confirms the FSA-report duty but the deadline line was truncated in the excerpt).

Pre-harvest sampling (7 CFR 990.24/990.25, govinfo excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “No more than 30 days prior to the anticipated harvest of cannabis plants, [the producer shall] have a sampling agent collect samples from the cannabis plant for total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol concentration” (990.25, in substance). Samples are cut from the flowering top (a bud) of the plant; the sampling method must be sufficient at a 95% confidence level that no more than 1% of plants in the lot exceed the acceptable level; sampling-agent guidelines and training requirements are available from USDA; measurement of uncertainty (MU) must be estimated and reported with the test result. Testing must use appropriate, validated methods, demonstrate that the method is fit for purpose, and that the laboratory can successfully perform the testing.

Harvest window (7 CFR 990.26, govinfo excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “The producer shall harvest the crop no later than thirty (30) days after the date of sample collection.” If harvest is not completed within 30 days, a second pre-harvest sampling/testing is required. Any producer may request additional pre-harvest testing if the original total delta-9 result is believed to be in error (same excerpt, in substance). DEA-registered lab: “After December 31, 2022, USDA licensees may only use laboratories registered with the DEA to conduct testing” (990.26/990.28 excerpt, same govinfo URL). Under the fixed federal baseline, USDA enforcement of the DEA-lab requirement is delayed to 31 Dec 2026 — the requirement itself predates the delay; the delay is an enforcement discretion, not a repeal. Current AMS enforcement-discretion guidance after 31 Dec 2026.

Acceptable level (7 CFR 990.1, eCFR excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: 7 CFR Part 990, eCFR): “The acceptable hemp THC level … is when the application of the measurement of uncertainty to the reported total delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinol content concentration level on a dry weight basis produces a distribution or range that includes 0.3 percent or less.” Federal total-THC change: after 11 Dec 2026 (fixed baseline), the federal hemp definition becomes a full total-THC definition with a 0.4 mg per-container cap. Whether 7 CFR Part 990’s “total delta-9” sampling/testing language is amended to match the new federal definition, and on what date, is Verify — do not assume Part 990 automatically updates on 12 Nov / 11 Dec 2026.

7.7.5 Disposal, remediation, violations, audits (USDA)

Non-compliant plants (7 CFR 990.27, via 990.28 excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026):

“Cannabis plants exceeding the acceptable hemp THC level constitute marijuana, a schedule I controlled substance under the Controlled Substances Act (CSA), 21 U.S.C. 801 et seq., and producers must either use a DEA-registered reverse distributor or law enforcement to dispose of noncompliant plants or ensure the disposal of such cannabis plant on site at the farm or hemp production facility.”
“Producers must notify USDA of their intent to dispose of or remediate non-conforming plants and verify disposal or remediation by submitting required documentation.”
“If a producer elects to perform remediation activities, an additional sampling and testing of the post-remediated crop must occur to determine THC concentration levels.”

Approved disposal methods (USDA Remediation and Disposal Guidelines, via secondary summary, accessed 30 Oct 2026): plowing under, composting, disking, bush mowing, deep burial, burning. Producer pays all costs of resampling, remediation and disposal. Secondary — Verify against AMS’s current Remediation and Disposal Guidelines.

Negligent violations / corrective action (7 CFR 990.28, same excerpts): USDA licensees may be audited (records review and site visits to farms, fields, greenhouses, storage facilities or other affiliated locations); audit verifications may be performed once every 3 years unless otherwise determined; if an audit finds negligent violations, USDA shall require a corrective action plan with a reasonable date for correction; a negligent violation includes producing hemp over the acceptable level (treated as negligent, not criminal, on first occurrence in the federal scheme). Secondary summary adds: Notice of Violation + corrective action plan for at least 2 years; three negligent violations in 5 years = licence revocation for 5 years; a crop over 0.3% but under 1.0% is treated as negligent, while over 1.0% raises intentional-violation / criminal-referral possibility. The 2-year plan duration, 3-in-5 revocation and 1.0% line are secondary summaries of 990.28/990.29 — Verify in the full eCFR text before advising on consequences. The SD pre-2027 analogue (3 negligent in 5 years = 5-year ineligibility, §7.5) is similar but not identical; do not merge the two. Records: USDA licensees must comply with reporting in §990.71, including reporting disposal of non-compliant plants (990.21(d)(3), same excerpt). Full §990.71 record-retention period was not retrieved — Verify (the SD pre-2027 period was 3 years; do not assume the federal period is the same).

7.8 Tribal hemp programmes

Finding (AUDIT-CORRECTED): South Dakota has six tribes with USDA-approved hemp plans on the 17 February 2026 AMS list, and those plans are NOT affected by SB 39 — but the six are NOT the six named in the original Run 2 text. The approved six are Cheyenne River Sioux, Crow Creek Sioux Tribe, Flandreau Santee Sioux, Oglala Sioux Tribe, Rosebud Sioux Tribe and Standing Rock Sioux Tribe. Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate is not an Approved Plan on that list; it is listed as “USDA Hemp Producer License.” Source: USDA AMS plan list, updated 17 Feb 2026 (opened in this audit). SB 39 repeals the state plan; it does not repeal tribal plans, and its new definition of “licensed hemp producer” expressly includes “a person licensed by … a tribal program.”

USDA approved-plans list, updated 17 Feb 2026 (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: USDA AMS plan review): the tribal section lists Flandreau Santee Sioux — Approved Plan and Oglala Sioux Tribe — Approved Plan (among others). A fuller secondary compilation (500 Nations, synthesising USDA approvals to Feb 2021) lists Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate in place of Crow Creek — that compilation is stale and is superseded by the 17 Feb 2026 AMS list for current status. Do not use the Feb 2021 secondary list for a 2026 filing decision. USDA’s first approvals included Flandreau Santee Sioux (announced Dec 2019 / Jan 2020; USDA GovDelivery) and Oglala Sioux (approved 3 Apr 2020, per 500 Nations summary of USDA announcement).

What a tribal plan means in practice: A tribal plan, once approved, lets the tribe regulate hemp production within its geographical jurisdiction (tribal land). A producer on tribal land must be licensed under the tribal plan (or, if the tribe has no plan, under USDA) — not under the SD state plan, and after 2027 not under a DANR licence that no longer exists. Flandreau’s plan was reported at approval as covering 1,000 acres of reservation land (500 Nations summary — secondary, dated Jan 2020, Verify current acreage/scope in the tribe’s approved plan PDF). Yankton, Crow Creek and Lower Brule (AUDIT-CORRECTED): Crow Creek does have an Approved Plan on the 17 Feb 2026 AMS list. Lower Brule is listed as “USDA Hemp Producer License” (USDA-direct, not an approved tribal plan). Yankton does not appear on that list at all. Producers on Lower Brule or Yankton lands, if any, fall under the USDA programme directly unless a tribal plan is later approved — verify on the live AMS list before filing. Source: USDA AMS plan list, updated 17 Feb 2026. Tribal plan texts, fees, sampling and enforcement for each tribe were NOT retrieved in this run — Unavailable. The AMS list links each tribe’s approved plan PDF; those PDFs must be opened tribe by tribe in Run 2 follow-up or Part 10. Do not apply SD DANR fees, SD sampling windows or SD penalties to a tribal-plan producer. Jurisdiction warning: a tribal hemp licence does not authorise cultivation off tribal land, and a USDA/state licence does not authorise cultivation on tribal land under a tribal plan. Transport across tribal/state boundaries must carry the documentation required by both the producing jurisdiction and SD §§38-35-17/17.1 (§7.5) — Verify the cross-boundary documentation rule with the tribe and DPS before moving hemp across a reservation boundary. Tribal medical vs tribal hemp — do not conflate: a tribe’s USDA-approved hemp plan is a hemp-production plan under 7 U.S.C. §§1639o–1639s. It is not a medical cannabis programme, not a “state-licensed” medical programme for Schedule III purposes, and not an adult-use licence. The tribal-medical Schedule III question remains Verify.

7.9 Hemp licence data — what exists publicly, and reconciliation of counts vs acreage

7.9.1 No public licence-holder list — confirmed again in Run 2

DANR: no searchable licence-holder list, CSV/XLSX or published grower/processor roster was found in Run 1 or Run 2. The DANR page gives programme rules and application guidance, not a register. Unavailable. USDA HeMP Public Search Tool: exists (HeMP Public Search Tool — URL from Run 1; not opened in this run, Verify). CSBS JobAid, 19 May 2026, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026, describes it as the “U.S. Domestic Hemp Production Program’s New Public HeMP Search Tool” usable to verify a borrower’s licence validity. Its SD result fields, export ability, and whether it returns state-plan producers, tribal-plan producers, USDA-direct producers, or all three, are Verify. It must be checked live after 1 Jan 2027 for SD USDA-direct producers; it cannot be cited in this report as a source of SD names/counts because no SD extract was retrieved. USDA Law Enforcement Search Tool: not public — access only on request to FarmBill.Hemp@usda.gov with eAuthentication (Run 1, USDA guide). Do not present it as a public source. Secretary of State business search: proves entity existence only, not hemp licence status (Run 1, Part 5.3). Same limitation applies to hemp as to medical cannabis.

7.9.2 Licence counts — the only figures retrieved, with tiers
