7.9.3 Reconciliation: licence counts vs NASS acreage — why they do not line up, and why that is expected

Run 1 NASS figures (official statistical, not licence data): 2023 — 3,200 planted / 2,900 harvested; 2024 — 3,900 planted / 3,700 harvested, fibre 3,550 acres, 13.6m lb, fibre value $3.54m. The 2025 testimony figure (~1,100 harvested acres) is far below NASS 2024 harvested acreage (3,700). The gap must not be averaged or explained away. Plausible explanations, all Verify, none adopted: (a) Different universes: NASS surveys all hemp operations meeting its survey frame (including tribal-plan producers, USDA-direct producers, and operations that NASS captures but DANR’s state-plan count does not); DANR testimony counts state-plan licensees only. (b) Different measures: “harvested acres” in testimony may mean acres harvested under state-plan licences and reported to DANR, while NASS “harvested” means acres harvested for any use in the survey response. (c) Real contraction: the state-plan base may genuinely have shrunk from 2024 to 2025 (17 growers is a small base; a few growers exiting moves the total sharply). (d) Timing: NASS 2025 data had not been released at the research date (Run 1: 2025/2026 NASS acreage Unavailable); testimony and NASS are therefore not same-year comparators for 2025. Rule for republication: present DANR counts and NASS acreage in separate tables with separate sources, never as a single “acres per licence” calculation, and never present a NASS acreage figure as a licence count.

7.9.4 Hemp production economics — only from dated, labelled sources

Official figures only (NASS, Run 1, not re-audited in Run 2): SD fibre value $3.54 million in 2024 (NASS SD release, 17 Apr 2025: NASS SD hemp release 2025 — Run 1 search-result; not re-opened in Run 2). No official SD figure for hemp-derived product (CBD, etc.) retail sales, farm-gate price per pound, or cost of production was found in Run 1 or Run 2 — Unavailable (official).

Secondary, dated, labelled: SD Industrial Hemp Association board member via SD News Watch (Run 1): SD 2023 hemp crop value “more than $23 million”; SD “recently became the No. 1 producer of hemp fibers,” highest production and yield-per-acre. Industry-association figure, not NASS — do not mix with NASS totals. Dakota Hemp LLC (Wakonda): planted 40 acres in 2021, expanded to 450 acres in 2024 (SD News Watch, Run 1, secondary). Complete Hemp Processing, Winfred, is the existing processor cited in the same reporting; a second Dakota Hemp processing facility (Wakonda) was slated to begin operation by end of 2026 (SD News Watch, Nov 2025–2026 article, Run 1 — Verify facility-opening status before stating it as operating).

Licence economics a producer can actually budget from this report (pre-2027 only): DANR application $50 + licence $500 (grower) or $2,000 (processor), 15-month term; grower inspection/sample $250 per lot; processor inspection $500 per location; background-check $50 (DCI). All other costs — seed, land, equipment, labour, testing beyond the $250 lot fee, transport, processing, compliance — are Unavailable (official) in this run and must not be estimated from other states’ figures. Post-2027 economics: USDA licence fee Unavailable (§7.7.1); USDA sampling/testing costs are producer-paid (sampling agent + DEA-registered lab fees — no dollar figure retrieved, Verify); disposal/remediation costs are producer-paid (§7.7.5). No post-2027 SD hemp cost estimate is offered.

Part 8 — Cannabis-Zoned Properties

8.1 The state floor that every property search must start from — SDCL 34-20G-55

State rule, verbatim in substance (Run 1, opened directly 30 Oct 2026: SDCL 34-20G-55): DOH shall register a prospective establishment and issue a certificate (with a random 10-digit alphanumeric identification number) not later than 90 days after receiving an application if, among other conditions, the application includes “(ii) The physical address… that is not within one thousand feet of a public or private school existing before the date of the medical cannabis establishment application.”

Three consequences that property searchers routinely get wrong:

  1. The school must exist before the application date. A school that opens after the establishment application does not retroactively disqualify the address under §34-20G-55(1)(b)(ii) as written. Verify how DOH measures “existing before” in practice (school licensing date vs building occupancy vs first school year) — the statute does not define it in the excerpt.
  2. The state floor is a floor, not the whole test. Every locality in §8.3 adds its own districts, setbacks (church, park, daycare, residence, other dispensary), hours and licensing. A property that passes the 1,000-ft school test can still fail a local test. Conversely, a local waiver (see §8.2) can never waive the state school distance — Lawrence County’s rule states this expressly (§8.3.13), and the same principle applies everywhere: “Any separation distance requirement, other than the state requirement from schools (1,000 feet), may be waived” (Lawrence County §II-10.013(B)(7), search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: Lawrence County §II-10.013).
  3. Measurement method varies by jurisdiction and must be checked parcel by parcel. Examples in this report: Lawrence County measures in a straight line from exterior walls of the establishment building (or portion thereof) to the property line of the protected use (§II-10.013(B)(1)–(3)), but its subsection (B)(8) then states distances “are to be measured from the lot line of the property where the dispensary is proposed” — the two measurement statements in the same section are in tension; Verify which controls for a given parcel. Vermillion measures “from closest property-line points” (Run 1, Ch. 123). Sturgis measures “from closest property-line points, excluding public rights-of-way” (Run 1, Ord. 2021-07). Sioux Falls’ joint ordinance measures “by a straight line in all directions, without regard to intervening structures or objects, from the nearest point on the property line” (search-result excerpt, §8.3.1). A GIS buffer drawn with the wrong origin (building wall vs lot line vs parcel centroid) can produce a false pass or false fail. The only safe method is a surveyor/planner measurement using the controlling ordinance’s stated origin, confirmed in writing by the zoning official.

DOH checklist restatement (Run 1, and DOH checklists retrieved in Run 2 search excerpts): DOH application checklists state the location test as “Allowable Location SDCL 34-20G-55 • Not within 1,000 feet of a public or private school” (DOH Dispensary/Cultivation/Testing Checklists, 2025, search-result excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026). The checklists do not substitute for the local zoning check.

8.2 How to find a cannabis-zoned property in South Dakota — method, in order

There is no statewide “cannabis-zoned properties” map, list or search filter. No DOH, DANR, Secretary of State or commercial source in this run publishes a parcel-level list of properties that are zoned for medical cannabis. The method below is therefore a process, not a database query, and each step must be documented for the specific parcel.

Step 1 — Fix the jurisdiction first (city vs county vs joint vs tribal)

  1. Identify the exact parcel (address + legal description + parcel ID).
  2. Determine whether the parcel is: (a) inside a municipality with its own §34-20G-58 ordinance → municipal rule governs; (b) outside any such municipality → county rule governs; (c) inside a joint-jurisdiction / extra-territorial area (Sioux Falls–Minnehaha–Lincoln joint area up to 3 miles beyond Sioux Falls; Pierre–Hughes 3-mile ETJ; Huron–Beadle joint area; Brookings city/county joint area) → joint ordinance governs, and the city-only and county-only rules do not by themselves decide the parcel; (d) on tribal land → state/county/city cannabis zoning does not govern; tribal law governs (Part 10 — not covered here beyond this warning).
  3. Do not use a city name as a jurisdiction. “Sioux Falls” the postal city includes parcels in Minnehaha County, Lincoln County, and the joint area, each under different rules. “Box Elder” straddles Pennington and Meade Counties. “Pierre” and “Fort Pierre” are separate cities in separate counties (Hughes vs Stanley) across the Missouri River — see §8.3.8.

Step 2 — Check the local licence slot before spending on the property

A zoned parcel is worthless without a licence slot. Order of checks (Run 1, Parts 3–4, not re-researched except as noted): Is there a local cap, and is it full? Sioux Falls 5 (full in practice — lottery 2021); Spearfish 1; Yankton 2; Pierre 3 dispensaries + 1 each other type; Rapid City population formula (15 in 2022, current figure disputed — Verify); Minnehaha County 1 (unincorporated); Lawrence County 1; Clay County 1 dispensary + 1 manufacturing; Meade County 1 in Aug 2022, special-election proposal to 12 total with result not found — Verify. Is the type allowed at all in that jurisdiction? Sioux Falls city: dispensary + testing only (no cultivation/manufacturing). Clay County: no cultivation/testing. Minnehaha County: no cultivation/manufacturing (secondary). Pierre ETJ: zero of any type. Is the local licence transferable to a new owner/location? Vermillion: non-transferable, lapses automatically on ownership/location change (Run 1, Ch. 123). Sioux Falls: transferable only under §121.003 criteria. Brookings: transfer application with full ARSD 44:90:03:01 information. A property whose value depends on an existing licence that cannot transfer is a different asset from a property that can support a new licence application — price them differently and say so. Is the state certificate available for that locality? State scoring + lottery (ARSD 44:90:03:15/:16) applies where applicants exceed the local allowance; Rapid City 2022 (47 applicants, all tied, lottery 9 Mar 2022 for 15 certificates, Puffy’s, 2025 S.D. 10) is the documented example. A parcel in an oversubscribed locality does not guarantee a certificate even if zoning passes.

Step 3 — Check zoning district, then setbacks, then use type

  1. District: Is the establishment type a permitted use, a permitted special use, or a conditional use in that parcel’s district? Examples: Watertown dispensary in C-1/C-2/C-3, cultivation/manufacturing/testing in I-1/I-2; Pierre all types in industrial park/light/heavy industrial, dispensaries+testing also in central/local business districts; Minnehaha County dispensary in Commercial and I-1 Light Ag (2021 commission action, see §8.3.1). If the type is not listed for the district, the parcel fails unless a rezoning, conditional-use permit or variance is granted — see Step 4.
  2. Setbacks: Measure every applicable separation from the parcel using the controlling ordinance’s origin (see §8.1): state school 1,000 ft (always); local school/church/park/daycare/residence/other-dispensary distances (jurisdiction-specific, §8.3). Include existing-before-application qualifiers where the ordinance states them (e.g. Fort Pierre: “existing before the date of the cannabis establishment application”).
  3. Other locational bars: no residence / mixed-use building containing a residence (Lawrence County, Brookings Ord. 22-026); no dwelling unit / paediatrician’s office / hotel-motel building (Brookings); no sharing premises or direct access from another establishment or an alcohol/tobacco business (Lawrence County, Sioux Falls Ch. 121); no establishment in a prohibited district list (Sturgis, §8.3.13).

Step 4 — Conditional-use permits, variances, and waivers (what they can and cannot do)

Conditional use (also called “permitted special use” in Sioux Falls/Watertown usage): where the ordinance lists the establishment type as a conditional/permitted-special use in the district, the applicant applies to the planning commission / board (Minnehaha County: application to Planning & Zoning, planning board vote, appeal to County Commission — Garretson Gazette summary of Minnehaha County action, 2021, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — secondary summary of procedure, Verify current Minnehaha procedure). Sioux Falls §160.616 (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: Sioux Falls §160.616) provides the conditional-use standard for medical cannabis dispensaries:

“Medical cannabis dispensary. The permitted special standards may be reduced to no less than 500 feet from sensitive land uses if the planning commission finds that the plan performs at the same or higher level with reduced separation distances. The applicant must then include mitigation measures that for example utilize topography, higher level of aesthetics, or barriers that are used to provide the same or higher levels of land use transitions. For no reason shall a reduction in the separation distance between two medical cannabis dispensaries be allowed as provided by the standards of the permitted special use.”

Read that carefully: Sioux Falls conditional use can reduce a sensitive-use separation to no less than 500 ft, with mitigation findings — but it cannot reduce the dispensary-to-dispensary separation, and it cannot reduce the state 1,000-ft school distance. A broker who says “we can get a variance to 500 ft from the school” is wrong on the state rule.

Waiver by the benefiting landowner: Lawrence County (§II-10.013(B)(7)) and Fort Pierre (§160.03(B)(3)) both allow a non-school separation to be waived only if the applicant provides documentation waiving the setback from the title holder of the land benefiting from the separation. That is a private waiver, not a planning-board discretion — the neighbour must agree in a document. Verify whether other jurisdictions have a similar waiver; do not assume they do.

Variance (general zoning): South Dakota zoning enabling law (SDCL ch. 11-2 for counties, ch. 11-4 for municipalities — Verify the current variance standard and board for each jurisdiction) generally requires a showing of unnecessary hardship / practical difficulty unique to the parcel, not mere economic advantage. No cannabis-specific variance standard was found in this run. A variance that would effectively nullify a local cap or the state school distance should be treated as Verify / likely unavailable until a zoning attorney confirms it for that jurisdiction.

Rezoning: a parcel can be rezoned to a district that allows the type, but rezoning is a legislative act (planning commission recommendation + governing-body ordinance, with notice/hearing), not an administrative permit, and it does not guarantee a licence slot or a state certificate. Do not price a rezoning-contingent parcel as if rezoning were granted.

Step 5 — Use county GIS / assessor and city zoning maps (with their limits)

What GIS can do: identify the parcel, its zoning district, its lot lines, nearby parcels’ uses, and (in some viewers) school/park/church layers for a first-pass buffer. What GIS cannot do: grant a licence, confirm a licence slot, measure a separation under the controlling ordinance’s origin, or substitute for a written zoning verification from the zoning official.

GIS / assessor entry points found in this run (all via secondary directories, NOT opened directly in this run — Verify live before publishing as “official GIS links”):

JurisdictionGIS / assessor entry point as cited in secondary directories (accessed 30 Oct 2026)What it is said to offer
City of Sioux Fallshttps://cityofsfgis.maps.arcgis.com/apps/OnePane/basicviewer/index.html (via CountyOffice Minnehaha directory)Parcel viewer, zoning information, aerial photos, parcel finder
Minnehaha Countyhttps://gis.minnehahacounty.org/minnemapviewer/ ; https://mcgis-minnehahacounty.opendata.arcgis.com/ (open data)County GIS map: assessment, school districts, aerial photos, topography, floodplain, aquifer; search by address, parcel ID, map ID or owner name

Do not invent a GIS URL for those counties.

Method note: for every parcel, the searcher should (a) pull the parcel’s zoning district from the city/county GIS or zoning map, (b) pull the official zoning ordinance text for that district, (c) draw buffers using the ordinance’s measurement origin, (d) list every protected use inside each buffer, and (e) obtain a written zoning verification / Form E-type local compliance certification from the zoning official before signing. Form E (DOH Local Government Compliance Certification, Run 1) is the state-application bridge document that proves the locality has confirmed zoning/ordinance compliance — Verify the current Form E (Run 1: 2021 version retrieved).

8.3 Jurisdiction-by-jurisdiction zoning — districts, setbacks, hours, site rules

How to read this section: “Run 1” = carried forward from Run 1, Parts 3–4, not re-opened in Run 2 unless a new source is cited. “New in Run 2” = found in this run’s searches. “Verify” = current text not confirmed. All places are the South Dakota places; same-name warnings from Run 1 apply throughout (Sioux Falls SD ≠ Sioux City IA; Aberdeen SD ≠ Aberdeen WA/Scotland; Brookings SD ≠ Brookings OR; Watertown SD (Codington) ≠ Watertown NY; Huron SD (Beadle) ≠ Huron OH; Vermillion SD (Clay) ≠ Vermilion OH; Sturgis SD ≠ Sturgis MI; Box Elder SD ≠ Box Elder UT).

8.3.1 Sioux Falls (city) + Minnehaha County + Joint Sioux Falls/Minnehaha/Lincoln area

City of Sioux Falls — Ch. 121 (licensing) + Ch. 160 (zoning) — Run 1 + New in Run 2: Types: dispensary + testing only in city limits; cultivation/manufacturing not permitted (Run 1, §121.002(b)(1)). Cap: 5 dispensaries (Run 1). New in Run 2 — testing facility zoning (§160.344, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: Sioux Falls §160.344): “(e) Permitted special: Cannabis testing facility. (1) Placed beyond 1,000 feet from sensitive uses. (2) One thousand feet separation of medical cannabis dispensary from each other. (3) Posting a sign at the entrance that shall state the nature of the business. (Ord. 30-23, passed 5-2-2023).” Note: the excerpt is headed §160.344 Principal Uses and lists testing as a permitted special use; the district(s) to which §160.344 applies were not in the excerpt — Verify the district list for §160.344 before stating “testing is allowed in [district].” The 1,000-ft sensitive-use and 1,000-ft dispensary-to-dispensary figures are consistent with Run 1’s final-passage summary. New in Run 2 — conditional-use reduction (§160.616, §8.2): sensitive-use separation may be reduced to no less than 500 ft with planning-commission findings + mitigation (topography, aesthetics, barriers); no reduction of dispensary-to-dispensary separation is allowed. State school 1,000 ft is not reducible by this provision. Hours (Run 1): dispensary 8:00 a.m.–10:00 p.m. (§121.002(b)(4)). No consumption on premises; no sharing space with another establishment or a practitioner (Run 1).

Joint area (Sioux Falls + Minnehaha + Lincoln Counties, up to 3 miles beyond city limits) — New in Run 2 + Run 1: Revised Joint Zoning Ordinance for Minnehaha County and the City of Sioux Falls (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — note the excerpt cites a Comprehensive Plan adopted 26 May 2026 and amendments MC30-57-26, i.e. the joint ordinance has been amended since 2021; Verify the current joint text). Joint rules in the excerpt:

“The facility shall not be operated or maintained on a parcel within 1,000 feet, measured by a straight line in all directions, without regard to intervening structures or objects, from the nearest point on the property line of a parcel containing a single-family dwelling, church, elementary, middle, or high school licensed by the state, day care, public use facility, park or other medical [cannabis establishment — truncated in excerpt].”
“There shall be no emission of dust, fumes, vapors, or odors which can be seen, smelled, or otherwise perceived from beyond the lot line for the property where the facility is operating.”
“No one under the age of eighteen (18) shall be permitted in the facility.”
“No use of medical cannabis shall be permitted on the premises of the facility.”
“The facility shall submit a disposal plan to, and obtain approval from, the Planning Director or his or her designee. Medical cannabis remnants and by-products shall be disposed of according to an approved plan, and shall not be placed within an exterior refuse container.”
“The facility shall at all times operate in compliance with all South Dakota Department of Health regulations pertaining to such facilities.”

2021 joint-area reporting (secondary, KELO, 25 Aug 2021, accessed 30 Oct 2026): joint zoning allows dispensaries, testing, cultivation and manufacturing, 1,000 ft from schools, single-family homes, churches, public service facilities and parks, and 1,000 ft from one another, with multiple establishments permitted at a single location; amendments to reduce non-school distances to 500 ft and to measure between building walls (not property lines) were defeated (joint commission 3–2; city council amendment also defeated). That 2021 reporting is consistent with the joint excerpt above, but the joint ordinance’s 2026 amendments must be checked — do not cite the 2021 joint text as current without that check.

Minnehaha County (unincorporated, outside joint area and outside cities with their own ordinances) — Run 1 + New in Run 2: Ordinance MC 60-21; 1 dispensary (unincorporated); no county cultivation/manufacturing (secondary). New in Run 2 — district + conditional use (Garretson Gazette summary of 28 Sept / 5 Oct 2021 commission action, accessed 30 Oct 2026): commission voted 5–0 to change the county zoning ordinance “to allow for medical marijuana dispensaries in Commercial and I-1 Light Ag zoned areas” and “To automatically put a dispensary in these areas, that business must meet all of the 11 stipulations, which include registration with the SD Department of Health, a 1,000-foot set-back from homes, schools, parks, etc., no drive-thru service or external sales, security measures, among other requirements. However, if a business does not meet the requirements, the owners can still apply for a conditional use permit to operate their dispensary” (application to Planning & Zoning, planning board vote, appeal to County Commission). The 11 stipulations were not listed in full in the excerpt — Verify in MC 60-21 / county zoning ordinance. Hours (Run 1, secondary): 8 a.m.–10 p.m., closed on holidays.

8.3.5 Watertown (city) + Codington County

City — Ord. 21-34 (Title 25, licensing/zoning/enforcement) + Ord. 21-36 (Title 21 zoning text amendment) — Run 1 + New in Run 2: Districts (2021 second-reading material, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “allow for medical cannabis dispensaries as permitted special uses in the C-1, Community Commercial District; C-2 Local Commercial District; and C-3 Highway Commercial District” and “allow for medical cannabis cultivation facilities, medical cannabis testing facilities, and medical cannabis manufacturing facilities in the I-1 Light Industrial District and I-2 Heavy Industrial District.” Plan Commission 19 Aug 2021: 5–1 to recommend Ord. 21-34 with an amendment to strike §25.07 (limitation on number of dispensaries) and 6–0 to approve Ord. 21-36 as presented. Setbacks (2021 proposal chart, Run 1): 1,000 ft schools; 500 ft daycares; 300 ft public park, pool, recreation facility, library; 300 ft religious institutions; 500 ft residential district. Enacted text Verify. New in Run 2 — fencing, Ord. 24-04 (first reading, 6 May 2024, same URL as §8.3.3): prior rule required “the perimeter of the property shall be fenced with a minimum of a six (6) foot high, solid fence and security access gates, in accordance with Chapter 21.61, for these medical cannabis uses” and “Medical cannabis uses are only allowed in the I-1 and I-2 Districts. I-1 and I-2 have lot size requirements of a minimum of 30,000 square feet (SF) and 220,000 SF, which would require a minimum of 800 linear feet (LF) of fencing, even if only a portion of the property is used for medical cannabis use.” The amendment “would eliminate the need to fence the entire property and would follow State guidelines and policies as required” (citing ARSD 44:90:05:06), with the amended rule retaining a 6-ft solid fence + gates only for “Any cultivation facility cultivating, processing, or storing cannabis outdoors or in greenhouses or other structures that do not meet all security requirements for buildings.” First-reading text — Verify enacted Ord. 24-04 and current Title 25 fencing. The memo also confirms, as staff research, that Beadle County, Brandon, Lincoln County, Rapid City and Vermillion do not require perimeter fences (security as required by state statutes) — secondary staff research, Verify in each jurisdiction’s code. Fees (Run 1): $5,000 ($2,500 refundable) proposed 2021; Res. 24-27 (2025, garbled OCR) appears to set $25,000 application (if available) + $5,000 renewal — Verify clean text + any 2026 successor. 8.3.8 Pierre (city) + Hughes County — and Fort Pierre (separate city, warning)

Pierre — Ords. 1836 (zoning) + 1837 (licensing), 31 Aug 2021 — Run 1 + New in Run 2 confirmation: Districts (Cannabis Law Report summary + Pierre commission minutes, 31 Aug 2021, search-result excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “all four types of cannabis facilities — cultivation, dispensary, manufacturing and testing — can set up shop in the city industrial park, light industrial or heavy industrial districts, while dispensaries and testing facilities can set up in the central and local business districts.” Minutes: “cultivation and manufacturing establishments in the City’s Industrial zoning districts” + “cannabis dispensaries and testing establishments in the central and local business zoning districts” + “The Ordinance also reflects the State law limitation of 1,000 feet from any private or public school.” Deliberate choice — no extra setbacks (Planning Commission draft minutes, 26 July 2021, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “The only distance requirement in the ordinance is for 1,000 feet from public or private schools as required by state law. Pruess passed out a copy of the zoning map showing the schools with 1,000 foot circles around them. She noted that the distance requirement for schools eliminates some large areas of the community. So the City working group was recommending not to impose any additional requirements for distance.” Manufacturing was removed from Local Business (truck route vs Sioux Ave./Euclid Ave. concern), leaving testing + dispensary as permitted uses in Local Business. Caps at adoption: 3 dispensaries, 1 each cultivation/manufacturing/testing (Run 1). Fee at adoption: $5,000 application, $2,500 reimbursed if state certificate fails (Run 1). Hours at adoption: 8 a.m.–9 p.m. daily; licence active ≥90 days in preceding 12 months (minutes summary, Run 1). Current caps/fees/hours/districts Verify (Run 1) — all figures above are adoption figures (2021). ETJ (Pierre + Hughes County joint area, up to 3 miles): NO establishment of any type (joint determination, Run 1). Hughes County standalone ordinance outside ETJ: Not found — Verify.

City of Pierre establishment requirements page (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: City of Pierre — Medical Cannabis Establishments): dispensary access control (identified cardholder/authorised person only; verification at external window or entryway window with locked interior door; visitors badged + directly supervised in cannabis areas); lighting sufficient for observance/cameras within 10 ft of perimeter entry; commercial-grade locks on perimeter doors; lockable windows in good condition; cameras at each exterior door/gate and each door separating non-public from retail areas, sufficient to view in entirety any area where cannabis/plants/products/waste are cultivated, manufactured, stored, destroyed or prepared; camera standards per ARSD 44:90:04:08, recording 24 hours/day (except exterior patient doors), backup power ≥2 hours, footage ≥90 days; alarm per ARSD 44:90:04:10 (cited in excerpt as “ARSD 90:44:04:10” — transposed citation in excerpt, Verify); agent badges displayed on premises; inventory/record-keeping, daily reporting; security/safety protocols, operation manuals, training. This is Pierre’s city summary of state + local requirements — Verify against current ARSD 44:90:04 and Pierre Ch. 10 Art. 16 before relying on the 90-day figure (see §8.4).

Fort Pierre — SEPARATE city, Stanley County — §160.03, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: Fort Pierre §160.03 — DO NOT APPLY TO PIERRE:

“(2) The city shall allow up to three cannabis cultivating facilities, provided the time, place and manner of said facilities complies with this subchapter. A cannabis cultivation facility must exist in conjunction with a cannabis dispensary. (3) The city shall allow up to three cannabis dispensaries, provided the time, place and manner of said dispensaries complies with this chapter. … The city shall allow up to zero cannabis testing facilities.”
“(B) Required separation distances. (1) A cannabis establishment shall be located not less than 1,000 feet from a public or private school existing before the date of the cannabis establishment application. (2) A cannabis establishment shall be located not less than 500 feet from a residence, church, public park, public swimming pool or day care facility existing before the date of the cannabis establishment application. (3) Any separation distance requirement, other than the state requirement from schools (1,000 feet), may be waived, provided: the applicant provides documentation waiving the setback requirement from the title holder of the land benefiting from the separation. (4) Prescribed separation/setback distances … are to be measured from the lot line of the property where the dispensary is proposed.”
“(C) … Permanent or temporary establishments are prohibited in all other zoning districts and not eligible for a home occupation use.”
“(D) … All cannabis establishments must be located within the municipal boundaries of the city.”
“(E) … No cannabis establishment shall share premises with or permit access directly from another medical cannabis establishment unless said establishment is licensed by the same entity.”
“Cannabis establishments are allowed to be open to the public between the hours of 7:00 a.m., Mountain Standard Time, and 7:00 p.m., Mountain Standard Time, on Monday through Sunday.”

Fort Pierre licence fee (§113.06, Run 1): $5,000 application, $2,500 reimbursed if state certificate fails. Fort Pierre zoning districts (beyond “all other districts prohibited”) were not in the excerpt — Verify in Fort Pierre’s full §160.03 / zoning code.

Lawrence County — Land Code §II-10.013 — Run 1 + New in Run 2 full setbacks (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: Lawrence County §II-10.013): Cap: “The county shall allow one cannabis dispensary provided the time, place, and manner of said dispensary comply with this chapter.” Separation distances (all 1,000 ft, straight line from exterior walls of the establishment building/portion to the property line of the protected use): (1) single-family dwelling; (2) public or private school; (3) church; (4) public service facility; (5) park; (6) another medical cannabis establishment (per the fuller excerpt in the Pierre search results, same URL — the Lawrence excerpt in the Rapid City search truncates after (3), but the Pierre-search excerpt gives (4)–(6) verbatim in substance). Waiver: (7) “Any separation distance requirement, other than the state requirement from schools (1,000 feet), may be waived, provided: The applicant provides documentation waiving the setback requirement from the title holder of the land benefiting from the separation.” (8) “Prescribed separation/setback distances from certain existing uses are to be measured from the lot line of the property where the dispensary is proposed.” (B)(1)–(6) vs (B)(8) measurement tension — see §8.1. Verify which origin the zoning official applies. Other locational requirements: (C)(1) permanent/temporary dispensaries prohibited in all other zoning districts, not eligible for home occupation; unlawful to operate in a building containing a residence or a mixed-use building with commercial/residential uses. (E) Hours: 8:00 a.m.–10:00 p.m., 7 days/week. (F) Documentation of state licensure required before acquiring/possessing/storing/delivering/transferring/transporting/supplying/dispensing. (G) Zoning official may issue building/use permits subject to site plan + documentation of setback compliance + state licensure. (H) Construction requirements (truncated in excerpt — Verify). 8.4 Commercial / industrial lease considerations and site requirements (medical cannabis establishments)

Landlord consent is a state-application item, not a courtesy. DOH checklists (search-result excerpts, accessed 30 Oct 2026: DOH 2025 manufacturing and cultivation checklists) require: “Proof of Property Owner Consent • Deed to property OR • [letter] from landlord specifically referencing type of cannabis activity.” A generic commercial lease that does not name the cannabis activity, or a landlord letter that says only “commercial use,” does not satisfy that checklist item as written. Verify the current checklist (2025 versions retrieved; 2026 package may have revised them).

Lease clauses a buyer/tenant must address before signing (checklist, not legal advice):

  1. Contingency on local slot + state certificate + zoning verification. The lease should be contingent (with a long-stop date and a deposit/termination regime) on: (a) written zoning verification for the parcel and type, (b) local licence/permit issuance (or a transfer approval where the licence is transferable — see Vermillion non-transferability warning in §8.2), and (c) DOH registration certificate issuance. A lease that starts rent before those three are in hand transfers licensing risk to the tenant.
  2. Use clause must name the exact establishment type(s). Cultivation, manufacturing, testing and dispensary are different state certificates and different local licences. A lease for “retail” does not authorise cultivation; a lease for “warehouse” does not authorise manufacturing with extraction. The landlord letter must “specifically reference [the] type of cannabis activity” (DOH checklist).
  3. No on-premises consumption, no sharing, no co-location traps. SDCL 34-20G-68 prohibits consumption on establishment property; local rules prohibit sharing premises / direct access from another establishment or an alcohol/tobacco business (Lawrence County, Sioux Falls) and, in Brookings, operating in a dwelling/paediatrician/hotel building. A multi-tenant building with shared access, shared HVAC that carries odour, or a neighbouring alcohol/tobacco tenant with a connecting door can fail a local rule even if the lease is silent.
  4. Security — state + local, and the retention conflict. State framework: ARSD art. 44:90 ch. 04 (facilities) — cameras at each exterior door/gate and each door separating non-public from retail areas, sufficient to view in entirety any area where cannabis/plants/products/waste are cultivated, manufactured, stored, destroyed or prepared; commercial-grade locks on perimeter doors; lockable windows; alarm system (ARSD 44:90:04:10); agent badges displayed; inventory/record-keeping with daily reporting (Pierre city summary, §8.3.8, and DOH cultivation checklist — camera placement per ARSD 44:90:04:07, alarm audible warning + remote disable + power-failure notification + ≥4-hour battery backup per ARSD 44:90:04:10 in the checklist excerpt). Retention: Pierre summary says footage ≥90 days; Minnehaha County ordinance says recordings retained 180 days (§7.1.1, conflict 7). Budget for the longer local figure where it applies, and Verify current ARSD 44:90:04:08. Lighting: sufficient for observance/cameras within 10 ft of perimeter entry (Pierre summary). Well-lit external parking lot, locked fully enclosed facility, internal access tracking (Minnehaha County ordinance, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: Minnehaha County MC 60-21 — “All medical cannabis establishments shall have the following security system at a minimum: (1) Electronic security recording system, internal and external, which retains recordings for 180 days. (2) Well lit external parking lot. (3) Locked, fully enclosed, facility. (4) Internal security to track access to building and all medical cannabis. (5) Any additional security necessary to comply with SDCL Chapter 34-20G, or ARSD Title 44, Article 90.”).
  5. Odour — the most common neighbour complaint and the most jurisdiction-variable rule. Joint Sioux Falls area: no dust/fumes/vapours/odours seen, smelled or otherwise perceived beyond the lot line. Mitchell (reported): no gas/vapours/odours/smoke/dust/heat/glare noticeable at or beyond the property line. Minnehaha County: written odour control plan required. Huron hearing example (Dakota Herb security/odour plan, 16 Feb 2022 hearing examiner, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026): “three layers of filtration,” airtight child-resistant packaging, “You will not be able to smell product inside the store,” 24/7 video inside/outside, secure vestibule + guard + buzz-in second door, windows filled in for no outside visibility. A cultivation/manufacturing lease must therefore address HVAC, carbon filtration, negative pressure, and who pays for odour mitigation if a neighbour complains — and must not promise “no odour” in absolute terms where the ordinance test is perception beyond the lot line.
  6. Waste and disposal. Joint area: approved disposal plan from the Planning Director; remnants/by-products per approved plan, not in an exterior refuse container. State: inventory/record-keeping and waste areas covered by cameras (Pierre summary). A lease must allocate waste-storage area, locked waste handling, and disposal-plan compliance — and must not assume municipal trash service accepts cannabis waste.
  7. Environmental / building systems. DOH cultivation checklist requires, among other items: proof of thorough training + OSHA-approved certification before using heavy agricultural/industrial/extraction/packaging equipment and forklifts; procedures to prevent diversion/theft including securing product when unoccupied (ARSD 44:90:03:05(8)); vehicle list for transport (make, model, plate) + secure, opaque, locking compartment (ARSD 44:90:04:18); and, for an on-site wastewater/septic system, a signed statement that the system complies with ARSD ch. 74:53:01 (ARSD 44:90:03:05(10)(b)). A cultivation/manufacturing tenant must therefore verify: electrical capacity (grow lighting, HVAC, extraction), water/wastewater capacity and discharge permits, fire suppression for extraction solvents, and whether the building’s existing septic/water system can lawfully serve the use — before signing, not after build-out.
  8. Insurance, banking and federal overlay (for the lease negotiation, not a zoning rule). No federal cannabis banking law exists (fixed baseline); a landlord’s lender or insurer may prohibit cannabis use in loan/insurance documents even if zoning allows it. After federal Schedule III for state-licensed medical activity, a landlord/lender may still treat adult-use or tribal adult-use activity differently — Verify with lender/insurer and counsel for the specific tenancy; Schedule III does not by itself override a private loan/insurance prohibition. South Dakota secondary summaries state dispensaries are not state-mandated to carry insurance (SouthDakotaStateCannabis.org, accessed 30 Oct 2026 — secondary, Verify); that, if true, makes landlord-required insurance a lease negotiation point, not a state compliance point.
  9. Rent / price data — see §8.5. No verifiable SD cannabis-property rent series was found. The only SD-specific lease figure retrieved is a secondary, outdated range ($40,000–$100,000/year for a dispensing space, SouthDakotaStateCannabis.org — secondary, undated methodology, and the same page states the outdated 4.5% sales-tax rate, so its figures must not be relied on without independent verification). Do not budget rent from that range.

8.5 Property listings and marketplaces — what was actually found (including zero results)

Method: searched 420Property (SD statewide + Box Elder, Belle Fourche, Tea, Spearfish, Yankton pages), LoopNet (SD storefront for sale), and Crexi (cannabis dispensary listings) via search results, accessed 30 Oct 2026. No live-browser marketplace search was performed in this run (generic subagents cannot operate a live browser); the findings below are search-index results, not a live filtered search, and must be re-checked live before any buyer relies on them. No listing below is presented as verified as currently available, licensed, or zoned — every marketplace listing is self-reported by the seller/broker.

8.5.1 420Property — ZERO qualifying South Dakota listings found

Statewide page: 420Property — South Dakota — “South Dakota cannabis businesses, dispensaries, and properties for sale and lease on 420 Property. South Dakota runs a medical cannabis program… Listings mix licensed medical businesses, hemp operations, and agricultural land.” Result block on the same page: “We couldn’t find a listing for your search criteria.” (Search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026.) City pages, same result: Box Elder, SD; Belle Fourche, SD (business + real estate); Tea, SD; Spearfish, SD (business + real estate); Yankton, SD — all “We couldn’t find a listing for your search criteria.” Interpretation: the “couldn’t find” blocks are followed on each page by out-of-state listings (e.g. Brooklyn dispensary, Los Angeles manufacturing space, New York edibles facility) — those are not South Dakota listings and must not be counted. Finding for Part 8: ZERO qualifying South Dakota cannabis business/property listings were found on 420Property in the search results retrieved. That is a finding about 420Property’s indexed SD inventory at crawl date, not proof that no SD cannabis property is for sale anywhere. Verify live on 420Property before republishing, as listings change and search-index crawls lag.

8.5.2 LoopNet — no cannabis-identified South Dakota listing found

SD storefront for sale page: LoopNet — SD storefront for sale — “Search through 13 Storefront listings currently available for sale in the state of South Dakota” (search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026, crawl 139 days ago). None of the 13 listings in the retrieved excerpt is identified as a cannabis dispensary, cultivation or manufacturing property, and 13 listings are described as having “unknown addresses” and not visible on the map. LoopNet does not have a cannabis-use filter in the retrieved excerpt. Finding: ZERO cannabis-identified SD listings were found on LoopNet in the results retrieved. A general commercial search on LoopNet (retail, industrial, warehouse in the target city) is still the correct method for finding a zoned building — but the searcher must then apply §8.2 Steps 1–3 to each general listing; LoopNet will not do the cannabis-zoning check.

8.5.3 Crexi — no South Dakota cannabis listing found in results retrieved

Crexi cannabis-dispensary results retrieved in this run were all out-of-state: Riverside, CA ($1,500,000, seller open to offers with $900k down); Webberville, MI (High Profile dispensary, single-tenant net-leased, lease through Sept 2031, 2.25% escalations); Colorado Springs, CO ($1,700,000, 4,500 sq ft, $3,700/month + NNN); Pensacola, FL (Sunnyside dispensary, long-term lease, 10% increases every 5 years). Finding: ZERO South Dakota cannabis listings were found on Crexi in the results retrieved. The out-of-state prices/rents above are not South Dakota comparables and must not be used as SD price evidence.

8.5.4 The one verifiable South Dakota cannabis business + property sale found — with limits

Five Leaf Clover, Colome, SD (Tripp County) — $1,695,000 asking price. Source: MMJDaily article, search-result excerpt, accessed 30 Oct 2026: MMJDaily — Five Leaf Clover listed for $1,695,000 — “Dustin Swatzell is listing Five Leaf Clover, a smoke shop, medical cannabis dispensary and seasonal fireworks operation in Colome, South Dakota, for $1,695,000, with the sale driven by a desire to spend more time with his young daughter in Sioux Falls rather than by any financial difficulty. He listed the business in early September and says he is in no rush to find a buyer. The Colome property brings together three ventures: Five Leaf Clover, which sells smoke shop and alternative products; Five Leaf Clover 420, a state-licensed medical cannabis dispensary; and Boom Boom Fireworks, a seasonal business on the same site. Swatzell owns the building outright, having purchased it in cash during his second year of operation… The shop sits near the Nebraska state line and draws customers from as far as 200 miles round trip, including from Valentine, Ainsworth and Alliance. … He says the asking price reflects current financial performance, including record revenue in the past year, not the possibility of future recreational legalization.”

Why this is NOT a clean “cannabis-zoned property price”: (a) it is a business + building + three ventures sale, not a bare property sale; the $1,695,000 cannot be decomposed into land/building vs dispensary licence value vs smoke-shop/fireworks value from the article; (b) Colome (population under 500, per the article) is not in the required Part 8 city list and its local licensing/zoning was not retrieved — do not assume Colome’s rules match any required city; (c) the article is a seller interview (secondary, ~Oct 2026 — search crawl “4 days ago” at research date), not a closed sale, appraisal or recorded deed; (d) whether the dispensary licence/certificate transfers with the sale, and whether a buyer must re-apply locally and to DOH, is Verify — see transfer rules in §8.2 Step 2 (Vermillion non-transferability is the warning example; Colome’s transfer rule was not retrieved). Use: as the only SD-specific cannabis business/property asking price found in this run, labelled as a single asking price for a mixed business in Colome, early Sept 2026 listing, seller-stated, not a closed price and not a market average.

Do NOT invent listings. In particular, do not convert a DOH establishment address (e.g. a dispensary address in a DOH inspection report) into a “property for sale,” do not convert a directory listing (Weedmaps, Genesis Farms location list) into a licence or a sale listing, and do not present a general commercial listing as “cannabis-zoned” until §8.2 Steps 1–3 have been applied to that parcel and confirmed in writing by the zoning official.

8.6 What a buyer / tenant must verify before signing — consolidated checklist

1Jurisdiction: city, county, joint area or tribal land for the exact parcel (address + legal description + parcel ID)City/county planning office; GIS (§8.2 Step 5); tribal government if on tribal landWrong rulebook applied — entire zoning analysis is invalid

12Building systems: electrical, water/wastewater/septic (ARSD 74:53:01 statement), fire suppression for extraction, OSHA equipment training/certificationDOH checklists; building/fire codesUse cannot be operated safely/lawfully in that building
13Lender / insurer / lease prohibitions: loan and insurance documents may prohibit cannabis use even if zoning allows itLender, insurer, lease — private documents, not zoning lawLease/lender default despite lawful zoning
14Hemp parcels (if the property is for hemp, not medical cannabis): USDA/tribal producer licence (post-2027), lot/field GPS + FSA report, sampling window, transport papers, processor question (§7.6)USDA HeMP / tribal plan; SD §§38-35-17/17.1Hemp grown/processed/transported without the right licence/papers is unlawful even on correctly zoned farmland
15Title, floodplain, environmental: parcel title, floodplain (GIS flood layers), prior uses, environmental conditionsCounty GIS / assessor; title search; Phase I if warrantedUnrelated to cannabis but determinative for any commercial purchase — do not skip because the cannabis checks passed

Parts 7–8 consolidated Verify/Unavailable list (54 items, Parts 7 items 1–26 and Part 8 items 27–54) is reproduced in full in the Combined Research Notes at the end of this file, merged with the Run 1 gaps and the Parts 9–12 lists.

Part 9 — Regulators and Full History

How to read this part. Run 1 (Parts 0–6) governs where it conflicts with a secondary summary, unless this part explicitly says a Run 1 gap has been filled and gives the new evidence. “Verify” means the fact was not confirmed by opening the primary source directly in this pass, or sources conflict; “Unavailable” means no source was found. Court and Attorney General opinions are labelled as opinions, not as statute. Proposed, failed, deferred or struck measures are labelled DEAD / FAILED / STRUCK / PROPOSED and are never presented as current law. No personal phone numbers or emails are reproduced. Research / access date: 30 October 2026. The 3 November 2026 election had NOT been held on 30 October 2026. No 2026 election result is stated anywhere in this report.

9.1.2 Fines — the 2026 rules package: timing-critical

DOH filed 38 proposed amendments to ARSD Art. 44:90 (hearing 23 September 2026, comments due 3 October, Interim Rules Review Committee 14 October 2026). Secondary reporting (WeedPress, 27 October 2026; Marijuana Herald, October 2026 — both accessed 30 October 2026) states: 35 sections amended, filed with the Secretary of State 16 October 2026, effective 5 November 2026 (20 days after filing, not counting the filing day); Interim Rules Review Committee voted 6–0 on 14 October 2026, on motion by Sen. Jim Mehlhaff, seconded by Rep. Jon Hansen, that review was complete; hearing notice 53 SDR 25, 31 August 2026; Secretary Magstadt Form 13 certificate.

At the 30 October 2026 research date these rules were filed but NOT yet effective. Do not present the new penalty framework as in force on 30 October 2026. Reported new framework: Category 4 (operating-procedure failures) and Category 5 (employee badges, visitors, scales) first offences = corrective action plan for approval rather than immediate fine; Category 4 second/subsequent $1,000; Category 5 second/subsequent $500; Category 1 $5,000 first to $10,000 third/subsequent; Category 2 $3,000–$5,000; Category 3 $1,500–$2,500 (unchanged for serious violations). WeedPress also notes the adopted-rules document was not downloadable from the rules portal as of 27 October 2026 and a numbering discrepancy (Register/certificate list “44:90:09:07” vs drafts amending §44:90:09:07.01) plus two §12:03 penalty sentences missing from the 7 October version — Verify against the filed text. Concrete fine example (secondary, August 2026): Bad River Cannabis, Fort Pierre, owner Rob/Robert Krogstad, fined $3,000 for two “clerical errors” corrected before inspectors left; he proposed a three-strikes approach for minor clerical errors. Happy Flower Healing, Yankton, also presented concerns about inspection fines to the 2025 Oversight Committee (2025 Final Report, above).

9.1.3 Appeals

State system: establishment certificate decisions and discipline are subject to the administrative-appeal framework in ch. 34-20G and ch. 1-26 (SD Administrative Procedures Act) — detailed appeal route and deadlines Verify against §§34-20G-80/81 and ARSD 44:90. The Puffy's litigation (§9.4) turned on exactly this point: the Supreme Court held the circuit court had jurisdiction because the Department had taken no final action triggering a ch. 1-26 appeal. Tribal systems have their own appeals (SWO: Commission administrative appeal within 10 days, then Tribal Court appeal within 20 days of final action — Part 10).

9.2 Attorney General Official Opinion 26-06 (6 October 2026) — OPINION, not statute

Audit upgrade: the opinion text itself was opened in this audit via the Attorney General's opinion page (Opinion 26-06, dated 6 October 2026, requested by Sen. John Carley, Vice Chair of the Medical Marijuana Oversight Committee). The quotations below are therefore Confirmed against the opinion, not merely against its press release. The opinion does not resolve tribal “state-licensed” status — that remains Verify (see §10.6). Source: AG Official Opinion 26-06.

  • AG press release, 13 October 2026: AG press release 13 Oct 2026 (accessed 30 October 2026). Opinion page (cited in the press release): Opinion 26-06 page (accessed 30 October 2026).
  • Requested by State Sen. John Carley, vice chair of the Medical Marijuana Oversight Committee (joined, per secondary summary, by Chair Garcia, Sen. Lauren Nelson and Rep. Bobbi Andera).
  • AG Jackley, verbatim from the press release:
“Because federal law explicitly permits state medical-marijuana programs, South Dakota's program may continue to operate as it has without impact from marijuana's federal rescheduling to a Schedule III controlled substance. How the program functions in the future will be a policy determination at the discretion of the Legislature or the voters of South Dakota.”

Further holdings (opinion text, confirmed in this audit): federal rescheduling will “likely have a negligible impact” on SD's programme; under the DEA's April 2026 rule, “an existing manufacturer, distributor, or dispensary operating under a valid state license may now do so in compliance with federal law if they register with the DEA”; federal rescheduling does not preempt SD's programme because Congress has not expressly preempted state action, has not totally occupied the field, and SD law does not conflict with federal scheduling; SD does not currently schedule marijuana as a controlled substance under SDCL ch. 34-20B, though possession/distribution outside the medical programme remain criminalised; if SD chose to reschedule marijuana to Schedule III under state law, “the general rules regarding Schedule III substances would apply to marijuana as with any other Schedule III substance” — but that action “would have to be taken by either the Legislature or the voters, independent from the rescheduling of marijuana federally”; placement in Schedule III does not make marijuana an FDA-approved drug (FDA has approved one cannabis-derived product, Epidiolex, and three synthetic cannabis-related products: Marinol, Syndros and Cesamet). Secondary summaries: Marijuana Moment and Cannabis Business Times, October 2026 (both accessed 30 October 2026).

What Opinion 26-06 does NOT resolve (Verify): whether tribal medical programmes count as “state-licensed” under the federal Schedule III rule (Part 10, §10.5). The opinion as summarised addresses state medical-marijuana programmes and state-licensed businesses; no tribal analysis was retrieved. Do not cite Opinion 26-06 as authority for tribal Schedule III status.

Advertising warning — unchanged by this opinion on the evidence available: ARSD 44:90:10:14.01 prohibits billboards (except the dispensary's own premises), handbills, unverified direct mail/phone/text/email, publications, radio/TV/media and practitioner-office placement, all prefaced “Unless and until the DEA removes marijuana or cannabis as a Schedule I controlled substance.” Whether DOH has revised this prohibition after 28 April 2026 Schedule III is Verify — do not state advertising is now free. The same applies to the ARSD 44:90:10:12.01 warnings (including “Cannabis has a high potential for abuse… not FDA-approved”) — whether DOH revised them after Schedule III is Verify.

9.4 Courts and litigation

Thom, Miller v. Barnett and In re Election Contest as to Amendment A, 2021 S.D. 65 (24 November 2021, 4–1)

  • UJS press release (official summary): UJS press release — Thom decision (accessed 30 October 2026). UJS opinion PDF (search-result text): UJS opinion PDF (accessed 30 October 2026) — the opinion text below is from the UJS press release and the UJS opinion as returned in search, not from a direct PDF open in this pass; Verify against the opinion PDF itself for pinpoint cites.
  • Parties: Sheriff Kevin Thom (Pennington County) and Col. Rick Miller (Superintendent, SD Highway Patrol), plaintiffs/appellees; Steve Barnett, Secretary of State, defendant; South Dakotans for Better Marijuana Laws, Randolph Seiler, William Stocker, Charles Parkinson and Melissa Mentele, defendants/appellants. Dockets #29546 (declaratory judgment) and #29547 (election contest). Argued 28 April 2021; reassigned 7 September 2021; opinion filed 24 November 2021. Appeals from the Sixth Judicial Circuit, Hughes County (Hon. Christina L. Klinger).
  • UJS press release, verbatim in substance:
“In a four-to-one decision, the Court held that Amendment A, as submitted to the voters in the November 2020 general election, violated the single subject requirement in the South Dakota Constitution. As a result of the constitutional violation, the Court has declared the amendment invalid.”
“The Court determined that the provisions of Amendment A embraced three separate and distinct subjects: 1. Recreational Marijuana—by creating a comprehensive plan to constitutionally legalize, regulate, and tax marijuana for all persons at least twenty-one years of age; 2. Hemp—by constitutionally mandating that the Legislature pass laws regarding hemp; and 3. Medical Marijuana—by constitutionally mandating that the Legislature pass laws ensuring access to marijuana for limited medical use for qualifying persons.”
“The drafters' failure to comply with the single subject requirement in the South Dakota Constitution Article XXIII, § 1 meant that voters were unable to separately vote on each distinct subject embraced in Amendment A.”

Further holdings (press release): one member issued a special writing joining the majority on the single-subject violation but expressing the view that such a violation is not necessarily fatal in all instances, adding that here there was no argument or proof SD voters would have adopted Amendment A without the medical-marijuana and hemp provisions. A separate opinion, concurring in part and dissenting in part, focused on the long history of the initiative in SD, deference to initiated constitutional amendments, and the belief Amendment A appropriately contains parts of a comprehensive plan addressing related issues. The Court unanimously held: (1) the circuit court properly dismissed the election contest; (2) neither Thom nor Miller had standing, but Governor Noem's written ratification of Miller's declaratory judgment action allowed the action to proceed as if commenced by the Governor.

Circuit history (from the opinion text as returned in search): the circuit court identified Amendment A's subject as “the legalization of marijuana” and its “single scheme” as “being able to possess and use marijuana… at any point from its growth through consumption,” but held Amendment A contained an additional, distinct subject — hemp — and provisions unrelated to legalising marijuana, violating Article XXIII, § 1; it also held Amendment A was a revision requiring a constitutional convention under Article XXIII, § 2. The Supreme Court affirmed the Article XXIII, § 1 holding; the revision holding is not relied on in this report without pinpoint verification — Verify whether the majority reached § 2.

Consequence (settled): Amendment A was declared invalid and never took effect. IM 26 (statute) was unaffected, effective 1 July 2021. Do not present Amendment A's 15% excise, licence types or tax structure as law.

Puffy's, LLC v. State, 2025 S.D. 10 (opinion filed 19 February 2025)

  • Direct open of the UJS opinion PDF failed in this pass with a transient error (UJS opinion PDF) and, per task instructions, was not retried. The holdings below are from search-result summaries and are flagged Verify against the opinion itself. Justia summary: Justia — Puffy's (accessed 30 October 2026). Justice Patricia DeVaney; argued 1 October 2024; appeal from the Seventh Judicial Circuit, Pennington County (judge's surname spelled Hendrickson/Henderson in different sources — Verify).
  • Facts as summarised: Rapid City ordinance limited dispensaries to 15 in 2022; 47 applicants, all tied on ARSD 44:90:03:15 scoring; lottery drawing 9 March 2022 awarded 15 certificates, remainder waitlisted in lottery order; Puffy's was first on the waitlist (for 3308 Campbell St., Rapid City) and had also received several lottery certificates, of which it could not make several operational within one year under ARSD 44:90:03:16; after extensions were denied, Puffy's sought mandamus to compel issuance of a certificate that became void (Greenlight Dispensary's certificate, which did not become operational within one year). The circuit court granted mandamus; the Supreme Court affirmed.
  • Holdings as summarised: circuit court had jurisdiction because Puffy's had no administrative remedy to exhaust (Department had taken no final action triggering ch. 1-26 appeal); matter not moot; Department had a clear duty under ARSD 44:90:03:16's plain language (“certificate is deemed void and must be awarded to the next applicant on the waiting list”) and the rule requires no additional application or fee from waitlisted applicants; waitlist applicants fit neither “initial” nor “renewal” applicant categories, and “it is not [the Court's] role to address a gap in the Department's rules.” Fee history in footnote 1 (Run 1): $5,000 (2021) → $5,310 (November 2023) → $9,000 (2024).
  • Follow-on Rapid City litigation (secondary only — outcome Verify; do not state a decided result): Puffy's sued Rapid City (2025) alleging arbitrary prevention of expansion while on a DOH one-year timer; 7 state licences, 2 locations operational, 5 in legal limbo; city moratorium (Resolution 2024-079, 7 October 2024) blocked redistribution/reallocation; city council repealed portions in August 2025 preventing transfer of ownership/location but the moratorium remained pending a Supreme Court ruling on the five licences; October 2025 local-licence renewals refused by the Rapid City Finance Office, with the Deputy City Attorney arguing under oath Puffy's had no local licences. Source: Rapid City Post (accessed 30 October 2026). Note the tension: the Supreme Court case is against the State/DOH (2025 S.D. 10, decided February 2025); the Rapid City suit is a separate, later, local dispute — do not merge them. This is a state–local dispute, not a tribal dispute.

Hemp Quarters 605 LLC v. Noem, D.S.D. No. 3:24-cv-03016 (HB 1125 challenge)

  • New in this pass: the preliminary-injunction stage is now documented; the final merits disposition remains Verify. Plaintiff (Pierre retail business; owner Brandi Barth) sued Governor Noem, AG Jackley and Hughes County State's Attorney Casey Deibert, alleging HB 1125 impermissibly violates the 2018 Federal Farm Bill, violates the Commerce Clause, constitutes a regulatory taking, and is void for vagueness. Hearing 27 June 2024 (live testimony from Barth and expert Mark Krause).
  • Opinion and Order Denying Motion for Preliminary Injunction, signed by U.S. District Judge Eric C. Schulte, 29 June 2024 (Justia, secondary mirror — Verify against the docket): Justia — Hemp Quarters PI order (accessed 30 October 2026). The court held the plaintiff failed to demonstrate likelihood of success on the merits (2018 Farm Bill allows states to impose stricter hemp regulations; standing questionable on the chemical-modification provisions because the plaintiff is solely retail and does not chemically modify) and insufficient evidence of irreparable harm, and that the balance of harms favoured the defendants. HB 1125 therefore took effect 1 July 2024. A preliminary-injunction denial does not settle the lawsuit or guarantee a win for the State. Final disposition: Verify — do not state HB 1125 was invalidated or definitively upheld on the merits. AG Opinion 26-06 summaries also cite Hempquarters 605 v. Noem for the proposition that police powers are traditionally reserved to the states — attribute that citation to the AG opinion summaries, Verify in the opinion text.

Part 10 — Tribal and Interstate

10.1 Jurisdictional framework — read before the tribe-by-tribe table

  • Tribes are sovereign governments. Tribal cannabis law governs on tribal/reservation and trust lands; state law governs off-reservation; federal law overlays both. Purchase or use lawful on tribal land under tribal law does NOT make possession or use lawful off tribal land. Off-reservation, SD state law (for SD patients/non-patients) or the neighbouring state's law applies, plus federal transport risk.
  • South Dakota is a Public Law 280 state (Reason, 17 June 2015, summarising KELO: SD is “one of 15 states that Public Law 280, enacted by Congress in 1953, let assume jurisdiction over crimes committed on tribal lands within their borders” — accessed 30 October 2026). Verify the precise scope of PL 280 jurisdiction in SD (mandatory vs optional PL 280, and its interaction with tribal cannabis ordinances) before stating enforcement consequences; do not over-claim from a 2015 news summary.
  • Federal tribal-cannabis framework history: Cole Memorandum, 29 August 2013 (8 enforcement priorities, including preventing distribution to minors; preventing diversion out of state; preventing violence/firearms; preventing drugged driving — as summarised in Flandreau AG Seth Pearman's Federal Bar presentation, accessed 30 October 2026); December 2014 DOJ clarification that state non-interference policies would also apply to the 326 recognised reservations (secondary summary — Verify original memo). The Cole Memo was rescinded in January 2018 — Verify successor guidance. No current federal tribal-cannabis enforcement policy document was retrieved in this pass — flag Verify.
  • No state–tribal cannabis compact was found for any SD tribe in this pass. Do not assert a compact exists. The SWO Code expressly contemplates “any compacts or agreements between the Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate and any other governmental entity” as implementation instruments (§81-03-01, search excerpt) — that is authorisation language, not evidence a compact was signed. Compact status is Unavailable/Verify for each tribe.
  • Tribal establishments are NOT DOH-registered and must never be merged into DOH counts without labelling the two systems separately (Run 1).
