10.3 Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate — verbatim Regulations (official tribal source, opened directly)

Source for every quotation in this section: SWO Medical Cannabis Regulations, adopted 31 March 2022, revised 7 June 2024: SWO Medical Cannabis Regulations (accessed 30 October 2026).

  • §1.1 Title: “These regulations shall be cited as the Medical Cannabis Regulations of the Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate of the Lake Traverse Reservation ('Oyate').”
  • §1.2 Purpose (in full, as printed): the Regulations civilly regulate persons and lands within the Reservation to protect health, safety and welfare re Medicinal Cannabis; ensure seriously ill people on the Reservation have the right to obtain and use Medicinal Cannabis where recommended by a Practitioner in a bona fide practitioner-patient relationship, OR where the person is a medical cannabis Cardholder whose card was issued by a State or tribal authority, OR where the person holds a Medical cannabis card “issued by a website in accordance with the terms in this Code”; establish protocols for cultivation/manufacture, packaging, distribution, advertisement, and retail outlet location/operation to meet the needs of Tribal Members and/or Qualified Patients and Designated Caregivers on the Reservation. The “(iii) website” phrase is unexplained in the text read — flag Verify; do not interpret it as state recognition.
  • §1.3 Authority: “enacted under the inherent sovereign authority of the Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate, as the governing body of the Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate, and pursuant to Article VII, §1(c), (d), and (h) of the Revised Constitution and Bylaws of the Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate.”
  • §1.4 Territorial Applicability: applies to Reservation or trust lands and all buildings/improvements of a permanent nature on reservation/trust lands.
  • §1.5 Nonwaiver of Immunity; §1.6 Inclusion of Other Laws (inclusion of state/federal language is NOT adoption of that law and NOT deference to state/federal jurisdiction); §1.7 Requirement to Comply with Other Tribal Laws (must also comply with Ch. 53 SWO Business License Ordinance, Ch. 67 SWO Tax Code, and any other applicable tribal law).
  • §3.1 Enabling Legislation: “The Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate of the Lake Traverse Reservation enacted the Amended Medical Cannabis Code on December 22, 2021.” (Note: this date differs from the Code-adoption date in the amendments PDF summary, 2 November 2021, Motion No. 11 — Verify which date is the Code's adoption vs amendment; do not silently reconcile.)
  • §3.2 Commission composition (as printed, including the internal inconsistency — do not “correct” it silently): “The Commission shall consist of three (4) [sic] Sisseton-Wahpeton Oyate tribal members meeting the qualifications and two (2) exofficio Tribal Council Member.” Minimum qualifications: experience/knowledge in government affairs, agriculture, cannabis, business or law, or tribal government regulatory experience, and understanding of applicable cannabis laws; completed approved federal, state and tribal background check before voting; no felony conviction relating to a controlled substance under tribal, state or federal law. “The Commission will be independent of, and act independently and autonomously from, the Tribal Council in all matters within its purview.”
  • §3.3 Registry (verbatim in substance): “The Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate Medical Cannabis Program shall create a Medical Cannabis Registry and Issue Medical Cannabis Cards to individuals based upon the following, if they: A. Completed SWO Medical Cannabis Program Application; B. They are 18 years or older; C. Under 18 years of age shall provide parent or guardian approval form; D. Any current State issued Medical Cannabis Card; E. Any current Native American Tribal Government issued Medical Cannabis Card; F. Pay SWO MCP Application fee $50”.
  • §4.3(C) Tribal Ownership Requirement: “At least 51% of a licensed business must be owned by a Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate tribal member, or an authorized tribal entity, or a tribal district owned business entity. Sale or transfer of a license is prohibited.”
  • §4.5(A): “All production, possession, processing, packaging, and storing of Medicinal Cannabis must take place in a secure facility located on the Sisseton Wahpeton Reservation.”
  • §6.3 Preventing Medical Cannabis from Leaving the Reservation (verbatim): “All medical cannabis transported, transferred, or sold must be kept within the Sisseton Wahpeton Oyate Reservation unless such cannabis is being securely provided to a thirdparty testing facility. The Commission Compliance Officer must be able to verify at a moment's notice that every shipment has arrived at its scheduled destination.” And: “The Commission reserves the right to audit the records of a Facility to ensure that no medical cannabis from the Facility was diverted to another state”.
  • §6.6(A): “no individual may produce cannabis or cannabis products on the Reservation for individual use or otherwise regardless of applicable state law.”
  • §6.7(D): “The possession and use of Medical Cannabis shall be limited to private residences of registered patients.”
  • §5.7 Administrative Appeal: “Any recipient of a fine or suspension order or an individual that is denied or has a license revoked shall have ten (10) days from receipt of the written order to respond to the same in writing.” §5.8 Tribal Court Appeal: recipient has “twenty (20) days from the receipt of such notice of final action to seek a review of the same by the Tribal Court”; to overturn, the appellant “must prove by clear and convincing evidence that the Commission's action was arbitrary, capricious, or an abuse of discretion.” §5.9: limited waiver of sovereign immunity for Tribal Court suit only; no monetary damages or other recourse from overturning a Commission decision. §5.10: licensing fees and civil fines dedicated to Commission operations; goal of self-sufficiency, then a reserve equal to twice the annual budget.
  • Retail (Amendment #1, §§L–V): access to the retail area only after age/identity confirmation; all sales within the retail area except delivery / drive-in / drive-through; curbside delivery under video surveillance within 30 feet of the facility, with age verification; hours 7 a.m.–10 p.m.; acceptable ID: “A valid Medical Card issued by the SWO Licensing department, a medical card issued by any other federally recognized tribe or a Medical Card issued by a state”; daily limits §Q (above); inventory tracked through Flourish; packaging warning (verbatim): “GOVERNMENT WARNING: THIS PRODUCT CONTAINS MARIJUANA. KEEP OUT OF REACH OF CHILDREN AND ANIMALS. MARIJUANA MAY ONLY BE POSSESSED OR CONSUMED BY PERSONS 21 YEARS OF AGE OR OLDER UNLESS THE PERSON IS A QUALIFIED PATIENT. MARIJUANA USE WHILE PREGNANT OR BREASTFEEDING MAY BE HARMFUL. CONSUMPTION OF MARIJUANA IMPAIRS YOUR ABILITY TO DRIVE AND OPERATE MACHINERY. PLEASE USE EXTREME CAUTION.”

10.4 Flandreau Native Nations and Pine Ridge No Worries — in detail

Flandreau — Native Nations Cannabis

  • 2015 (secondary summaries — no ordinance text quoted, see §10.2): Executive Committee approved the marijuana ordinance in June 2015 (Federal Bar presentation: enacted by Resolution 15-56 on 11 June 2015 — OCR-fragmented, Verify resolution number/date against the tribal code). SDPB, 16 June 2015 (accessed 30 October 2026). Original plan (President Tony Reider, Tribal AG Seth Pearman): grow facility (10–12 jobs) + separate sale/consumption facility; sales 1 gram or less at a time; consumption only within the facility boundary; 21+ with ID for recreational purchase; under-21 only with doctor's recommendation; no home consumption for tribal members living on the reservation under the 2015 plan; no home cultivation provision; three-member marijuana control commission to be established; security personnel and surveillance cameras. The 2015 plan also contemplated a smoking lounge with nightclub, bar and food service and eventually an outdoor music venue — described as a “marijuana resort” / “adult playground” in contemporary reporting.
  • 2021 relaunch (secondary): Native Nations Cannabis, tribal business through wholly owned LLC FSST Pharms, LLC; dispensary south of First American Mart, east side of Veterans Street, Flandreau (Moody County Enterprise, accessed 30 October 2026). Sales began 1 July 2021 (IM 26 effective date), medical only at launch. Tribal medical programme (2021 reporting): requires a medical professional's signature/recommendation; tribe recognises cards from other tribes, states, territories and countries; tribal card cost $50. Run 1 address: Native Nations Cannabis, 1301 S Veterans St Ste 1, Flandreau (tribe-owned, vertically integrated, first medical seller in SD).
  • Current (2026) status: Verify — purchase/possession limits, prices, tax rate, licence structure and adult-use status were not retrieved for 2026 (see §10.2). Native Nations is described in Run 1 and current tribal-business listings as medical; that description alone does not prove the absence of an adult-use tribal authorisation.

Pine Ridge — No Worries Dispensary

  • Referendum and ordinance (secondary — no ordinance text quoted): tribal members voted 10 March 2020 (results announced 11 March 2020, certification by end of March 2020 per AP via Cannabis Business Times, accessed 30 October 2026): medical legalisation 82% approval, adult-use 74% approval (Verify against tribal election certification). Tribal Council voted 11–8 in October 2020 to enact an ~80-page ordinance; effective in 30 days. Sources: NORML, 28 October 2020; MJBizDaily, 2 November 2020 (both accessed 30 October 2026). Provisions as reported (secondary, NOT ordinance text): use permitted for both patients and adults (non-patients 21+); tribal members may cultivate and dispense; social consumption facilities permitted, accessible to both tribal members and non-members; initial plan was for the Tribe NOT to take ownership of production/retail, but to license individuals and levy a retail tax (Verify current licensing structure). Pine Ridge is described as the first tribe to legalise marijuana in a state that had not yet legalised it (NORML/MJBizDaily, October–November 2020).
  • No Worries (secondary): tribally licensed, privately owned (not tribe-operated, unlike Native Nations), vertically integrated (grow/process/sell), opened late May 2022. Business site (Run 1): No Worries Dispensary (accessed 30 October 2026). Listings show 4 E Ridge Loop Rd, Pine Ridge (Verify). Contemporary opening report (accessed 30 October 2026) — No Worries was the first dispensary to open on Pine Ridge; “We're South Dakota's first recreational marijuana dispensary” (Eagle Bull, quoted); products: pre-rolls, flower, concentrates, edibles; at opening, “up to a quarter (of an ounce)” per purchase, subject to supply; roughly half of customers from off-reservation at opening (including from across the Nebraska border); many off-reservation customers stayed in town and consumed on-reservation.
  • Current operation (secondary, KFF Health News, 2022, republished, accessed 30 October 2026): recreational marijuana legal on Pine Ridge for 21+ (alcohol sale/consumption banned on the reservation for 100+ years; 2013 alcohol-legalisation referendum passed narrowly but Tribal Council never implemented it; 2020 voters rejected alcohol sales/consumption in the two casinos while approving cannabis); dispensaries may sell only reservation-grown marijuana; customers prohibited from transporting cannabis off-reservation; providing marijuana to minors punishable by jail time; fines for drugged driving; several dispensaries on the reservation. KFF reports ~40% of No Worries customers live outside the reservation (owner estimate, Adonis Saltes). Current (2026) No Worries prices, limits, licence count and regulator name: Verify — none retrieved.

10.5 Tribal cards for non-members and state recognition — THREE-POSITION CONFLICT (do not resolve by picking a side)

Run 1 statutory reading governs as the starting point: tribal medical cards are NOT in the current SD nonresident-cardholder definition (which lists state/district/territory/commonwealth/insular possession/country). 2024 SB 141 (add tribal card) DIED — deferred to 41st day 5–1, 29 January 2024. No AG opinion or court case on tribal cards was located in Run 1 — Verify, do not invent. Flandreau's Native Nations accepting other jurisdictions' cards is a tribal-law purchase rule on tribal land, not state recognition off-reservation.

Three positions found, all to be presented together:

  1. Argus Leader review (via ND Insider, 18 June 2021, updated 20 July 2021, accessed 30 October 2026): claims SDCL ch. 34 “nonresident” includes cards from “another government entity, including tribes”, and that state lawmakers who vetted both laws said a Flandreau cardholder could purchase in Flandreau and take product anywhere else in SD. Label: 2021 press interpretation, not statute text.
  2. SD Highway Patrol, July 2021 (SDPB, 1 July 2021, above): Highway Patrol said it would not arrest people possessing ≤3 oz with supporting physician documentation, but “a tribal card will only protect tribal members” until the state began issuing cards/licences; >3 oz vulnerable to arrest. Label: enforcement statement, not statute.
  3. Statutory + legislative history (Run 1): definition excludes tribes; SB 141 (2024) would have added tribal cards and died. This points the other way from position 1.

Conclusion for the dossier: the question whether a tribal card protects a non-member off-reservation is UNRESOLVED on the evidence retrieved — flag Verify. Warn non-members that relying on the Argus interpretation off-reservation is legally risky. SWO's own rule (§1.2, §3.3, §N in §10.3) recognises state and other-tribal cards for SWO on-reservation purposes; that is tribal-law recognition on SWO land, not SD state recognition off-reservation. The SWO “(iii) website” card phrase (§1.2) is unexplained — Verify, do not interpret.

SD nonresident rule itself (Run 1, restated): nonresident cardholders are recognised with DOH confirmation/registration (not by mere possession of another state's card); §34-20G-3 protects transporting/purchasing/possessing/using up to 3 oz + DOH product amount; DOH rules designate no more than two dispensaries (procedure/fee Verify).

Secondary-guide error to flag, not follow: one secondary guide (southdakotamarijuanacard.org, accessed 30 October 2026) states “South Dakota does not permit medical marijuana cardholders from other states to purchase within its borders” and “no licensed dispensary accepts out-of-state cards.” This contradicts §34-20G-3's purchase protection for confirmed nonresidents and the Marijuana Herald “temporary registration pathway” description. The final dossier must flag this as a secondary-guide error/confusion (likely conflating “no automatic reciprocity” with “no purchase pathway”) and restate the statute + DOH-confirmation requirement, with DOH procedure Verify. Corroboration for the DOH-enrolment position (secondary, not authority): socommjclinic.com, June 2025 update (accessed 30 October 2026) — “Non-resident cannabis patients must submit proof of qualifying medical conditions to the state to enroll as non-resident patients before they can purchase.”

10.6 Tribal medical Schedule III “state-licensed” question — VERIFY (unresolved)

  • Given baseline: state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026. AG Opinion 26-06 (§9.2) addresses state medical-marijuana programmes and state-licensed businesses and the DEA registration pathway for them. No federal guidance document was found in this pass stating whether tribal medical programmes (e.g. Flandreau Santee Sioux) count as “state-licensed” under the federal rule. Mark as Verify. Do not resolve it.
  • Tribal adult-use activity (e.g. Oglala Sioux Tribe law) remains Schedule I federally under the baseline, regardless of tribal or state law.
  • Practical consequence of the unresolved question: whether a tribal medical establishment can register with DEA as a Schedule III manufacturer/distributor/dispensary, and whether §280E relief applies to tribal medical activity, cannot be stated as settled — Verify against DEA guidance and any future AG opinion. Do not cite Opinion 26-06 for tribal status.

10.7 Interstate — Minnesota, Montana, North Dakota, Nebraska, Iowa, Wyoming

Overarching rules (apply before any state-by-state row)

  1. Interstate transport is a separate federal offence risk even between two legal states. Reciprocity / visiting-patient rules, where they exist, address possession/purchase after entering the destination state; they do not authorise carrying cannabis across a state line. Sources (secondary guides — Verify against destination-state law for any travel advice): Marijuana Herald reciprocity guide, updated 11 October 2026; CannaCon buyer guide, 21 August 2025; Leafwell reciprocity guide (all accessed 30 October 2026). TSA follows federal law, not state law (same guides — secondary, Verify TSA current guidance if cited).
  2. SD outbound: an SD card does NOT authorise carrying SD-purchased cannabis into another state, and does NOT guarantee purchase/possession rights there. Each destination's rule below governs, and the destination's own purchase must be used if lawful there. A non-patient carrying any cannabis out of SD faces that state's penalties plus federal transport risk; a patient carrying cannabis out of SD also loses SD's §34-20G protection once outside SD jurisdiction (the protection is for conduct “in accordance with this chapter” in SD — Verify whether §34-20G-3's “transporting” protection extends to transport into SD by a confirmed nonresident vs transport out of SD by a resident; do not overstate).
  3. SD state-line / in-state penalties (Run 1, restated for bring-back): non-patient possession in SD is §22-42-6 (≤2 oz Class 1 misdemeanour; >2 oz–<½ lb Class 6 felony; ½–<1 lb Class 5; 1–10 lb Class 4; >10 lb Class 3; + up to $10,000 civil penalty); distribution/possession with intent §22-42-7 (tiers Verify); ingestion §22-42-5.1 (post-SB 83, §9.3.3); vehicle smoking §22-42-24 (enactment Verify in Parts 9–10; confirmed in Part 12: SL 2021 ch. 100); operating under influence unprotected (§34-20G-18(4)). No SD “border checkpoint” or SD-specific transport-permit regime for medical cannabis was found — DPS hemp transport permits (SDCL ch. 38-35, pre-2027) are a hemp regime, not a medical-cannabis regime; do not conflate.
  4. Practical advice in secondary guides (label as advice, not law): consume/gift/dispose in the destination state before driving back; do not assume a neighbouring-state medical card works in SD without DOH confirmation. MN guide (secondary, accessed 30 October 2026).
NeighbourWhat an SD patient / non-patient may lawfully do thereReciprocity (visiting-patient)Bring-back into SDPenalties / notes
Minnesota (borders SD east; I-90 / US-14 / US-75 corridor to Sioux Falls, Watertown, Brookings)Adult-use legal in MN for 21+ (state law). SD patient 21+ may lawfully purchase adult-use cannabis in MN if MN retail is open to non-residents (Verify MN residency/non-resident retail rule and purchase limits at time of travel). For 21+ SD patients the adult-use purchase route is the safer statement, with medical pricing/limits Verify.Same interstate-transport bar as MN. Nebraska (borders SD south)Update vs older secondary summaries: Nebraska voters approved limited medical cannabis legalisation in November 2024 via Initiative Measures 437 and 438 (Marijuana Herald state guide, updated 1 October 2026; Herb Omaha guide — both accessed 30 October 2026). Qualifying patients are protected for possession of up to 5 oz for medical purposes (same guides). Nebraska has established a Medical Cannabis Commission and is continuing to implement a regulated system; as of the 2026 secondary guides, there is NO operational, state-licensed dispensary anywhere in Nebraska — Verify implementation status at 30 October 2026 from the Nebraska Medical Cannabis Commission directly before stating it as current. Adult-use remains fully illegal in NE.No out-of-state medical patients accepted (CannaCon 2025; LegalClarity lists NE among states with no legal path for visiting patients / no medical reciprocity, accessed 30 October 2026). Iowa medical dispensary in Council Bluffs (~1.5 miles from Omaha) requires Iowa residency + Iowa provider certification + Iowa card + $100 annual fee (Herb guide — secondary, Verify Iowa rules). Missouri recreational dispensary in Rock Port, ~70–75 driving miles from Omaha, is the nearest adult-use option for 21+ Nebraskans/SD travellers in that corridor (Herb guide — secondary, distance Verify). Do NOT use the older Cannigma summary (predates 2024 medical approval, says medical attempts “all failed”) for Nebraska.SD ↔ NE: an SD patient has no confirmed NE purchase route (no dispensary + no reciprocity); an NE qualified patient has no SD purchase route without SD DOH nonresident confirmation. Bring-back in either direction is unlawful in the destination state plus federal transport risk. Law-enforcement patrols on I-80/I-29 near borders are noted in secondary guides (Herb) — attribute as secondary observation, not a legal rule.NE possession penalties (secondary, Herb — Verify against Nebraska statutes): first offence ≤1 oz = infraction, up to $300 fine; second offence up to $400 + 5 days jail; third/subsequent up to $500 + 7 days jail. Wyoming (borders SD west)Adult-use illegal; comprehensive medical illegal.
