Wyoming prohibits synthetic substances in hemp products and counts psychoactive THC isomers (same guide, truncated in excerpt — Verify full Wyoming hemp rule; do not quote a threshold not retrieved).None — Wyoming listed among states with no legal cannabis access for visitors / no medical reciprocity (LegalClarity, above; Ganjly August 2026 list of no-programme states includes Wyoming — note Ganjly also wrongly lists SD among “limited or no programs” states in one passage and contradicts itself on Nebraska, so use it only as a lead, flagged secondary).SD patient/non-patient in WY: SD card gives no protection; possession is unlawful except within the narrow low-THC exemption (Verify). WY resident in SD: no WY card/recognition to bring; SD DOH nonresident confirmation required.WY penalties Verify against Wyoming statutes; SD §22-42-6 on the SD side.

10.8 Tribal tourism carry-off rules — single rule, tribe-by-tribe variations

General rule: purchase/use lawful on tribal land under tribal law does NOT make possession/use lawful off tribal land. Off-reservation, SD state law or the neighbouring state's law applies, plus federal transport risk. This is stated consistently for Flandreau (AG Jackley 2015/2021; Highway Patrol July 2021), Pine Ridge (KFF; Rapid City Post: “any products purchased on the reservation must only be consumed there and cannot be taken off tribal lands”), and SWO (Regulations §6.3, §10.3).

  • Flandreau carry-off (conflicted, see §10.5): 2015 plan = consume on-reservation only, 1-gram sales to deter carry-off. 2021 Argus interpretation = tribal cardholder may take product anywhere in SD. Highway Patrol July 2021 = tribal card protects tribal members only (until state cards issued). Run 1 statutory reading + SB 141 failure = tribal card not in state definition. Present as an unresolved 2021 conflict, not as a settled carry-off permission, and warn non-members that relying on the Argus interpretation off-reservation is legally risky.
  • Pine Ridge carry-off: prohibited by tribal law itself (reservation-grown only; customers prohibited from transporting elsewhere — KFF/NORML summaries). No exception for SD cardholders was found: an SD cardholder who buys adult-use cannabis on Pine Ridge and carries it into SD is (a) violating Pine Ridge tribal law, (b) possessing in SD without SD §34-20G protection for that product unless the person is independently an SD cardholder/confirmed nonresident AND the product was obtained in compliance with SD law — and SD dispensary purchase rules (§34-20G-70/71) do not authorise tribal-dispensary purchases as SD dispensary purchases. Flag the precise interaction Verify; do not state that an SD card “launders” Pine Ridge product into SD legality.
  • SWO carry-off: Regulations §6.3 governs facility transport/transfer/sale (kept within the Reservation except secure provision to a third-party testing facility); §6.7(D) limits possession/use to private residences of registered patients. Whether an individual cardholder may carry purchased product off-reservation is not expressly answered in the text read — flag Verify against the Code; do not assume.
  • Tourism practicalities (secondary only, label as such): Flandreau's proximity to Sioux Falls / Minneapolis / Omaha and Pine Ridge's size (~3,500 square miles / 2+ million acres, Rapid City Post / Beard Bros) explain why MN/NE/SD visitors are the relevant tourist population, but they are not legal rules. Beard Bros summary (secondary, accessed 30 October 2026).

10.9 Disputes and litigation between tribes and state/federal government over cannabis

Flandreau 2015 — federal-raid warning, crop destruction, consultant charges, USDA hemp suit

  • Tribe legalised in June 2015 under the Cole Memo / December 2014 DOJ Indian Country framework. AG Jackley warned from the outset that changes in tribal law would only affect tribal members and that non-tribal members using marijuana on the reservation risked prosecution under state law, and that non-tribal members returning to state land with marijuana in their system were breaking state law (GGB Magazine summary of the 2015 position, accessed 30 October 2026).
  • Crop burned, November 2015 (now confirmed, correcting Run 1's “reason not confirmed” gap — but note the source limits): after government-to-government consultation with the United States, the Tribal Council voted to temporarily suspend cultivation/distribution; the first crop was burned in batches — about 600 plants in all — after federal officials signalled a possible raid. Tribal attorney Seth C. Pearman, quoted in GGB Magazine: “After government-to-government consultation with the United States, the Flandreau Santee Sioux Tribe is temporarily suspending its marijuana cultivation and distribution facilities.” DOJ representative Tim Purdon reported the department did not say a raid was “imminent” and that a threat was not made (Green Eugene summary, accessed 30 October 2026). Lakota Times, 30 January 2020 (accessed 30 October 2026): “In 2015, the consultant the tribe had used regarding cannabis production didn't give accurate information regarding the transportation process of marijuana which resulted in the tribe's future production of cannabis to be halted indefinitely. Unfortunately, this costly error raised questions from the federal government which resulted in the tribe deciding to burn their crop in order to save the Indoor Growing Facility they had invested in. Since then their facility sat dormant.” The facility built for marijuana was later repurposed for hemp (same article; USDA authorised Flandreau hemp, below).
  • Consultant charges, August 2016 (AP via Native Sun News, 17 August 2016, accessed 30 October 2026): two consultants who helped plan the “marijuana resort” entered opposing pleas to drug offences: Jonathan Hunt (oversaw the first crop) pleaded guilty to a drug conspiracy count in the city of Flandreau (adjacent to the reservation); Eric Hagen (CEO, Colorado consulting firm Monarch America) pleaded not guilty to conspiracy to possess, possession and attempt to possess more than 10 pounds of marijuana. Charges filed 3 August 2016, eight months after tribal leaders destroyed the crop. Court documents: Hunt ordered 55 different strains of marijuana seeds from a Netherlands company, shipped in CD cases sewn into shirts to the tribe's office in August 2015; Hunt and others planted about 30 strains in September–October 2015 at the greenhouse. Hagen's attorney argued the prosecution proceeded under a “legal fiction” (that Jackley was charging conduct on tribal land under state law). Final dispositions of the Hunt/Hagen charges: Verify — not retrieved.
  • USDA hemp suit, 2019 (secondary): Flandreau Santee Sioux Tribe sued USDA in SD federal district court seeking to force USDA to approve its tribal hemp plan or get out of the way, after the 2018 Farm Bill (Cannabis Now, 5 July 2019, accessed 30 October 2026). Pearman statement (via Indian Country Today, quoted in Cannabis Now): the Tribe “will continue to consult with the federal and state governments and hopes to be granted parity with states that have legalized marijuana.” Outcome of the 2019 suit: Verify. Flandreau's hemp plan was subsequently approved by USDA (AMS list, §10.2; 500nations: Flandreau approved 22 January 2020 / first to grow hemp, secondary — Verify exact approval date against AMS).
  • No Flandreau-specific state enforcement action, seizure or lawsuit beyond the items above was retrieved — Unavailable/Verify.

Oglala / White Plume — federal CSA / tribal-sovereignty precedent (hemp, not marijuana)

  • 1998: Oglala Sioux Tribal Council Ordinance 98-27 amends Tribal Penal Code Title 9 §106 (Marijuana) and §106.00 (Controlled Drugs and Substances), defining industrial hemp to exclude cannabis with less than 1% THC (Eighth Circuit opinion summary).
  • April 2000: Alex White Plume and family plant hemp on Pine Ridge trust land (Manderson area) under tribal ordinance, without DEA registration, contracted to Tierra Madre, LLC.
  • August 2000: federal agents (DEA, FBI, U.S. Marshals — about 30 agents in the 2000 raid per HempBuild Magazine, accessed 30 October 2026) raid and destroy the crop under search/court order.
  • 2001: Percy White Plume crop destroyed; DEA registration application sent but never completed, no registration issued.
  • 2002: Alex White Plume crop destroyed again; U.S. Attorney serves 8 civil charges (secondary) and the government seeks a declaration of CSA violation + permanent injunction instead of prosecution.
  • District Court (D.S.D.) grants summary judgment for the United States, permanently enjoining cultivation without DEA registration.
  • United States v. White Plume, 447 F.3d 1067 (8th Cir. 2006) (submitted 12 December 2005, filed 17 May 2006, Judges Bye, Beam, Gruender; Nos. 05-1654, 05-1656): affirmed. Holdings as summarised (secondary summaries of the opinion — Verify against the opinion itself): industrial hemp is included in the CSA definition of marijuana unless specifically exempted; the Treaty of Fort Laramie does not grant the Tribe a right to cultivate hemp; the Court acknowledged DEA registration could be a burden and hemp might be a good crop for Pine Ridge, but that did not change the CSA result.
  • Injunction not lifted until 2016, after the 2014 and 2018 federal Farm Bills opened hemp cultivation nationally (HempBuild Magazine, secondary — Verify lifting order). White Plume later built one of the first U.S. hemp houses in Manderson in 2008 (same source — note the tension: hemp house built while the injunction was still in place; the source does not explain the material source, so do not infer the house used his own 2008 crop). Legal fight described as 14 years with a threatened 30-year sentence at one point, draining the family's bison ranching operation (same source, secondary — attribute, do not adopt as court fact).
  • Relevance to 2026: White Plume is a federal CSA / tribal-sovereignty precedent, NOT a ruling on SD's 2020 hemp law or on the 2018 Farm Bill hemp definition (which post-dates it). Do not cite it as current authority that tribal hemp is illegal.
  • No Pine Ridge-specific adult-use lawsuit was retrieved. Distinguish White Plume (hemp, federal) from any Pine Ridge adult-use dispute (none found).

State–tribal tax / regulatory disputes — none retrieved for cannabis specifically

SWO Tax Code (Ch. 67) compliance is required by SWO Regulations §1.7, and Flandreau charges an 8% tribal sales tax (secondary, 2021) — but no state–tribal tax compact, revenue-sharing agreement, or tax dispute was found. Flag compact/tax-agreement status as Unavailable for all nine tribes. Puffy's / Rapid City (§9.4) and Hemp Quarters 605 (§9.4) are state–local and state–retailer disputes respectively, not tribal disputes — do not conflate. Puffy's Sturgis location and Rapid City litigation do not involve tribal land or tribal licences.

Parts 9–10 consolidated Verify/Unavailable list (sections A–C, 26 + 9 + conflicts) is reproduced in full in the Combined Research Notes at the end of this file, merged with the Run 1 gaps and the Parts 7–8 and 11–12 lists.

Part 11 — Operations Rules

Research / access date for every source below: 30 October 2026. The 3 November 2026 election has NOT passed; no 2026 result is stated. Scope: Part 11 only. This is a research report for incorporation into the Run 2 HTML, not the HTML itself. How to read flags: Opened directly = official page/PDF text was read in this research. Search-result / secondary = excerpt or non-official source; flagged Verify where the full official text matters. Verify / Unavailable = not confirmed or not found; do not treat as settled law. No personal phone numbers or emails are reproduced.

0. STATUS AND TIMING — READ FIRST

  • In-force version used here: ARSD Article 44:90 as amended effective 5 October 2021 (48 SDR 40), 8 August 2022 (49 SDR 9), 22 November 2022 (49 SDR 47), 27 November 2023 (50 SDR 62, covering the 1 November 2023 adopted-rules package), and 9 September 2024 (51 SDR 24, the $9,000 fee). The cleanest in-force anchor opened in this research is the Legislative Research Council compilation printed 13 January 2026 (https://lexi-cann.com/assets/documents/South_Dakota_ARSD_44-90_Admin_Rules.pdf) and the marked-up 1 November 2023 adopted rules (https://rules.sd.gov/Uploads/782_AdoptedRules.pdf), both read 30 Oct 2026. Where the two differ, the later compilation controls and the difference is flagged.
  • NOT in force at 30 Oct 2026 — do not apply: DOH's 2026 ARSD 44:90 amendments, filed with the Secretary of State 16 October 2026, effective 5 November 2026 (53 SDR 56, 26 Oct 2026; hearing 23 Sept 2026; Interim Rules Review Committee review complete 14 Oct 2026, 6–0). Secondary reporting (Marijuana Herald, 2026; WeedPress, 27 Oct 2026) describes 35 sections amended, including a first-offence corrective-action-plan regime for Category 4/5 violations, COA decontamination/remediation disclosure, and video-surveillance/visitor-access changes, but the adopted-rules document was not downloadable as of 27 Oct 2026 and its exact text could not be verified. Run 1's "38 proposed amendments" vs the Register's "35 sections" is a counting/proposal-vs-final difference — Verify the final adopted text before Run 2 publication. Nothing in this Part 11 report relies on the 5 Nov 2026 amendments.
  • Statutory anchor: SDCL ch. 34-20G, especially §§ 34-20G-55 (application), -63 (oversight/record-keeping), -64 (security), -65/-66 (cultivation/manufacturing), -67 (no shared office/referral), -68 (no consumption on establishment property), -69 (inspection), -70/-71 (dispensing/records), -72 (rulemaking, incl. (4) establishment governance, (5) suspension/termination procedures, (6) fines/probation, (7) labelling). Opened directly in Run 1; not re-opened section-by-section here except where cited.
  • Fixed federal baseline (as given, not re-researched): state-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; §280E generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity, fully applicable to adult-use; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 Nov 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 Dec 2026, after which the full total-THC definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA enforcement of DEA-registered lab testing for hemp is delayed to 31 December 2026; federal medical appropriations rider through 11 Dec 2026; no federal cannabis banking law. Tribal medical as "state-licensed" is Verify.

1. CONFLICTS WITH RUN 1 — EXPLICIT CORRECTIONS AND CONFIRMATIONS

# Run 1 position Part 11 finding Treatment for Run 2 HTML
C1 ARSD 44:90:10:03–:05 mg/serving/package numbers "not extracted — Verify" Resolved in part. In the 1 Nov 2023 adopted rules, ARSD 44:90:07:04(3)–(4) (manufacturing) as amended reads (strikethrough = old text): manufacture no edible (except tincture, oil, capsule) with "more than ten fifty milligrams of THC per serving" and package no marketing layer (except tincture/oil/capsule containing oil) "with more than 100 five hundred milligrams of total THC". Source: https://rules.sd.gov/Uploads/782_AdoptedRules.pdf, opened directly 30 Oct 2026. The 2021 Cornell text still showing 10 mg / 100 mg is therefore superseded. Use 50 mg/serving, 500 mg/marketing layer as the in-force manufacturing limits at 30 Oct 2026. Do NOT use the 2026 SB 194 proposed caps (30% flower, 60% concentrate, 5 mg/100 mg edibles) — those were DEAD (Run 1) and are far below the adopted rule limits.
C2 Product-equivalency table "Verify" Resolved. ARSD 44:90:02:16, 1 Nov 2023 adopted rules (opened directly): smokable concentrate 8 g = 1 oz; vaporizer pens/cartridges 8 g = 1 oz; cannabis oil/tincture in oral dosage syringe/capsule 15 g = 1 oz (amended from 5 g); edibles (excluding oils) 2,000 mg THC = 1 oz (amended from 800 mg); topical ointment/cream/lotion 12 fl oz = 1 oz; topical dried plant material/powder 16 oz = 1 oz (amended from 1 oz); transdermal patch 800 mg THC = 1 oz. A cardholder's products plus flower/trim must not exceed 3 oz equivalent (SDCL 34-20G-1(1)). Adopt this table. It replaces the pre-2023 5 g / 800 mg / 1 oz figures still repeated in secondary summaries.
C3 Beverage packaging limits "not extracted" Partly resolved, partly Verify. Cornell's 2021 text of ARSD 44:90:10:05 (https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-10-05, search-result 30 Oct 2026) states: single-serving beverages ≤10 mg THC in child-resistant container, metal can with stay-tab, or glass bottle with cork/crown cap; multi-serving beverages >10 mg and ≤100 mg THC in child-resistant resealable container; bundled marketing layer total ≤100 mg THC; hash marks do not qualify as a measuring device. The 1 Nov 2023 adopted-rules excerpt read here does not reproduce §§ 44:90:10:03–:05 in full, so whether the beverage 10/100 mg limits were also raised with the edible limits is Verify against the Jan 2026 compilation. State the 2021 beverage limits only as "2021 text, Verify whether amended in 2023/2026". Do not assume beverages follow the 50/500 mg edible amendment.
C4 Labelling serving reference Apparent tension — Verify. ARSD 44:90:10:12(3) in the 1 Nov 2023 text still requires an edible label (except tinctures/oils/capsules) to "identify the size of a serving providing ten milligrams of [THC]" plus servings per marketing layer and a nutrition panel (21 CFR 101.9), even though § 44:90:07:04 now allows 50 mg per serving. Whether the Jan 2026 compilation or the Nov 2026 package conforms § 44:90:10:12 to 50 mg is Verify. Flag the tension; do not silently rewrite either section.
C5 Facility plant/canopy limits "Verify; do not apply patient 2+2" Confirmed: no statewide facility plant/canopy cap was found. No ARSD 44:90:05 section states a maximum plant count or canopy area for a cultivation facility. Facility scale is controlled through the approved site plan/operating procedures, Metrc plant tagging (see §5), and local zoning/caps (Run 1 Part 3). The patient 2 flowering + 2 non-flowering limit (SDCL 34-20G-1(1)(c)) applies to cardholders only. Repeat the Run 1 warning verbatim in Run 2; do not invent a facility cap.
C6 One-year operational rule (Run 1, via Puffy's) Confirmed and clarified. ARSD 44:90:03:16 (1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly): a certificate awarded under the scoring/lottery procedure "must become operational within one year, defined as three hundred sixty-five days, or, if a leap year, three hundred sixty-six days, of the date of [award/issue] or the certificate is deemed void and must be awarded to the next applicant on the waiting list", subject to one extension of up to an additional year on documented timely efforts, requested at least two weeks before expiry. Puffy's, LLC v. State, 2025 S.D. 10 (Run 1) held a waitlist applicant is neither an initial nor a renewal applicant for fee purposes under the rules as then written. State the one-year + one-extension rule; flag waitlist fee treatment as Verify post-Puffy's and under the Nov 2026 package.
C7 Establishment fee $9,000 Confirmed. Jan 2026 compilation, § 44:90:03:17: "registration certificate of nine thousand dollars", source line 48 SDR 40 (2021); 50 SDR 62 (27 Nov 2023); 51 SDR 24 (9 Sept 2024); authority SDCL 34-20G-72(10), SL 2024 ch. 138 § 2. No conflict.
C8 ARSD chapter list (Run 1: 44:90:06/:08 uncertain) Resolved. Jan 2026 compilation contents: 44:90:01 Definitions; :02 Registry cards; :03 Registration certificates; :04 Establishments; :05 Cultivation; :06 Testing; :07 Manufacturing; :08 Dispensaries; :09 Sampling/testing; :10 Packaging/labelling/advertising; :11 Recordkeeping; :12 Enforcement; :13 Petitions, Repealed (consistent with repeal of SDCL 34-20G-26 by 2023 SB 1, Run 1). Use this chapter list.
C9 Food-service licence for manufacturing (Run 1: secondary only) Still secondary only. No ARSD 44:90:07 section requiring a food-service licence was located in this research. Keep as Verify; do not state it as a rule.
C10 "38 amendments" (Run 1) vs "35 sections" (Oct 2026 Register) Unreconciled. See §0. The difference may be proposal (38) vs final (35), or section-counting. Flag in Run 2 notes; do not merge the numbers.
C11 Low-quality secondary sources rejected A foodhandlersguide.com cultivation page found in search states medical cultivation is illegal/felony and that no permits, inspections or pesticide rules exist — directly contradicted by SDCL ch. 34-20G and ARSD art. 44:90. It is not used anywhere in this report. A mediajel.com advertising page mixes North Dakota rules into a "South Dakota" page; its North Dakota content is not used; only its South Dakota ad-content summary is cited, as secondary/Verify (see §9). Do not cite either page as authority.

2. TESTING — MANDATORY TESTS, LABS, BATCHES, SAMPLING, FAILURE

2.1 Mandatory testing (ARSD 44:90:09:01, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; 49 SDR 9 eff. 8 Aug 2022)

Verbatim core (https://sdlegislature.gov/api/Rules/Archived/12513.pdf, search-result 30 Oct 2026; confirmed in 1 Nov 2023 adopted rules, opened directly):

"A cultivation facility or cannabis product manufacturing facility must test every batch of cannabis or cannabis product intended for consumption prior to transfer for retail sale."

1 Nov 2023 amendment adds: testing is in final form prior to transfer; any alteration after receipt of results creates a new final form (and therefore a new testing obligation). Tests required: - From 1 July 2022: (a) potency — THC content and, if so labelled, CBD content; THC variance may not exceed ±10% (1 Nov 2023 text); (b) microbials listed in § 44:90:05:10(3). - From 1 July 2023: (a) mycotoxins § 44:90:05:10(2); (b) metals § 44:90:05:10(4) and § 44:90:07:03; (c) pesticides § 44:90:05:10(1); (d) solvents § 44:90:07:03. - Absence / prohibited transfer: §§ 44:90:09:02–:03 (titles: "Absence of mandatory testing"; "Prohibited transfer of cannabis or cannabis product — Exceptions"). Full text not extracted — Verify the exceptions (e.g. transfers for testing/remediation/destruction) before stating them. A product that has not passed a required test may not be manufactured into a further product (§ 44:90:07:04(8): no manufacture "using cannabis or concentrated cannabis that has not passed any test required by the department").

2.2 Action levels — contaminants that make cannabis "nonusable" (ARSD 44:90:05:10, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021)

"Cannabis is nonusable if it contains a level of any of the following contaminants at or in excess of the action level." Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-05-10 (search-result 30 Oct 2026). Selected levels (full analyte tables Verify in the Jan 2026 compilation): - Pesticides (µg/g / ppm): abamectin 0.5; azoxystrobin 0.2; bifenazate 0.2; etoxazole 0.2; imazalil 0.2; imidacloprid 0.4; malathion 0.2; myclobutanil 0.2; permethrins 0.2; spinosad 0.2; spiromesifen 0.2; spirotetramat 0.2; tebuconazole 0.4; any other prohibited synthetic pesticide 0.5. Use or presence of a pesticide whose active ingredient is not on the DOH-approved list is a violation and the treated cannabis is nonusable; knowing use/presence of an unapproved synthetic agent, or of a nonsynthetic substance prohibited in organic production (7 CFR 205.602), is a "serious and knowing violation". - Mycotoxins (µg/kg / ppb): aflatoxins B1, B2, G1, G2: 20; ochratoxin A: 20. - Microbials (cfu/g): total aerobic bacteria 10⁵; Shiga toxin-producing E. coli (STEC) <1; Aspergillus (fumigatus, flavus, terreus, niger) <1; total yeast and mold 10⁴. - Heavy metals (µg/g / ppm): arsenic 0.2; cadmium 0.2; lead 0.5; mercury 0.1. - Solvents: listed in § 44:90:07:03. The solvent action-level table was not extracted — Verify. A secondary summary states products containing detectable residual solvent other than glycol, glycerin, ethanol, propylene or cooking oil are nonusable; that summary is not adopted here as the rule text — Verify against § 44:90:07:03.

2.3 Testing facilities — accreditation and independence

  • ISO/IEC 17025: ARSD 44:90:06:01 (1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly): on successful registration and before accepting cannabis for testing, a testing facility must begin working with an accreditation body toward ISO/IEC 17025 accreditation with a scope covering all analytical tests it performs, and successfully complete accreditation within 32 months of registration (amended from 18 months); failure = the department must revoke registration. Accrediting bodies: IAS, A2LA, ANAB, or another board meeting ISO/IEC 17025:2017.
  • DEA registration: the facility "shall register with the Drug Enforcement Agency pursuant to 21 CFR part 1301.13 (in effect on June 28, 2021)". This is a state-rule DEA-registration requirement for medical testing labs; it is distinct from the federal Schedule III / DEA-registration discussion in Run 1 Part 2 and from the hemp DEA-lab rule (§11). Its interaction with Schedule III medical registration after 28 Apr 2026 is Verify — no DOH guidance was located.
  • Independence / colocation: the statutory definition calls a testing facility "independent" (SDCL 34-20G-1(5), Run 1). Operationally, ARSD 44:90:04:04(4)(a) prohibits a testing facility in the same structure as any other cannabis establishment (see §7). Whether common ownership (without colocation) is permitted is Verify — no rule text beyond "independent" and § 44:90:04:04 was located.
  • Proficiency testing / inter-laboratory comparison: stated only in a secondary testing-licence summary (southdakotastatecannabis.org, search-result 30 Oct 2026) — Verify against §§ 44:90:06:01–:10 before stating it as a rule.

2.4 Batches, sampling, and sample handling (ARSD 44:90:09:06–:09)

  • Batch definition (§ 44:90:01:01, 1 Nov 2023 text): (a) cannabis of the same strain, grown under the same conditions, harvested during a specified period from a specified cultivation area (except trim); or (b) cannabis products produced during a specified period using the same extraction/manufacturing method, formulation and/or recipe.
  • Maximum batch size: the sampling tables in § 44:90:09:07.01 top out at 50 lb for flower/trim and for concentrated cannabis — i.e. no sampled batch in those tables exceeds 50 lb. Run 1's "batch sizes not to exceed 50 pounds" (citing SDCL 34-20G-72(4)(l)) is consistent with the tables. Verify whether § 44:90:09:06 states a free-standing 50 lb cap or whether 50 lb is only the top table band; infused-product batches are banded by servings, not weight (up to 70,000 servings in the table).
  • Sampling tables (§ 44:90:09:07.01, 49 SDR 9 eff. 8 Aug 2022; 1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly):
  • Flower/trim (lb → minimum sample g → increments): 0–1.00 → 2.50 g / 5; 1.01–10.00 → 4.00 / 8; 10.01–20.00 → 7.50 / 15; 20.01–40.00 → 11.0 / 22; 40.01–50.00 → 16.50 / 33. Above-minimum sampling uses 0.5 g increments.
  • Concentrated cannabis: 0–1.00 lb → 1.25 g / 5; 1.01–2.00 → 2.00 / 8; 2.01–5.00 → 3.75 / 15; 5.01–15.00 → 5.50 / 22; 15.01–50.00 → 8.25 / 33. Above-minimum uses 0.25 g increments.
  • Infused products (unpackaged servings → minimum sample servings → units per 5/10/20/100-serving package): 0–100 → 5 / 2,2,2,2; 100–1,000 → 8 / 2,2,2,2; 1,000–5,000 → 15 / 3,2,2,2; 5,000–10,000 → 22 / 5,3,2,2; 10,000–50,000 → 33 / 7,4,2,2; 50,000–70,000 → 43 / 9,5,3,3. Above-minimum uses one-serving increments. A "serving unit" is the total servings in one product package for sale; the manufacturer must determine serving size per § 44:90:07:04 and servings per batch; if the minimum sample does not align with final form, the lab/designee must increase increments so products are sampled in final form.
  • Who collects, and when: § 44:90:09:07 (search-result, sdlegislature.gov, 30 Oct 2026): at minimum one sample from each batch created under § 44:90:09:06 or as directed by the testing facility based on batch size. Before 1 Jan 2024, samples could be collected by an agent of the testing facility or of the submitting establishment if collected in full view of security cameras (and other conditions in the full text — Verify); on or after 1 January 2024, samples must be collected by an agent of the testing facility. Collection must comply with the testing facility's SOPs and ISO/IEC 17025 requirements. Testing-facility SOPs for sample collection must address batch/sample identifiers, minimum quantities, representative methodology, cleaning/sanitising/contamination prevention, containers/sealing, and prevention of damage/degradation in storage/transport (§ 44:90:06:03, Cornell search-result 30 Oct 2026).
  • Final-form rule (§ 44:90:09:07.01): except pre-rolls, cannabis/products must be in final form ready to be packaged on receipt of passing results before sampling; the establishment may not alter the batch after sampling. The Nov 2026 package reportedly retains the pre-roll exception — but that package is not in force at 30 Oct 2026 (see §0); the pre-roll exception stated here is the in-force 2023 rule.
  • Sample packaging/transport: samples in appropriately labelled containers with tamper-evident seals (§ 44:90:04:21, see §6); sample packaging for testing § 44:90:09:08 (full text Verify); storage while awaiting results § 44:90:09:09 (full text Verify — do not state a storage-duration or segregation rule beyond "awaiting results" from the title).
  • Certificate of analysis (COA): retention § 44:90:09:04; payment of testing fees § 44:90:09:05. Neither section's operative text (retention period, who pays) was extracted — Verify. The Nov 2026 package reportedly requires COAs to state whether decontamination/pretreatment or post-failure remediation occurred — not in force at 30 Oct 2026; do not state it as current law. COA contents for labelling are § 44:90:10:09 (see §8).

2.5 Failure, remediation, destruction, recall (§§ 44:90:09:10–:12, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021)

  • On passing results: after the testing facility updates the inventory tracking system, the cultivation/manufacturing facility may transfer the batch to another establishment, subject to inventory-tracking requirements (§ 44:90:09:10).
  • On failing results: the batch is "nonusable" and must remain, until remediated or destroyed, in the same storage container with a new label showing the establishment ID, batch number, testing-facility name/ID, reason for failure, and, in bold capital letters no smaller than 12-point font, "PRODUCT FAILED TESTING" (§ 44:90:09:10).
  • Remediation (§ 44:90:09:11): a facility may elect to remediate a failed batch only as the section allows, using a department-approved process addressing the reason for failure; remediated product must be retested and pass all required tests. The section's analyte-specific bars (the search excerpt is truncated at "cannabis that fail tests for metals or pesticides may not be…" and "that fail tests for prohibited solvents may not be…") were not fully extracted — Verify § 44:90:09:11(1)–(5) in full before stating which failures are remediable. Do not state that metals/pesticide/solvent failures are remediable.
  • Destruction (§ 44:90:09:12): if unable or unwilling to remediate, the establishment must (1) note in the inventory tracking system, or if unavailable give written notice to the department within one business day, that it will destroy the batch; (2) follow its approved cannabis-waste destruction procedures; (3) ensure destruction is captured by functioning security cameras and stored according to this article. The section title in later compilations adds "Notice and recall" — the recall procedure itself was not extracted — Verify.
  • "Nonusable" definition (§ 44:90:01:01): "unfit for sale or, except for the purposes of remediation, transfer". A nonusable batch therefore cannot be transferred for sale even to another establishment except for remediation.

3. TRANSFERS BETWEEN ESTABLISHMENTS (ARSD 44:90:04:18–:23; 44:90:11:04)

  • What counts as a transfer: "Authorized transfer" (§ 44:90:01:01) = distribution between medical cannabis establishments allowable within inventory-tracking-system procedures. Only the four establishment types transfer to each other, within their statutory definitions (cultivation → any establishment; manufacturing → dispensary; testing = service, not sale; dispensary → cardholders only). No separate transporter/distributor licence exists (Run 1).
  • Manifest required for every transfer, any amount, except retail sales at a dispensary (§ 44:90:04:19). Contents: (1) transporting establishment's name, address, phone, licence number; (2) receiving establishment's name, address, phone, licence number; (3) phone number and web address of the department's secure verification system; (4) description and quantities (weight or unit) of all items including samples; (5) date of transport and approximate departure/arrival times; (6) vehicle make, model, licence plate; (7) name/signature of driver and any accompanying agent; (8) name/signature of the person accepting delivery. Source: https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-04-19 (search-result 30 Oct 2026); 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; 49 SDR 9 eff. 8 Aug 2022.
  • Separate manifest per recipient; three copies in the vehicle (§ 44:90:04:20): one for the recipient, one returned to the originator for record-keeping, one for law enforcement / department agent on a traffic stop or collision. Refused items must be noted on the manifest and in the originator's inventory records after return. A manifest may not be altered by anyone except as the section allows, and it does not replace any required chain-of-custody form.
  • Storage in transit (§ 44:90:04:21): all cannabis/products in an enclosed, locked area of the vehicle, out of public view; testing samples in appropriately labelled containers with tamper-evident seals showing clear, lasting evidence of prior opening; non-sample product in sealed containers identifying the recipient; refrigerated transport for edibles requiring refrigeration; temperature-controlled vehicles where necessary to prevent spoilage.
  • Conduct in transit (§ 44:90:04:22): only agents listed on the manifest may be in the vehicle, each wearing an agent badge; travel directly to manifest destinations, stopping only for meals (transport >3 hours round trip), legally required rest, refuelling, or exigent circumstances (collision, traffic stop, breakdown, weather/medical emergency); product may not be removed from the vehicle before the destination except in exigent circumstances in consultation with the department (§ 44:90:04:23); the vehicle must be made immediately available for department inspection on request; on law-enforcement contact, agents must produce badges and all manifests.
  • Incident notification (§ 44:90:04:23): title only in the materials read — Verify contents/deadline. Vehicle requirements (§ 44:90:04:18): title only — Verify (e.g. any vehicle registration/identification rule beyond the manifest's make/model/plate).
  • Inventory-tracking overlay (§ 44:90:11:01, :04): every transfer must be reflected in the daily transfer record (by midnight each day of operation; same units as the inventory record; reflects all manifests; secure; cardholders identified only by ID number). Reconciliation of on-premises and in-transit inventory each day by midnight; written explanations for destructions and weight adjustments; correct batch numbers, cultivation-room identification, departure/transfer status, test results, and product categories; package tags kept with product until sold, then shredded (§ 44:90:11:01(7)–(16)).
  • Patient delivery: no patient-delivery right was located (Run 1, secondary only). The transport rules above are establishment-to-establishment; a dispensary sale must occur at the dispensary (§ 44:90:08:04, see §8). Do not describe a lawful patient-delivery service under ARSD 44:90.

4. DISPENSARY OPERATIONS (ARSD 44:90:08; SDCL 34-20G-67/-68/-70/-71)

  • Sales only at the dispensary, on camera: "No cannabis or cannabis product sale may take place at any location other than at a certified medical cannabis dispensary. All sales must take place at a certified medical cannabis dispensary in clear view of security cameras." (§ 44:90:08:04, 1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly.)
  • Access/age verification: §§ 44:90:08:01–:02 (preventing unauthorised access; age verification, including website/mobile application). Operative detail not extracted — Verify, except that under-21 cardholders may not smoke/vape (SDCL 34-20G-18(3)(c), Run 1) and inhalable-product possession by under-21s is labelled illegal (§ 44:90:10:12.01(3)).
  • Staff training before cardholder contact (§ 44:90:08:03, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; search-result 30 Oct 2026): dispensary employees must be trained, before interacting with any cardholder, to (1) determine authenticity of registry cards; (2) verify the presenter is the authorised cardholder with valid photo ID; (3) use the verification system by phone, point-of-sale software and mobile application; (4) track amounts dispensed for a patient and consolidate sales to the patient and the patient's designated caregiver (i.e. the 14-day limit is patient-level, not per-buyer); plus further subsections not extracted — Verify (5) and any later subsections.
  • Dispensing limits: 3 oz flower-equivalent per 14 days rolling (Run 1, DOH bulletin 16 Nov 2023), counted via the § 44:90:02:16 equivalent-weight table (§1 C2). Dispensary inventory records must include product type, testing batch identifier, number of marketing layers, and quantity per marketing layer (§ 44:90:11:09, search-result 30 Oct 2026).
  • Prohibited at dispensaries: consumption on dispensary property (SDCL 34-20G-68); sharing office space with, or referral to/from, a practitioner (SDCL 34-20G-67); sales to non-cardholders (SDCL 34-20G-70 framework, Run 1). See also colocation bars in §7 (no ethanol/hazardous/compressed-gas extraction and no pesticide application in the same structure as a dispensary).

5. CULTIVATION (ARSD 44:90:05; SDCL 34-20G-72(4)(e))

  • Indoor and outdoor both contemplated: SDCL 34-20G-72(4)(e) directs rules for "both the indoor and outdoor cultivation of cannabis by a cultivation facility". No rule text was located that bans outdoor cultivation statewide; local bans (e.g. Pennington County, secondary, Run 1 Part 3) operate locally. Verify any ARSD outdoor-specific cultivation section — the chapter 44:90:05 section list was not fully extracted.
  • Facility scale: no statewide plant/canopy cap (see §1 C5). Control is by (a) approved operating procedures and site plan (§ 44:90:03:06, additional cultivation-procedure requirements — full text Verify); (b) Metrc plant-level tracking: each immature/mature plant has a unique identifier printed on a tag/label on the growing container or a tracking-system plant tag around the stalk; a plant tag is required once a plant is over 12 inches in height; the record must be updated when a seedling exceeds its size limit and becomes a plant, when a plant first flowers, when trimmed/manured/harvested, when a testing batch is created, and when packaged for retail sale; a testing-batch record must show every plant used (§ 44:90:11:06, 1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly). Definitions: "immature plant" = nonflowering plant ≥12 inches from base of main stalk to most distant leaf/branch point; "seedling" = nonflowering plant/rooted cutting <12 inches; "mature plant" = a plant that has flowered (§ 44:90:01:01).
  • Pesticides/chemicals: only DOH-approved active ingredients (§ 44:90:05:10, see §2.2); cultivation application checklist (DOH, 2025, https://doh.sd.gov/media/ddpjvqog/2025-cultivation-checklist.pdf, search-result 30 Oct 2026) requires listing every chemical/active ingredient present or used (insecticides, fungicides, fertilisers, essential oils) and describing application method (§§ 44:90:03:06(7), 44:90:05:08–:09 — full text Verify), scales with NTEP certificate of conformance and DPS certification once every 2 years (§ 44:90:04:25; SDCL ch. 37-21), and NRTL approval listing for greenhouses/equipment where applicable.
  • Seed supply: SDCL 34-20G-12 (sale/donation of seeds to a cultivation facility) is the statutory route; its operating detail is Part 7/licensing material and was not re-extracted here — Verify before stating seed-source rules.
  • Workplace safety: operating procedures must include a workplace safety plan consistent with 29 CFR 1910.23, 1910.123 and 1200 (PPE, hazard assessment, safe equipment operation, proper application of agricultural chemicals, ladder use, hazard communication) (§ 44:90:03:05(4), Cornell search-result 30 Oct 2026). Heavy-equipment operation training: § 44:90:04:15 (title only — Verify content).

6. SECURITY, SURVEILLANCE, ACCESS, COLOCATION (ARSD 44:90:04)

  • Operating-procedure security plan (§ 44:90:03:05(6), Cornell search-result 30 Oct 2026): must indicate all doors, windows, gates, exterior lights, alarm sensors and cameras, and describe how alarms and cameras will be monitored; plus (7) additional patron/community safety steps; (8) diversion-prevention plans; (12) processes limiting unauthorised access, including identity verification for vendors/contractors, visitor badges, and close monitoring of visitors.
  • Lighting (§ 44:90:04:05): DOH 2025 cultivation checklist (search-result 30 Oct 2026) states lighting must be sufficient for observers to see and cameras to record activity within 10 feet of an entry, and outdoor lighting must be indicated on the site plan. Full rule text Verify.
  • Doors/windows (§ 44:90:04:06); alarm sensors on all exterior doors, windows and gates (§ 44:90:04:10(1), per 2025 checklist). Full text of §§ 44:90:04:05–:06, :10 Verify beyond the checklist summaries.
  • Cameras (§ 44:90:04:07, per 2025 checklist and Jan 2026 compilation contents): permanently fixed cameras (1) at each exterior door and gate to identify persons entering/exiting; (2) at each door separating non-public dispensary areas from sales areas, to identify persons entering/exiting; (3) viewing in their entirety any area where cannabis/products/waste are cultivated, stored, destroyed, or prepared for transfer, sale or testing. Recording/access/storage: §§ 44:90:04:08–:09 (titles: "Recording by security cameras — Access by department"; "Storage of camera footage"). The footage-retention period was NOT extracted in this research — do NOT state 30, 60 or 90 days. Verify §§ 44:90:04:08–:09 in the Jan 2026 compilation. Destruction must be camera-captured (§ 44:90:09:12, see §2.5) and dispensary sales must be on camera (§ 44:90:08:04).
  • Badges and access (§§ 44:90:04:11–:14): the establishment must provide each agent an identification badge (photograph; first/last name and establishment name in plain black font, size per the 2023 text — the 2021 text states not less than 24-point, the 2023 marked-up text shows a reduction, Verify the in-force point size) and the agent must display it whenever on the premises or transporting cannabis/products. Controlled access with identity verification (§ 44:90:04:13); visitor badges for contractors performing work (§ 44:90:04:14). Historic badge fee $50 (2021, Run 1) — Verify current fee; the fee is not in the sections read here.
  • Duties to report: change in management — updated management plan to the department within 7 days of any change (§ 44:90:04:01, 2021 draft text via search-result 30 Oct 2026 — Verify against the adopted text); criminal activity — report to department and law enforcement (§ 44:90:04:03, title only — Verify deadline/content); corrective and preventive action — written procedures required (§ 44:90:04:02, title only — Verify content).
  • Colocation (§ 44:90:04:04, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-04-04, search-result 30 Oct 2026): an establishment must have separate means of ingress and egress from any other establishment, except multiple establishments under common ownership may be colocated if (1) lockable, alarmed doors separate activities under different licences; (2) the door separating a dispensary from cultivation/manufacturing remains locked when cardholders are present, with signs stating entry is limited to employees/authorised persons; (3) the local government allows the colocated types; and (4) none of the following occurs: (a) a testing facility in the same structure as any other establishment; (b) extraction using ethanol, inherently hazardous substances, or compressed gas in the same structure as a dispensary; (c) pesticides applied in the same structure as a dispensary. Shared access from a parking lot, walkway, concourse or other area generally open to the public as part of a shopping centre/business park is not prohibited by this section if the local government allows it. DOH inspection reports treat § 44:90:04:04 as a separately scored item (e.g. Dakota Natural Solutions, manufacturing, Wessington, 6 Aug 2025, scored OUT on ingress/egress, lockable doors/alarms, locked doors and signage — https://doh.sd.gov/media/rynhjm15/20250806_dakotanaturalsolutions_m_wessington.pdf, search-result 30 Oct 2026; an OUT finding is an inspection finding, not by itself a suspension/revocation — see §10).
  • Storage (§ 44:90:04:24): health and safety standards for storage; scored on every DOH inspection report ("Product Storage 44:90:04:24"). Full text Verify — do not state temperature, segregation or vault rules beyond the title and the waste-storage fencing point in §7.

7. WASTE, ENVIRONMENT, ODOR, WATER, ENERGY

  • Waste-management plan — mandatory operating-procedure content (§ 44:90:03:05(9), Cornell search-result 30 Oct 2026): (a) how cannabis waste will be rendered unrecognizable and unfit for use by grinding and mixing the waste with at least 50 percent other waste, including soil, sawdust, grease, food waste, yard waste, or shredded paper; (b) how it will be composted, if applicable; (c) how it will be hauled from the premises. The 2025 checklist adds: specify how waste will be protected from diversion and what hauler (if any) or composting method is used. Definition: "cannabis waste" = flower/trim, seeds, products, byproducts containing cannabis, or plants, excluding stalks without trichomes and root balls, that have been designated for destruction (§ 44:90:01:01).
  • Outdoor waste storage fencing: 2025 cultivation checklist (search-result 30 Oct 2026): "If waste is to be stored outdoors, fences & gates must be secured, at least 6 feet high, and obscured from public view" (§ 44:90:04:26); "If site plan includes outdoor cultivation or waste, fences & gates must be secured and indicate alarm sensors" (§§ 44:90:04:26, 44:90:04:10(1)). The DOH 2025 Oversight Committee letter (https://mylrc.sdlegislature.gov/api/Documents/285696.pdf, search-result 30 Oct 2026) states a 2025 rule package "expanded the requirement for fences and gates around waste areas to all establishments" and "updated waste management requirements to prevent diversion" — the rule section (presumably § 44:90:04:26) and its effective date are Verify against the Jan 2026 compilation; do not cite the letter as the rule.
  • Destruction on camera / in Metrc: see §§2.5, 3 (written explanation for any destruction in the inventory tracking system, § 44:90:11:01(13)).
  • Disaster-damaged product: the same 2025 Oversight letter states a rule was added "to address product damaged by natural or manmade disaster" — section and procedure Verify / Unavailable in the sections read here.
  • Wastewater plan — mandatory operating-procedure content (§ 44:90:03:05(10)): (a) for public-wastewater connection, a pretreatment industrial-use permit or a DANR determination that no such permit is necessary; or (b) for an on-site system, the applicant's certification of compliance with ARSD ch. 74:53:01. No cannabis-specific effluent limit was located — Verify whether DANR imposes one case-by-case.
  • Water, energy, odor — no SD-specific cannabis rule located: no ARSD 44:90 section was found imposing a water-use cap, an energy-conservation plan, a lighting-energy standard, or an odor-control/abatement standard for medical cannabis establishments. Odor control, where it exists, is local (e.g. Mitchell's reported property-line odor rule, Run 1 Part 3, secondary) and/or general nuisance/zoning law. Do not import another state's energy/odor rules. General environmental law (Clean Water Act, Clean Air Act, RCRA, SDCL Title 34A, DANR permitting) applies as it would to any comparable facility, but no cannabis-specific environmental permit was located — Verify with DANR for a specific site type (especially solvent handling and wastewater pretreatment).
  • Hemp waste/remediation (for contrast, not medical rules): see §11.

8. MANUFACTURING, PACKAGING, LABELLING, ADVERTISING, PROMOTIONS

8.1 Manufacturing (ARSD 44:90:07)

  • Only these product categories may be manufactured (§ 44:90:07:04(5)): (a) vaporizer pens/cartridges (products); (b) concentrated cannabis; (c) cannabis tinctures, oils, and capsules containing oil; (d) cannabis beverages; (e) other edible cannabis products; (f) topical cannabis products for external use.
  • Prohibited (§ 44:90:07:04, 1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly): (1) products in the distinct shape of a human, animal, creature, vehicle, fruit, cartoon character, toy, emoji, or other artwork likely/intended to appeal to anyone under 21; (2) adding/infusing cannabis into a commercially available noncannabis end product; (3) edibles (except tincture/oil/capsule) over 50 mg THC per serving (amended from 10 mg); (4) marketing-layer packaging (except tincture/oil/capsule containing oil) over 500 mg total THC (amended from 100 mg); (6) products for ophthalmic, otic, rectal or vaginal administration; (7) inhalation products using/containing polyethylene glycol, vitamin E acetate, or medium-chain triglyceride oil; (8) products from cannabis/concentrate that has not passed required testing; (9) products for nonhuman consumption; (10) non-cannabis products on the same premises as cannabis products; (11) extraction using pressurised canned flammable fuel, handheld torches, refillable lighters or similar consumer products.
  • Additives ban (§ 44:90:07:10, 1 Nov 2023 text): only unadulterated cannabis/concentrates may be used; no products containing (1) botanical, synthetic or artificial terpenes, or (2) artificial, synthetic or hemp-based cannabinoids, including delta-8 THC. "Synthetic" = formulated/manufactured by a chemical process or a process chemically changing a substance extracted from naturally occurring sources. This manufacturing ban is consistent with, and narrower than, the statewide conversion/sale ban in SDCL 34-20B-118 (Run 1 Part 1): a medical manufacturer cannot lawfully add delta-8 even if it were otherwise available.
  • Extraction methods (§ 44:90:03:08, Jan 2026 compilation search-result 30 Oct 2026; §§ 44:90:07:05–:08): operating procedures must describe in detail: (a) mechanical extraction using potable water, ice, dry screening/sieving, cryonic extraction, pressure or temperature; (b) infusion into propylene glycol, glycerin or food-grade fats; (c) extraction using food-grade ethanol; (d) extraction using an inherently hazardous substance. Also required: a diagram of areas where any solvent/chemical/potentially hazardous substance (excluding water) will be stored, and ventilation/filtration plans reducing fire risk (full text Verify).
  • Inherently hazardous extraction (§ 44:90:07:08, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; 49 SDR 47 eff. 22 Nov 2022; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-07-08, search-result 30 Oct 2026): before performing it, the establishment must have prior physical inspection and written approval by an engineer licensed under SDCL ch. 36-18A that storage, preparation, electrical, gas-monitoring, fire-suppression and exhaust systems are adequate for the methods/substances used. The method must be listed in the operating procedures on file, the substance must be ≥99% pure, and the resulting extract must not exceed department residual limits. Approved solvents: butane, propane, acetone, heptane, pentane; any other inherently hazardous substance requires written department approval on documentation of safety/efficacy. Flammable gas storage/handling per local ordinance, SDCL ch. 11-10, ARSD art. 61:15 and ch. 20:44:22. A 2021 draft text also required documentation of a professional-grade closed-loop system recovering solvents; whether that closed-loop documentation requirement survived into the adopted §§ 44:90:07:05–:07 is Verify — do not state it as adopted law on draft text alone.
  • Generally safe vs potentially hazardous methods (§§ 44:90:07:06–:07): titles only in the materials read — Verify which methods fall in each class beyond the § 44:90:03:08 list above.
  • Work environment / nonusable product (§§ 44:90:07:01–:03, :09 edible products): section titles only in the materials read, except § 44:90:07:03 solvent/nonusable cross-reference in §2.2 — Verify full manufacturing-practice, work-environment and edible-production standards (e.g. any kitchen/food-handling standard) before stating them. Food-service licence: still Verify (see §1 C9). No ARSD food-service requirement was located; the Aug 2026 secondary claim (Run 1) remains unconfirmed.
  • Definitions that matter for manufacturing: "cannabis beverage" = a liquid edible product with <1 mg delta-9 THC per ounce of liquid; "tincture" = a liquid edible product with >1 mg THC per ounce in ethanol, propylene glycol, glycerin or food-safe oil; "cannabis oil" = an edible product using a food-safe oil as the primary noncannabis ingredient with no added flavours, colours or scents (§ 44:90:01:01, 1 Nov 2023 text). Note the beverage/tincture line is concentration-based (1 mg/oz), not serving-based — do not conflate it with the § 44:90:10:05 package limits (§1 C3).

8.2 Packaging (ARSD 44:90:10:01–:06)

  • General retail packaging (§ 44:90:10:01, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; 1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly): a dispensary must transfer cannabis/products to the patient/caregiver in packaging that is (1) child-resistant per 16 CFR 1700.15; (2) tamper-evident, using a sealing method giving clear, lasting evidence of prior opening; (3) resealable, except single-serving products; (4) opaque, not allowing the product to be seen without opening. Unless otherwise specified, each requirement may be met by the cultivation/manufacturing container or by dispensary exit packaging at sale. This confirms Run 1's "child-resistant, tamper-proof, resealable, opaque" summary, with "tamper-evident" as the rule's word (Run 1's "tamper-proof" is a paraphrase — use "tamper-evident" in Run 2).
  • Product-specific packaging (§§ 44:90:10:02–:06): flower (:02), edibles (:03), tinctures/edible oils (:04), beverages (:05), topicals (:06). Only :05 was read in full (2021 Cornell text, see §1 C3). :02–:04 and :06 full text Verify in the Jan 2026 compilation. In particular, do not state an edible package/serving rule from :03 without that check — the manufacturing limits in § 44:90:07:04 are the confirmed limits; the packaging sections may restate or differ from them.
  • Expiration / use-by dates: § 44:90:10:12(3)(c) (1 Nov 2023 text): an edible label must include a product expiration date (after which no longer fit for consumption) or a use-by date (after which no longer optimally fresh); once affixed, an establishment may not alter the date or affix a new label with a later date. The 2025 Oversight letter states packaging/expiration requirements were updated in the 2025 package — Verify whether § 44:90:10:12 was further amended after Nov 2023.

8.3 Labelling (ARSD 44:90:10:07–:13)

  • General (§§ 44:90:10:07–:08): required labelling or establishment-printed exit labels; clear, ≥6-point font, nothing required covered/obstructed (Run 1 summary; full text Verify).
  • Test results (§ 44:90:10:09, 48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-10-09, search-result 30 Oct 2026): "The results of any testing mandated by the department must be included on the label of any cannabis or cannabis product. The label must state the THC content in milligrams of total THC and as a percentage of the product's weight. No label may contain claims regarding cannabidiol content or the absence of microbials, metals, solvents, or pesticides except to list the results of analytical tests performed by a registered cannabis testing facility."
  • Onset/duration, ingredients, allergens (§§ 44:90:10:10–:11): onset time and duration of effects + effects warning (:10, full text Verify); ingredients/allergens (:11, https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/ARSD-44-90-10-11, search-result 30 Oct 2026): "The label of any cannabis or cannabis product must identify any pesticides used in cultivation. The label of any cannabis product must list all ingredients and, if applicable, gases, solvents, or other chemicals used in extraction. The label of any edible cannabis product must identify any major allergens contained in the product in accordance with 21 U.S.C. § 321(qq), including milk, eggs, fish, crustacean shellfish, tree nuts, peanuts, wheat, and soybeans."
  • Net contents / equivalent weight / servings (§ 44:90:10:12, 1 Nov 2023 text, opened directly): net weight/volume in both ounces+grams/mg (solids) or fluid ounces+millilitres (liquids/colloids); equivalent cannabis weight per § 44:90:02:16; for edibles (except tinctures/oils/capsules) serving size (see §1 C4 tension), servings per marketing layer, nutrition panel (21 CFR 101.9), expiration/use-by date; for tinctures/oils/capsules, dosage size (mL, drops, capsules) with THC mg per dosage; for vaporizer/topical products, weight of concentrated cannabis used (mg/g).
  • Standard symbol and warnings (§ 44:90:10:12.01, 48 SDR 54 eff. 15 Nov 2021; Cornell search-result 30 Oct 2026): DOH-designed standard symbol indicating cannabis/cannabis extract, on the front/most predominantly displayed area of an edible's marketing layer, no smaller than ½ inch × ½ inch; warnings in no smaller than 6-point font: (1) for all cannabis/products: (a) "Contains cannabis. For medical use by qualifying patients only. There may be health risks associated with the use of this product. There may be additional health risks associated with the use of this product for women who are pregnant, breastfeeding, or planning on becoming pregnant. Do not drive a motor vehicle or operate heavy machinery while using this product."; (b) "Cannabis has a high potential for abuse. This product has not been approved by the United States Food and Drug Administration for preventing or treating any condition or disease process."; (2) for flower/trim including pre-rolls: "Not for retail sale to persons under 21 years of age."; (3) for inhalables: "Possession by persons under 21 years old is illegal."
  • Schedule III interaction with warnings — Verify, do not rewrite: the (1)(b) warning ("high potential for abuse", "not FDA-approved") was written when marijuana was Schedule I federally. The given baseline moves state-licensed medical marijuana to Schedule III (28 Apr 2026) but does not make it FDA-approved and does not reschedule marijuana generally. No DOH revision of § 44:90:10:12.01 was located at 30 Oct 2026. Run 2 must state the warning as written and flag the Schedule III wording question as Verify; it must not present the warning as repealed or as proof of current federal scheduling.

8.4 Advertising (ARSD 44:90:10:14–:19) and Schedule III interaction

  • Prohibited forms (§ 44:90:10:14.01, 48 SDR 54 eff. 15 Nov 2021; https://www.law.cornell.edu/regulations/south-dakota/SDCL-44-90-10-14.01, search-result 30 Oct 2026), verbatim trigger and list:

"Unless and until the United States Drug Enforcement Administration removes marijuana or cannabis as a Schedule I controlled substance, no establishment may advertise: (1) On a sign or billboard, except that a dispensary may advertise on signs on its own premises; (2) By distributing handbills in public areas or on publicly owned property; (3) Through direct mail, phone, text, or email without verifying the recipient is a cardholder or medical cannabis establishment and offering a permanent opt-out feature; (4) Through publication, except that a dispensary may advertise on publications within its own premises; (5) Through radio, television, and other media; or (6) Through a practitioner or health care facility, by placing advertising material at a practitioner's office or health care facility, or by targeting the practitioner's or health care facility's patients through direct mail, phone, text, or email."

  • Schedule III analysis — the trigger is NOT clearly met: the rule's trigger is DEA removal of "marijuana or cannabis as a Schedule I controlled substance". The given baseline is a split schedule: state-licensed medical marijuana (and FDA-approved products) to Schedule III, adult-use marijuana remaining Schedule I. Marijuana/cannabis as a general category has therefore not been removed from Schedule I. On a literal reading the prohibition remains in force for establishments; on a purposive reading, a DOH could argue the medical programme's Schedule III status lifts it for medical advertising. No DOH guidance, rule amendment or AG opinion resolving this was located at 30 Oct 2026 — Verify and state both readings; do NOT state that advertising is now free, and do NOT state that the ban is definitively still in force without the DOH position. The 2025 Oversight letter states the 2025 package "clarified [the] rule around advertising" — section and content Verify.
  • Other advertising rules: a secondary advertising summary (mediajel.com, search-result 30 Oct 2026 — page mixes North Dakota rules, use with care) states South Dakota ads must contain "For medical use by qualifying patients only" and the establishment identification number, must avoid deceptive/misleading statements and unverified potency claims, must not depict consumption, pregnancy/breastfeeding, or vehicle/machinery operation, and website/social/mobile ads must verify the recipient is not an age-restricted cardholder and offer a permanent opt-out. All of that is secondary/Verify — the operative §§ 44:90:10:14–:19 text beyond :14.01 was not extracted. Do not quote it as rule text.
  • Sponsored social media: Run 1 notes a DOH "MC Minute" (June 2023) flagging sponsored social-media ads under §§ 44:90:10:16–:18 — Verify current text; no social-media rule is stated here on that basis alone.

8.5 Promotions and discounts

  • No discount/promotion rule was located in the ARSD sections read. No section was found banning or expressly allowing BOGO, loyalty, first-patient, veteran, low-income or bulk discounts, free samples, or giveaways. Market evidence (Run 1 Part 6, secondary listings) shows dispensary promotions exist (e.g. "$100 ounce" deals), but existence in listings is not a rule permitting them. Verify §§ 44:90:10:16–:19 and any 2025/2026 advertising amendment for a promotions rule before Run 2 states discounts are allowed or banned. Related constraints that do apply regardless: sales only to cardholders, only at the dispensary on camera, within the 14-day limit, in compliant packaging/labelling, and without prohibited advertising forms (§8.4) — a promotion communicated through a prohibited channel would still be prohibited advertising even if the discount itself is not separately banned.

9. STAFF, AGENTS, BACKGROUND CHECKS, BADGES, TRAINING

  • Who is an agent: "an owner, officer, board member, employee, or volunteer at a medical cannabis establishment" (SDCL 34-20G-1(18), Run 1).
  • Background checks: SDCL 34-20G-61 (criminal background check of establishment officers, employees and volunteers) and § 34-20G-62 (employment restrictions). Operating procedures must include pre-employment screening procedures including criminal background checks (§ 44:90:03:05(11)). Disqualifying felony = a violent crime classified as a felony (SDCL 34-20G-1(11), Run 1); principal officers/board members must be 21+, at least one SD resident, and must not have served for a revoked establishment (SDCL 34-20G-55, Run 1). The detailed background-check procedure (fingerprinting, DCI/FBI, renewal re-checks) was not extracted — Verify ARSD 44:90:03 and DOH checklists.
  • Badges: see §6 (provide + display; transport agents must wear badges, § 44:90:04:22). Badge fee historic $50 (2021) — Verify current.
  • Training (by subject, all required before the relevant work): inventory-tracking-system use (§ 44:90:04:16); security protocols (§ 44:90:04:17); heavy/agricultural/industrial equipment operation (§ 44:90:04:15); dispensary unauthorised-sales prevention (§ 44:90:08:03, see §4); sample-collection designee training (§ 44:90:09:07 and § 44:90:06:03 SOPs); workplace safety (§ 44:90:03:05(4)). Training content/duration/records for each subject beyond the titles and the § 44:90:08:03 list were not extracted — Verify. DOH inspection reports check "Training Certificates on site?" and score inventory-tracking training and unauthorised-sales training as separate items (e.g. Black Hills Weed Shop inspection, 13 June 2025, https://doh.sd.gov/media/ahgnzcdz/20250613_blackhillsweedshop_d_rapidcity.pdf, search-result 30 Oct 2026).

10. INSPECTIONS, ENFORCEMENT, FINES, SUSPENSION/REVOCATION, PUFFY'S IMPLICATIONS

  • Inspection authority: SDCL 34-20G-69 (inspection); SDCL 34-20G-63 (oversight/record-keeping procedures). DOH publishes individual establishment inspection reports as PDFs (Run 1 Part 5): each item scored IN (in compliance), OUT (out of compliance), COS (corrected on site), N/O (not observed), N/A (not applicable), by ARSD section (lighting, doors/windows, storage, scales, colocation, transport manifests, inventory tracking, training, age verification, testing-batch/COA retention, cultivation/manufacturing standards). SFY 2024: 74 on-site inspections (64 DOH, 10 DPS) — Run 1 Part 5, SFY 2024 annual report opened directly. An OUT/COS finding is an inspection finding, not by itself a fine, suspension or revocation.
  • Statutory fine/probation/suspension/termination power: SDCL 34-20G-72(6) (2024 SB 43, SL 2024 ch. 138): rules for "(a) The imposition of fines, not to exceed ten thousand dollars per inspection, on a medical cannabis establishment that is found to have committed multiple or serious violations of this chapter; and (b) The probation, suspension, and termination of the registration certificate of a medical cannabis establishment that commits multiple or serious violations". Source: https://mylrc.sdlegislature.gov/api/Documents/SessionLaws/266886.pdf (2024 SB 43 session law, search-result 30 Oct 2026). SDCL 34-20G-72(5) separately requires suspension/termination procedures for certificates/cards of establishments/cardholders committing multiple or serious violations; SDCL 34-20G-80/81 (suspension/revocation, notice/hearing/duration — Run 1 Part 4) were not opened in full here — Verify grounds/timelines/hearing rights before advising on enforcement risk.
  • Rule fine categories (in force at 30 Oct 2026, per secondary reporting of ARSD 44:90:12:10 — Verify against the Jan 2026 compilation): Category 1: $5,000 first offence to $10,000 third/subsequent; Category 2: $3,000–$5,000; Category 3: $1,500–$2,500; Category 4: $1,000; Category 5: $500. Total fines per inspection capped at $10,000 (§ 44:90:12:10, per WeedPress 27 Oct 2026, https://weedpress.org/2026/10/27/south-dakota-revised-medical-cannabis-rules-take-effect-nov-5-44-90/, search-result 30 Oct 2026). Category 4 is described as failures to follow required operating procedures; Category 5 as badge/visitor/scale problems (Marijuana Herald summary, same reporting). The category-to-violation mapping was not verified against the rule text — Verify § 44:90:12 in full.
  • Pending change — NOT in force at 30 Oct 2026: under the 5 Nov 2026 amendments (see §0), Category 4/5 first offences would require a department-approved corrective action plan instead of an immediate fine ($1,000 / $500 from the second offence), with DOH verification by inspection/record review; Category 1–3 fines unchanged. The WeedPress analysis notes two proposed § 44:90:12:03 penalty sentences do not appear in DOH's 7 Oct 2026 "latest version" PDF, so even the pending text is uncertain. Do not apply the corrective-action-plan-first regime to the 30 Oct 2026 position.
  • Cardholder enforcement (for contrast): ARSD 44:90:12:08 — the department may, under SDCL ch. 1-26 and § 34-20G-84, revoke a registry card for serious/multiple violations including transferring cannabis to an unauthorised person, submitting false information, false statements to law enforcement, unauthorised card use, accepting remuneration beyond direct costs, or cultivating in violation of ch. 34-20G (48 SDR 40 eff. 5 Oct 2021; https://sdlegislature.gov/api/Rules/Archived/11524.pdf, search-result 30 Oct 2026).
  • Puffy's operational implications: (1) scoring ties go to a lottery and a ranked waiting list (§ 44:90:03:16, see §1 C6); (2) a waitlist applicant's fee status is not settled by the pre-Puffy's rules — budget a waitlist award as Verify on fees; (3) the one-year operational deadline is real and voiding, with only one extension — an awarded but non-operational certificate is not a bankable asset; (4) local licence disputes (Rapid City/Puffy's building-permit and forfeiture litigation, Run 1 Part 3) show state and local tracks can diverge — an operator needs both tracks clean, not just the state certificate. The Puffy's opinion itself (2025 S.D. 10, 19 Feb 2025) was cited via search-result in Run 1; its full reasoning on the one-year rule and fees is Verify before Run 2 quotes it beyond the holdings stated here.
  • Local enforcement is additional: local civil penalties under SDCL 34-20G-58 (Run 1 Part 3) and local licence revocation (e.g. Vermillion Code § 123.19: revocation for noncompliance, $500/day unlicensed operation, reinstatement fees $10,000 first / $15,000 second, automatic termination on third — city code, search-result 30 Oct 2026, https://codelibrary.amlegal.com/codes/vermillion/latest/vermillion_sd/0-0-0-22790) sit alongside state fines/suspension. Do not present a state fine as the only enforcement exposure.

11. RECORD-KEEPING AND RETENTION (ARSD 44:90:11)

  • System: the DOH-prescribed inventory tracking system is Metrc (Run 1 Part 1 §1.8; SFY 2024 report: contract Oct 2021, live Aug 2022). ARSD 44:90:11:01 requires establishments to use the prescribed system for all inventory, transfer, testing-sample and transaction records, and states that omissions/misinformation in the system are violations of the article and ch. 34-20G. The rule's "absence of a live system does not excuse §§ 44:90:11:02–:13" language is historical (pre-Aug 2022); Metrc is live.
  • Daily records, all by midnight each day of operation: inventory (§ 44:90:11:03, units in §3/§5 above; reflects destruction/waste disposal, theft/loss, transfer-record data; secure; cardholders by ID number only); transfer (§ 44:90:11:04, same units, reflects all manifests, secure, cardholders by ID number only); testing sample (§ 44:90:11:05: batch ID/quantity, sample ID/quantity/batch ID, tests to be performed, results including whether the lab indicated the batch safe/unsafe for transfer — search-result 30 Oct 2026, https://sdlegislature.gov/api/Rules/Archived/11515.pdf); transaction records (dispensary sales — § 44:90:01:01 definition; section number Verify). Testing-facility inventory records (§ 44:90:11:08): all samples in possession (IDs/quantities), other cannabis/extracts/products acquired for training, quantities rendered unusable by testing, returned, destroyed, and lost/stolen/unaccounted for, updated by midnight each day of operation.
  • Retention period — Verify: no retention period (years) for inventory/transfer/testing/transaction records, COAs, manifests, or camera footage was extracted in this research. Do not state "retain for X years" for any record type. The sections to check are §§ 44:90:09:04 (COA retention), 44:90:11:02–:13 (recordkeeping generally), and 44:90:04:08–:09 (footage storage) in the Jan 2026 compilation.
  • Scales: any scale used in cultivating, manufacturing, testing or dispensing must be certified under SDCL ch. 37-21 (§ 44:90:04:25, 1 Nov 2023 text); the 2025 checklist adds NTEP certificate of conformance and DPS certification once every 2 years.
  • Metrc–registry integration: Run 1 records a secondary claim that South Dakota "expressly prohibited integration with the state patient registry, so purchase limits are not tracked in any state system" and the point of sale must enforce limits internally. That claim was not confirmed against ARSD 44:90:11 or DOH guidance in this research — Verify; the dispensing-tracking duty in § 44:90:08:03(4) (consolidate patient + caregiver amounts) is confirmed, but the system-architecture claim is not.

12. UNIVERSITY AND OTHER RESEARCH

  • No medical-cannabis research licence or university research programme was found in SDCL ch. 34-20G or ARSD art. 44:90. There is no fifth "research establishment" type (Run 1 Part 4: four types only). A university cannot become a medical establishment by virtue of being a university; it would need a cultivation/manufacturing/testing/dispensary certificate like any applicant, subject to local rules — and no such university-held certificate was located.
  • Regents marijuana-free policy (secondary, dated): South Dakota's public universities "won't allow medical marijuana use on campuses or at university-sponsored events" under a Board of Regents decision (June 2021), because of federal drug-free schools/workplace law and federal funding risk; students may hold cards but may not possess/use on Regents property (University Business, updated 24 Oct 2026, search-result 30 Oct 2026, https://universitybusiness.com/sd-universities-will-stay-marijuana-free-regardless-of-medical-marijuana-program/). Verify against the current Board of Regents policy — the article is secondary and the policy may have been restated since 2021. Its interaction with Schedule III medical status after 28 Apr 2026 is Verify; the article's Oct 2026 update date does not by itself show a post-Schedule III policy change.
  • SDSU — what was actually found (all secondary, none a cultivation/research licence): (a) SDSU Agricultural Heritage Museum (part of SDSU, Brookings) + South Dakota Industrial Hemp Association hosted "The Future of Hemp" public session, 6 Feb 2025, on hemp vs marijuana, cultivation and products (SD Ag Connection, 27 Jan 2025, search-result 30 Oct 2026); (b) an SDSU School of American and Global Studies poll of 747 registered voters on adult-use legalisation (49% favour / 41% oppose / 10% undecided, reported in a 2024 secondary article) — polling, not cannabis research; (c) a secondary "State Regs Today" page claiming SDSU offers a minor in Medicinal Plant Chemistry with a cannabis-science focus and USD offers a graduate certificate in Cannabis Science and Therapeutics — unverified, low-quality source; Verify against SDSU/USD catalogues before stating any degree/minor exists. Do not state that SDSU (or USD, or any SD university) cultivates medical cannabis, holds a testing licence, or runs a medical-cannabis research programme on the sources above.
  • Hemp research (separate track): a hemp research licence existed under the pre-2027 state programme (SDCL 38-35-3.1, repealed by 2026 SB 39 eff. 1 Jan 2027, Run 1 Part 5). After 1 Jan 2027, hemp research cultivation falls under USDA/tribal licensing like any hemp cultivation. No SDSU hemp research licence or plot was confirmed in this research — Unavailable.
  • Federal research route (context only, not SD law): after the baseline Schedule III move, state-licensed medical activity is Schedule III federally, but university research with marijuana remains subject to federal DEA registration and funding rules; no SD rule was located that creates a state research pathway. Verify with DEA/NIH guidance for any specific university protocol — outside this Part's SD-law scope.

13. HEMP OPERATIONS (CONTRAST — DANR/USDA RULES, NOT ARSD 44:90)

Part 11's commission asks for operations rules; hemp operations are governed separately and must not be conflated with medical rules: - Pre-2027 state programme (ARSD art. 12:82; DANR programme page, https://danr.sd.gov/Agriculture/Inspection/IndustrialHemp/default.aspx, search-result 30 Oct 2026): planting verification inspection (GPS, acreage/square feet, FSA farm/tract/field number); pre-harvest inspection/sampling of each lot not more than 30 days before harvest (ARSD 12:82:06:03, 75 SDR eff. 28 Dec 2020; 49 SDR 47 eff. 22 Nov 2022); harvest only after sample collection; no commingling of a harvested lot with another lot/material except with documented prior written DANR permission; no hemp may leave the grower's dominion/control until a DANR laboratory result confirms the lot meets the acceptable hemp THC level; grower or key participant must be present at inspection; grower inspection/sample fee $250 per lot (DANR page). Noncompliant plants must be remediated or disposed of under DANR/DPS oversight per USDA/DANR guidelines; disposal required if post-remediation testing fails; disposal must chemically/mechanically destroy the material and render it non-retrievable, non-ingestible and unfit for commerce under USDA-approved guidelines. - Federal overlay (given baseline + USDA FAQ, https://www.ams.usda.gov/rules-regulations/hemp/frequently-asked-questions-faq, search-result 30 Oct 2026): sampling by an authorised agent ≤30 days before anticipated harvest; testing by post-decarboxylation or similarly reliable method for total THC = delta-9 THC + (THCA × 0.877) on a dry-weight basis, with measurement uncertainty reported. The given baseline delays USDA enforcement of DEA-registered-lab testing to 31 December 2026 — this supersedes older secondary statements of a 31 Dec 2025 deadline. After 11 Dec 2026 (given baseline), the federal total-THC definition and 0.4 mg/container cap apply; how DANR/USDA will apply the cap to South Dakota hemp products in the 2027 season is Verify (see Run 1 Part 1 §1.6). - Post-1 Jan 2027: SB 39 repeals the state grower/processor/research licences; cultivation licensing moves to USDA (or tribal) programmes (Run 1 Part 5). DANR's programme page still describing the state system at 30 Oct 2026 is therefore stale for 2027 planning — re-check DANR/USDA pages after 1 Jan 2027. Hemp product operations (finished-product testing/labelling, retail) have no comprehensive SD statute located (Run 1 Part 1) — that gap is unchanged by this Part. - Medical/hemp boundary for operations: a medical manufacturing facility may not use hemp-based cannabinoids as additives (§ 44:90:07:10); a hemp processor may not use the medical testing/manufacturing rules as authority; and chemically derived cannabinoids are banned in both tracks (SDCL 34-20B-118 for sale/distribution; § 44:90:07:10 for medical manufacturing). Do not transfer a medical batch, manifest or Metrc procedure to a hemp lot, or vice versa.

  2. §§ 44:90:10:03 (edibles), :04 (tinctures/oils), :06 (topicals) full packaging text; whether beverage limits in § 44:90:10:05 (2021: 10/100 mg) were raised with the 2023 edible amendment; conformance of § 44:90:10:12(3)'s 10 mg serving reference to the 50 mg manufacturing limit.
