  15. Patient delivery: no delivery right located (Run 1, secondary only) — confirm against §§ 44:90:04:18–:23 and 44:90:08:04 in the Jan 2026 compilation before Run 2 states a ban as a rule.
  16. SDCL 34-20G-72(4)(l) batch-size language vs the § 44:90:09:07.01 tables: whether 50 lb is a free-standing cap or only the top table band.

Part 12 — Consumer and Social Rules

Consumer and social rules

Research / access date for every source: 30 October 2026. The 3 November 2026 election has NOT passed. No 2026 election result is stated anywhere in this report.

Scope and method. This is Part 12 only. It was checked against Run 1 (Parts 0–6, file ~/workspace/your_files/south-dakota-cannabis-hemp-run1/south-dakota-cannabis-hemp-run1-parts-0-6.html). Where this run found a different or more precise result than Run 1, the conflict is flagged explicitly (§12.15) and the directly opened official text is preferred. Official pages opened directly in this run are marked Opened directly. Everything else is Search-result / secondary — Verify. No personal phone numbers or emails are reproduced.

Fixed federal baseline (used as given, not re-researched). State-licensed medical marijuana moved to Schedule III effective 28 April 2026 (91 FR 22714); adult-use remains Schedule I; §280E is generally lifted for Schedule III medical activity and fully applies to adult-use; hemp PL 119-37 §781 takes effect 12 November 2026 but PL 119-103 §2019 limits it until 11 December 2026, after which the full total-THC definition and the 0.4 mg total THC per container cap apply; USDA DEA-registered-lab testing enforcement is delayed to 31 December 2026; the federal medical-marijuana appropriations rider runs through 11 December 2026; there is no federal cannabis banking law. Whether a tribal medical programme counts as "state-licensed" under the federal rule is Verify throughout.

Run 1 facts respected (not re-argued). Medical-only; SDCL ch. 34-20G; patient possession 3 oz, rolling 14-day dispensary limit of 3 oz of flower, home grow 2 flowering + 2 non-flowering plants on the grow property only; non-patient possession SDCL 22-42-6 (≤2 oz Class 1 misdemeanor; >2 oz to <½ lb Class 6 felony; ½ lb to <1 lb Class 5; 1–10 lb Class 4; >10 lb Class 3; civil penalty up to $10,000); ingestion SDCL 22-42-5.1 as amended by 2025 SB 83 (1st/2nd Class 1 misdemeanor with evaluation/treatment/probation; 3rd or subsequent Class 6 felony); paraphernalia SDCL 22-42A-3 Class 2 misdemeanor / 22-42A-4 Class 6 felony, with SDCL 34-20G-7 protection for supplying paraphernalia to cardholders; chemically derived intoxicating hemp banned (2024 HB 1125, SDCL 34-20B-118, Class 2 misdemeanor for conversion and for sale/distribution, not mere possession); smokable/inhalable hemp banned (SDCL 38-35-21, Class 1 misdemeanor); 2026 SB 61 (broader hemp-intoxicant ban + 0.4 mg state cap) FAILED; employment SDCL 34-20G-22/-24 as amended by 2024 SB 12; medical-purpose defence SDCL 34-20G-51/-53 (2025 HB 1124 repeal of §34-20G-53 FAILED Senate 17–18, 4 Mar 2025; 2026 HB 1065 DEAD).

12.1 Paraphernalia — what is protected, what is not

Non-patient rule — SDCL 22-42A-3, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/22-42A-3):

"No person, knowing the drug related nature of the object, may use or to possess with intent to use, drug paraphernalia to plant, propagate, cultivate, grow, harvest, manufacture, compound, convert, produce, process, prepare, test, analyze, pack, repack, store, contain, conceal, inject, ingest, inhale, or otherwise introduce into the human body any controlled substance or marijuana in violation of this chapter. Any person who violates any provision of this section is guilty of a Class 2 misdemeanor."

Source line: SL 1983, ch. 180, § 3; SL 1984, ch. 172, § 3; SL 1998, ch. 139, § 6. Class 2 misdemeanor = up to 30 days / $500 (Run 1 penalty scale). SDCL 22-42A-4 (delivery/manufacture with intent to deliver) is a Class 6 felony per Run 1 — its current text was not re-opened in this run; Verify before quoting it verbatim.

Cardholder / establishment protection — SDCL 34-20G-7, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-7; IM 26, eff. 1 July 2021):

"No person is subject to arrest, prosecution, or penalty of any kind, or may be denied any right or privilege, including any civil penalty or disciplinary action by a court or occupational or professional licensing board or bureau, for: (1) Providing or selling cannabis paraphernalia to a cardholder, nonresident cardholder, or to a medical cannabis establishment; (2) Being in the presence or vicinity of the medical use of cannabis that is exempt from criminal or civil penalty by this chapter; (3) Allowing the person's property to be used for an activity that is exempt from criminal or civil penalty by this chapter; or (4) Assisting a registered qualifying patient with the act of using or administering cannabis."

Practical reading. (a) A shop may lawfully sell paraphernalia to a cardholder, nonresident cardholder or establishment; §34-20G-7(1) does not on its face protect selling paraphernalia to a non-patient for unlawful use — that sale remains exposed under §22-42A-4. (b) A cardholder's own possession/use of paraphernalia for lawful medical use is protected through §§34-20G-2/-7, not through §22-42A-3 itself. (c) "Drug paraphernalia" for hemp: a device used for lawful hemp (e.g. a CBD tincture dropper) is not paraphernalia "in violation of this chapter" on its face; a device for smoking hemp is tied to the separately banned act (SDCL 38-35-21) — Verify whether prosecutors charge the device separately in smokable-hemp cases; no such charging practice was located. (d) Fentanyl test strips were exempted from the paraphernalia definition by 2023 HB 1041 (secondary, Drug Policy Watch brief) — Verify current §22-42A-1 definition; do not extend that exemption to cannabis paraphernalia.

12.2 Hemp-product age rules — including 2026 SB 45, now verified

Result: Run 1's "reported SB 45" is confirmed and upgraded from secondary-only to enacted law. 2026 SB 45, 2026 Session Laws ch. 151, signed 16 March 2026, amends SDCL 34-20B-117. Session law: https://mylrc.sdlegislature.gov/api/Documents/SessionLaw/305990.pdf?Year=2026 (search-result; enrolled text also at https://mylrc.sdlegislature.gov/api/Documents/305983.pdf). It does not create a general 21+ rule for all hemp: it covers only products intended for human consumption containing the listed substances.

SDCL 34-20B-117 as amended by SB 45 (enrolled text, search-result excerpt):

"(1) To knowingly sell or distribute a product intended for human consumption containing delta-8 tetrahydrocannabinol, delta-9 tetrahydrocannabinolic acid, THC-O acetate, or hexahydrocannabinol to a person under the age of twenty-one, unless the substance was obtained directly or pursuant to a valid prescription or order from a practitioner, acting in the course of the practitioner's professional practice; (2) The purchase or attempt to purchase, the receipt or attempt to receive, the possession, or the consumption of, a product intended for human consumption containing [the same four substances] by a person under the age of twenty-one, unless [same practitioner-prescription exception]; and (3) To purchase [such a product] on behalf of, or to give [such a product] to, any person under the age of twenty-one, unless: (a) The purchaser is a parent or guardian of the person under the age of twenty-one; and [further condition(s) truncated in the excerpt — Verify the full subdivision (3) exception lettering in the current §34-20B-117]. A violation of subdivision (1) is a Class 1 misdemeanor. A violation of subdivision (2) or (3) is a Class 2 misdemeanor."

What SB 45 changed. (a) It added delta-9 THCA to a pre-existing under-21 list (delta-8, THC-O acetate, HHC — and, per Dakota Scout reporting, delta-10 was already described as listed; the enrolled SB 45 text reproduced above lists four substances and does not include delta-10 in subdivisions (1)–(2) — Verify whether delta-10 sits in a separate pre-existing subsection or was dropped in engrossment; do not state the SB 45 list as five substances). (b) It reduced the seller penalty from the introduced Class 6 felony to a Class 1 misdemeanor (up to 1 year / $2,000), aligning it with alcohol sales to minors (Dakota Scout, 2026, secondary: https://www.thedakotascout.com/p/bill-to-ban-sale-of-pot-like-delta). The under-21 purchase/possession/consumption penalty is a Class 2 misdemeanor (up to 30 days / $500). (c) Effective date: 1 July 2026 per the Rapid City Sentinel July 2026 new-laws roundup (secondary: https://therapidcitysentinel.com/new-laws/) — Verify the session law's effective-date clause; South Dakota acts generally take effect 1 July unless stated otherwise, but the clause was not opened in this run. (d) Interaction with the bans: SB 45 does not legalise any product. Delta-8/THC-O/HHC products remain banned for everyone when chemically derived (SDCL 34-20B-118) and smokable hemp remains banned for everyone (SDCL 38-35-21); SB 45 adds a freestanding under-21 offence for the listed consumable products, including non-chemically-derived delta-9 THCA products that would otherwise sit in the hemp definition. A compliant, non-intoxicating CBD product (≤0.3% total delta-9, not chemically derived, not for smoking/inhaling) is not within §34-20B-117's list — no general 21+ CBD age rule was found; Verify whether any retailer or local ordinance imposes one voluntarily.

Medical cannabis age rules (for contrast, from Run 1, not re-opened except §34-20G-18 below). Cardholders under 21 may not smoke or vape cannabis (SDCL 34-20G-18(3)(c)); minors under 18 need custodial-parent/guardian consent and a designated caregiver (SDCL 34-20G-33). There is no freestanding "21 to buy medical cannabis" statute beyond cardholding itself — a 19-year-old cardholder may purchase non-smokable medical products from a dispensary. Verify ARSD 44:90 for any additional under-21 product restriction.

12.3 THC-impaired driving — no adult per se THC limit; under-21 zero tolerance; metabolite trap

Adult DUI — SDCL 32-23-1, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/32-23-1; last amendment shown SL 2006, ch. 168, § 1):

"No person may drive or be in actual physical control of any vehicle while: (1) There is 0.08 percent or more by weight of alcohol in that person's blood…; (2) Under the influence of an alcoholic beverage, marijuana, or any controlled drug or substance not obtained pursuant to a valid prescription, or any combination…; (3) Under the influence of any controlled drug or substance obtained pursuant to a valid prescription, or any other substance, to a degree which renders the person incapable of safely driving; (4) Under the combined influence…; or (5) Under the influence of any substance ingested, inhaled, or otherwise taken into the body as prohibited by § 22-42-15."

Analysis — read the subsections in order, because the common secondary shorthand is wrong in two ways. 1. No per se THC limit for adults. The 0.08% figure in subdivision (1) is alcohol only. There is no ng/mL THC blood or urine limit in §32-23-1. Adult cannabis DUI under subdivision (2) (marijuana "not obtained pursuant to a valid prescription") turns on being "under the influence" — in practice, officer observation, driving behaviour, field sobriety / drug-recognition evidence, admissions, and chemical testing together — not on crossing a number. Secondary law-firm summaries agree ("no per se limit for controlled substances"; Kolbeck Law, 2023, secondary: https://www.kolbecklaw.com/blog/2023/08/how-south-dakota-defines-drug-impairment-while-driving/). Do not import another state's 5 ng/mL rule. 2. A medical card is not a prescription and does not immunise driving. Medical cannabis is dispensed on a practitioner written certification, not a prescription (Run 1, Part 1.4). Subdivision (2) therefore captures a cardholder driving under the influence of marijuana; subdivision (3)'s "incapable of safely driving" qualifier applies to prescribed drugs, not to subdivision (2) marijuana. Separately, SDCL 34-20G-18(4) (below) denies chapter 34-20G protection for operating a vehicle "while under the influence of cannabis." A cardholder who is not under the influence — e.g. used days earlier, no impairment — is not within §32-23-1(2) on impairment alone, but faces the practical risk in §12.3(4). 3. Implied consent / testing. Any person operating a vehicle in South Dakota is deemed to have consented to withdrawal of blood or other bodily substance and chemical analysis to determine the presence of marijuana or any controlled drug/substance (SDCL ch. 32-23 implied-consent provisions, search-result excerpt from the ch. 32-23 index: https://sdlegislature.gov/api/Statutes/32-23.html?all=true). Refusal can be used as evidence and can trigger licence revocation by the Department of Public Safety — Verify the current revocation period and hearing procedure in §§32-23-10 to -11; the 30-day / 1-year figures in older secondary summaries are not re-confirmed here. There is no roadside THC breath test; testing is blood/urine, which detects metabolites long after impairment ends. 4. Metabolites — the trap, and a Run 1 conflict flagged. Run 1 quoted a §34-20G-18 proviso that "a registered qualifying patient… is not considered to be under the influence of cannabis solely because of the presence of metabolites… in insufficient concentration to cause impairment." The §34-20G-18 text opened directly in this run contains no such proviso. SDCL 34-20G-18 as opened (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-18; IM 26; SL 2022, ch. 116, § 2) lists only the five unauthorised-conduct categories (see §12.5). Flagged conflict: the metabolite proviso, if it exists in current law, sits in a different section (candidate: §34-20G-2 definitions / a DOH rule / a separate driving provision) or was in an earlier bill text — Verify its location before quoting it in the final dossier; do not cite it as §34-20G-18. What is certain from opened text is the opposite-direction rule for under-21 drivers (next paragraph) and the ingestion offence (§22-42-5.1), under which metabolite evidence can support a charge even for lawful out-of-state consumption (Run 1, Part 1.3; State v. Schroeder (2004) / State v. Whistler (2014) — Verify, not opened). 5. Under-21 drivers — SDCL 32-23-21, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/32-23-21; SL 1998, ch. 198, § 1, as amended through SL 2019, ch. 137, § 1):

"It is a Class 2 misdemeanor for any person under the age of twenty-one to drive, operate, or be in actual physical control of any vehicle: (1) If there is physical evidence of 0.02 percent or more by weight of alcohol in the person's blood…; or (2) After having consumed marijuana or any controlled drug or substance, other than a controlled drug or substance lawfully prescribed for the person, for as long as physical evidence of the consumption remains present in the person's body."

Licence suspension on conviction/adjudication: 30 days (1st), 180 days (2nd), 1 year (3rd+), with a court-ordered restricted privilege for employment/school/counselling on proof of financial responsibility. This is a true zero-tolerance / metabolite rule for under-21s: no impairment need be shown, only that physical evidence of consumption remains. A 20-year-old medical cardholder is not saved by the card under this section, because medical cannabis is not a "lawfully prescribed" controlled drug; the only express carve-out is a lawfully prescribed drug. Verify whether any court has read the cardholder protection into §32-23-21(2); none was located. 6. Smoking/consuming in a vehicle — SDCL 22-42-24, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/22-42-24; SL 2021, ch. 100, § 1 — this also resolves Run 1's "Verify enactment" flag for HB 1061's vehicle-smoking provision):

"While a motor vehicle is located upon a public highway or the right-of-way of a public highway, it is a Class 2 misdemeanor if any person operating or in actual physical control of a motor vehicle smokes or consumes marijuana or marijuana concentrate while the vehicle is being operated."

SDCL 22-42-25 (passenger use, same chapter index) was not opened — Verify its text; do not assume it mirrors §22-42-24. 7. DUI penalties (general, secondary — Verify current §§32-23-2 to -4). First-offence DUI is a Class 1 misdemeanor (up to 1 year / $2,000) in secondary summaries, second offence within 10 years a Class 1 misdemeanor with mandatory jail, third offence within 10 years a Class 6 felony, with a 10-year lookback (Enjuris DUI summary, secondary: https://www.enjuris.com/south-dakota/auto-accident-lawsuits/drunk-driving-laws-penalties-cases/). These penalty tiers were not confirmed from opened §§32-23-2/-3/-4 in this run — Verify before publishing a penalty table. Enhanced penalties for DUI causing injury/death (SDCL 22-16-41/-42, vehicular homicide/battery) are outside Part 12's scope but are the route by which impaired driving becomes a felony — Verify if the final dossier needs them. 8. Other vehicles. SDCL 34-20G-18(4) extends the no-protection rule to aircraft, trains and motorboats while under the influence. Commercial-vehicle / DOT testing is federal and outside this Part — Verify DOT rules separately; do not state a SD commercial THC limit. 9. Federal Schedule III effect on driving: none established. Nothing in the given baseline changes §32-23-1 or §32-23-21. A state-licensed medical user remains subject to state DUI law exactly as before; Schedule III does not create a driving privilege. Verify any future DOH/DPS guidance if the final dossier is updated after 2026.

12.4 Expungement and sealing — petition-based for most cannabis offences; automatic removal is narrow; no cannabis-specific automatic expungement

Headline: South Dakota has NO general automatic expungement of cannabis convictions and NO cannabis-specific expungement statute in force. The one bill that would have created automatic removal for Class 1 marijuana misdemeanors — 2022 SB 151 — was vetoed by Governor Noem and the veto override failed in the Senate 17–17 on 28 March 2022 (Vote Smart key-vote record, secondary: https://justfacts.votesmart.org/bills/SD/1/54/?p=3&s=state&resultsperpage=25; NORML veto report, 28 Mar 2022, secondary: http://norml.org/blog/2022/03/28/south-dakota-governor-noem-vetoes-expungement-legislation/amp/). Any secondary summary quoting SB 151's text ("Class 1 misdemeanors for the use or possession of marijuana… shall be automatically removed") as current law is quoting a vetoed bill, not §23A-3-34. The current §23A-3-34 opened directly below proves the point.

Automatic removal — SDCL 23A-3-34, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/23A-3-34; SL 2016, ch. 134, § 1; SL 2021, ch. 106, § 1 — the 2021 amendment reduced the wait from 10 years to 5):

"Any charge or conviction resulting from a case where a petty offense, municipal ordinance violation, or a Class 2 misdemeanor was the highest charged offense shall be automatically removed from a defendant's public record after five years if all court-ordered conditions on the case have been satisfied and the defendant has not been convicted of any further offenses within those five years. However, the case record remains available to court personnel or as authorized by order of the court and may be used as an enhancement in the prosecution of subsequent offenses as provided by law."

Consequences by offence class (apply the opened text, not the vetoed bill): - Paraphernalia (§22-42A-3, Class 2 misdemeanor) and under-21 hemp-product possession/consumption (§34-20B-117(2), Class 2) and under-21 driving (§32-23-21, Class 2) and vehicle smoking (§22-42-24, Class 2): automatically removed from the public record after 5 years if conditions are satisfied and no further conviction in that period — provided no higher charge was in the same case. "Removed from public record" ≠ destroyed: the record remains available to court personnel / by court order and may be used as an enhancement. - Simple possession ≤2 oz (§22-42-6, Class 1 misdemeanor) and 1st/2nd ingestion (§22-42-5.1, Class 1) and selling hemp-THC products to a minor (§34-20B-117(1), Class 1): NOT automatically removed under §23A-3-34, because a Class 1 misdemeanor is higher than a Class 2. Relief, if any, is by petition (§23A-3-27/-28 below), pardon, or suspended imposition — see below. - Felony possession / distribution / manufacture (§§22-42-5/-6/-7, Class 3–6 felonies): NOT automatically removed. Same petition/pardon/suspended-imposition routes only. - A case with mixed charges takes the highest charged offence: a Class 2 paraphernalia charge in a case that also charged Class 1 possession does not get automatic removal.

Petition expungement — SDCL 23A-3-27, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/23A-3-27; SL 2010, ch. 126, § 2, as amended through SL 2022, ch. 71, § 1, eff. 15 Mar 2022):

"An arrested person may apply to the court that would have jurisdiction over the crime for which the person was arrested, for entry of an order expunging the record of the arrest: (1) After one year from the date of any arrest, if no accusatory instrument was filed; (2) After one year from the date the prosecuting attorney formally dismisses the entire criminal case on the record; (3) At any time after an acquittal; or (4) Within one year from the date the prosecuting attorney formally dismisses the entire criminal case on the record upon a showing of compelling necessity."

Procedure — SDCL 23A-3-28, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/23A-3-28; SL 2010, ch. 126, § 3): at least 14 days before any hearing, a copy of the motion must be served on the prosecuting attorney, who may contest in writing and at the hearing; the movant pays a fee equal to the civil-action filing fee, waivable on a showing of indigency. UJS forms (secondary, Collateral Consequences Resource Center guide, updated 24 June 2026: https://ccresourcecenter.org/state-restoration-profiles/south-dakota-restoration-of-rights-pardon-expungement-sealing/): Instruction Sheet UJS-390, Motion for Expungement and Affidavit of Mailing UJS-391, Notice of Hearing UJS-393; filing fee $72 in that guide (secondary — Verify current UJS fee schedule; §23A-3-28 ties it to the civil filing fee, so $72 may be stale). The court standard in §23A-3-29/-32 (not opened — Verify): the court "may enter an order of expungement if satisfied that the ends of justice and the best interest of the public as well as the defendant… will be served" (CCRC paraphrase), and expungement means sealing, not physical destruction, and restores the person "to the status the person occupied before the arrest or indictment" (§23A-3-32, per CCRC).

Other relief routes (secondary, CCRC guide — Verify current sections before advising): - Petition after 10 years / age 75: CCRC summarises a petition route for misdemeanors and felonies where the petitioner is at least 75, or at least 10 years have passed since sentence/probation completion and any court-ordered programme — Verify the governing section (candidates §§23A-3-26 to -32); do not cite a 10-year petition rule as confirmed until the section is opened. The commonly repeated "75 years old and no offence in ten years" formulation in low-quality secondary pages conflates the petition standard with the Bureau of Criminal Statistics erasure categories and should not be quoted. - Suspended imposition of sentence (SDCL 23A-27-13, secondary): if the court suspended imposition and the defendant completed probation, no conviction is entered — the most complete relief for a first felony/misdemeanor, but it must have been granted at sentencing; it cannot be retroactively created. Verify §23A-27-13 text and whether it was available for the specific offence class. - Pardon / exceptional pardon (SDCL ch. 24-14; §24-14-8, secondary): felony relief via the Board of Pardons and Paroles and the Governor; after pardon, a Form UJS-391 petition follows. Verify current Board procedure. - Records of conduct that is no longer a crime (SDCL 23-6-8.1, secondary via National Reentry Resource Center): the Bureau of Criminal Statistics may destroy records for an incident that is no longer a crime at any time. This does not help most cannabis offences, because non-patient possession, ingestion and felony distribution remain crimes in 2026; it would matter only if a future legislature legalised the conduct. Verify §23-6-8.1's scope. - Effect of expungement: sealing, not destruction (SDCL 23A-3-26, per CCRC); sealed records remain available to court personnel / DCI / by court order and for enhancement. A person whose record was automatically removed under §23A-3-34 may not be held guilty of perjury/false statement for failing to recite the arrest/indictment (vetoed SB 151 would have added this expressly for marijuana; the current §23A-3-34 text opened above does not contain that sentence — Verify whether it sits in §23A-3-32 or another section before advising a client they need not disclose). - No retroactivity from SB 83: Run 1 records that 2025 SB 83 (ingestion reform) is not retroactive and does not expunge past ingestion convictions. A pre-July 2025 Schedule I/II ingestion felony therefore remains a felony conviction for expungement purposes even though the same conduct today would be a Class 1 misdemeanor. - Federal consequences survive state expungement: state sealing does not by itself restore federal firearms rights or erase a federal immigration / federal-employment consequence — Verify federal law separately; no SD statute claims otherwise.

12.5 Consumption rules — private, public, vehicles, establishments, parks, public buildings

State baseline — SDCL 34-20G-18, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-18; IM 26; SL 2022, ch. 116, § 2):

"This chapter does not authorize any person to engage in, and does not prevent the imposition of any civil, criminal, or other penalty for engaging in, the following conduct: (1) Undertaking any task under the influence of cannabis, when doing so would constitute negligence or professional malpractice; (2) Possessing cannabis or otherwise engaging in the medical use of cannabis in any correctional facility; (3) Smoking or vaping cannabis: (a) On any form of public transportation; or (b) In any public place or any place that is open to the public; or (c) If under the age of twenty-one; (4) Operating, navigating, or being in actual physical control of any motor vehicle, aircraft, train, or motorboat while under the influence of cannabis; or (5) Performing any safety-sensitive job under the influence of cannabis."

Reading the smoking/vaping ban carefully. §34-20G-18(3) bans smoking or vaping in the listed places. It does not on its face ban non-smokable medical use (edible, tincture, topical) in a public place — but (a) no consumption lounge or licensed public-consumption area exists in current law (Run 1, Part 4.1: no consumption-lounge licence type); (b) local ordinances can be broader (see Minnehaha example below); (c) a property owner may prohibit any use on their property (§34-20G-27(2)); and (d) a non-patient has no lawful consumption anywhere in any event. Do not state "patients may eat edibles in public" as a freestanding right — the accurate statement is that §34-20G-18(3) as written addresses smoking/vaping, and public edible use sits in a gap filled by local ordinance and property rules. Verify ARSD 44:90 for any broader consumption rule.

Establishment property — SDCL 34-20G-68, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-68; IM 26, eff. 1 July 2021): "A medical cannabis establishment may not permit any person to consume cannabis on the property of a medical cannabis establishment." No on-site consumption at dispensaries, full stop — including tribal-adjacent state-licensed sites. (Tribal consumption lounges, if any exist on tribal land under tribal law, are tribal-law matters — Verify in Part 10; do not import them into state law.)

Public buildings — SDCL 34-20G-27(4), opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-27; IM 26; SL 2022, ch. 124, § 1; SL 2022, ch. 125, § 1): nothing in ch. 34-20G requires a state or local government to allow any otherwise-permitted conduct "within a building owned, leased, or occupied by the state or local government." A city hall, courthouse, public school building or state office may prohibit medical use on its premises even by a cardholder.

Local public-consumption ordinances — example, not a statewide rule. Minnehaha County Ordinance (search-result PDF: https://www.minnehahacounty.gov/residents/countyOrdinances/cannabis/ProhibitConsumptionSmokingCannabisPublicPlaces.pdf, adopted 2021, unincorporated county only) prohibits consumption and/or smoking of cannabis, medical cannabis or cannabis products "in any public place or in any place that is open to the public" in unincorporated Minnehaha County, defining public place to include places of employment and common areas of multifamily residential buildings, enforceable by a fine up to the Class 2 misdemeanor fine and/or up to 30 days' imprisonment per violation. This is a county ordinance, not state law, and its current text / enforcement were not re-confirmed — Verify before citing it as current. Other cities/counties may have parallel ordinances; no statewide survey of local consumption ordinances was retrieved — Verify locality by locality (Part 3 method).

Parks and public land. No state statute specifically addressing cannabis consumption in state parks, municipal parks or federal land within South Dakota was located. The default rules apply: smoking/vaping in a "public place or place open to the public" is unprotected under §34-20G-18(3)(b); a park is a public place on any ordinary reading; park-specific rules, federal-land rules (federal law applies on federal land regardless of state medical status) and local ordinances may add penalties. Do not state a park-specific fine or a park exemption — Verify the relevant park authority's rules for any specific park. Note the separate drug-free zone enhancement (§12.10) is about manufacture/distribution/possession-with-intent near schools/youth facilities, not about a patient consuming in a park.

Private consumption. A cardholder may consume lawfully at home (and, for smoking/vaping, only where not otherwise prohibited — e.g. not in a smoke-free rental if the lease bans it, §12.7) and in another private place with the property owner's permission. A non-patient may not lawfully consume anywhere in South Dakota outside tribal land under tribal law. Private consumption does not create a right to be under the influence at work, while driving, or while performing a safety-sensitive task (§34-20G-18(1),(4),(5)).

Correctional facilities. §34-20G-18(2): possessing cannabis or engaging in medical use in any correctional facility is unprotected. Jail/prison medical-cannabis administration is therefore not a chapter 34-20G right — Verify DOC/UJS medical policies for any compassionate-use exception; none was located.

12.6 Visitors and tourists — nonresident cardholders, out-of-state patients, tribal tourism, bring-back

Nonresident cardholders — the only lawful visitor route (Run 1, Part 1.4, confirmed in structure). SDCL 34-20G-3 protects a confirmed nonresident cardholder (another state/district/territory/commonwealth/insular possession/country card, with DOH confirmation/registration — procedure and fee Verify, Run 1 gap) for transporting, purchasing, possessing or using medical cannabis in accordance with ch. 34-20G, up to 3 oz + the DOH product amount, designating no more than two dispensaries under DOH rules. Mere possession of another state's card, without DOH confirmation, is not the protection — that is the single most common visitor error. A visitor who is not a cardholder anywhere has no lawful purchase or possession route in state jurisdiction.

Out-of-state patients who use at home and then enter South Dakota — the ingestion trap. SDCL 22-42-5.1 criminalises knowing ingestion / having the substance in an altered state in the body unless obtained by valid prescription or otherwise authorised by ch. 34-20B. Run 1 records the doctrine (secondary case law, Verify): lawful out-of-state consumption can still be charged in South Dakota on metabolite evidence, and the statute provides venue where the substance is detected. A Minnesota or Montana medical patient who medicates lawfully at home and then travels to South Dakota is therefore not safe by virtue of the home-state card unless they are also a DOH-confirmed nonresident cardholder and their South Dakota presence/use is within ch. 34-20G — and even then, the ingestion charge turns on facts (where/when consumed, cardholder status at the time) that no secondary summary resolves. Do not advise visitors that a home-state card "covers" South Dakota.

Tribal tourism carry-off — the bright line. Flandreau (medical, Native Nations, accepts cards from any jurisdiction for purchase on tribal land under tribal law) and Pine Ridge / Oglala (medical + adult-use under tribal law, 21+, reservation-grown only, no off-reservation transport permitted under tribal rules) are tribal-law systems. Secondary reporting is consistent that products purchased on tribal land must be consumed there and cannot lawfully be taken off tribal land into state jurisdiction (Rapid City Post explainer, secondary: https://rapidcitypost.com/cannabis-defined-everything-to-know-about-the-plant; KFF Health News, secondary: https://kffhealthnews.org/public-health/tribal-land-recreational-marijuana-sales-where-alcohol-banned/ — KFF notes Pennington County sheriff's office said it had not arrested anyone on such transport charges, while Flandreau's tribal attorney general said state/local officers are charging people leaving Flandreau with cannabis). Reconcile, don't pick one: enforcement varies by county and by tribe; the law (state possession/ingestion offences apply off-reservation to non-cardholders) does not vary. A tourist who buys adult-use cannabis on Pine Ridge and drives to Rapid City is committing state possession (and, if they consumed, ingestion) offences on the Run 1 penalty scale unless independently protected as a state/nonresident cardholder. A tourist who buys medical cannabis at Flandreau and is also a DOH-confirmed nonresident cardholder is in a materially better position for possession up to the allowable amount — Verify whether DOH treats a Flandreau purchase as a qualifying dispensary purchase for the 14-day limit; no DOH guidance on this was located. Tribal cards are not state-recognised nonresident cards (2024 SB 141 to add them DIED; Run 1, Part 1.4).

Bring-back from legal states (Minnesota, Montana, Colorado, etc.). No "personal import" allowance exists. Bringing cannabis across the state line into South Dakota is distribution/possession under ch. 22-42 for a non-cardholder, and even a cardholder's transport is protected only "in accordance with this chapter" (§34-20G-3 for nonresidents; §34-20G-2 for cardholders) — Verify whether DOH guidance treats out-of-state-purchased product as within the allowable amount; no such guidance was located, so do not advise cardholders they may import their home-state supply. Federal transport law is an additional, separate exposure and is outside this Part.

Visitors and paraphernalia / hemp products. A visitor may lawfully purchase a compliant hemp product (≤0.3% total delta-9, not chemically derived, not for smoking) subject to the §34-20B-117 under-21 rules (§12.2) and the §38-35-21 smoking ban; a visitor may not lawfully purchase or possess smokable hemp or chemically derived intoxicants at any age. Hotel/motel rules are property rules (§12.7): a hotel may ban smoking/vaping on its property and a guest has no chapter 34-20G right to override it (§34-20G-27(2)).

12.7 Landlord and tenant — status protection with a wide landlord carve-out

Status protection — SDCL 34-20G-19, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-19; IM 26; SL 2022, ch. 122, § 1):

"A cardholder may not be refused enrollment by a school or a lease by a landlord, or otherwise be penalized by a school or landlord solely for the person's status as a cardholder, unless failing to do so would violate federal law or regulations or cause the school or landlord to lose a monetary or licensing-related benefit under federal law or regulation. This section does not prevent a landlord from imposing reasonable restrictions on the medical use of cannabis by a cardholder who resides at the landlord's property."

Mirror protection for the landlord — SDCL 34-20G-25, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-25; IM 26, eff. 1 July 2021): "No school, landlord, or employer may be penalized or denied any benefit under state law for enrolling, leasing to, or employing a cardholder."

What a landlord MAY do (opened text + Run 1): - Impose reasonable restrictions on a cardholder-tenant's medical use (§34-20G-19, last sentence) — the statute does not define "reasonable"; smoke-free rules, designated-use areas, odour/ventilation conditions and no-smoking lease clauses are the ordinary examples, but whether a blanket ban on all medical use (including non-smokable forms) is "reasonable" is Verify / fact-specific and was not decided in any case located. - Refuse cultivation on rental property outright — SDCL 34-20G-27(3), opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-27): "Nothing in this chapter requires: … (3) A landlord to allow the cultivation of cannabis on the rental property." A home-grow card does not override a lease cultivation ban. A tenant who grows in defiance of a lease ban faces eviction for lease violation, not a chapter 34-20G defence. - Refuse to allow smoking/vaping by guests/visitors — §34-20G-27(2): nothing requires a person/establishment in lawful possession of property to allow a guest, client, customer or other visitor to smoke or vape cannabis on or in that property. - Refuse a lease / penalise where federal law requires it — §34-20G-19's federal-law / federal-benefit exception. Federally subsidised housing (public housing, Section 8) is the paradigm: federal law still treats marijuana as unlawful, and housing authorities may (and, for public housing, generally must) treat use as a lease violation. Verify the specific housing programme's rules; do not state that §34-20G-19 protects a public-housing tenant — on the statute's own exception, it likely does not. - Enforce generally applicable lease terms (damage, odour nuisance, illegal activity by non-cardholders, fire-safety for extraction) through ordinary eviction law (SDCL ch. 43-32). No cannabis-specific eviction procedure was found — Verify ch. 43-32 notice periods for the specific ground.

What a landlord may NOT do: refuse a lease, or otherwise penalise a tenant, solely for cardholder status, unless the federal exception applies (§34-20G-19). "Solely" is doing real work: a landlord who cites status plus a legitimate, documented lease violation (e.g. smoking in a smoke-free unit, unauthorised cultivation) is not within the "solely" prohibition on the statute's face. Eviction standard: Verify whether any SD court has applied §34-20G-19 to an eviction; none was located.

Smoke-free housing. No SD statute creates a cannabis-specific smoke-free-housing regime. The ordinary framework applies: a landlord may designate a property smoke-free by lease; SD's general smoking laws (SDCL ch. 34-46, Clean Indoor Air) address tobacco and, by local ordinance, may extend to cannabis smoking in multi-unit common areas (see Minnehaha example, §12.5) — Verify ch. 34-46's current scope for cannabis; do not state that the statewide indoor-smoking ban covers cannabis without that check. Secondhand-smoke / odour complaints between tenants are lease/nuisance matters, not chapter 34-20G matters.

Commercial landlords. §34-20G-19 speaks to leasing to a cardholder (residential in context); commercial leases for establishments are governed by the lease plus local zoning/licensing (Run 1, Part 3). No commercial-tenant cannabis protection was found — Verify before advising an establishment tenant.

12.8 Firearms — federal prohibition stands on the given baseline; state law adds notice, not a state ban; HB 1036 signage is NOT confirmed law

Federal prohibition (given baseline + §34-20G-28.1's own statement). 18 U.S.C. §922(g)(3) prohibits a person who is an unlawful user of or addicted to a controlled substance from shipping, transporting, receiving or possessing a firearm or ammunition. ATF's position, as reflected in the §34-20G-28.1 notice itself, is that a current marijuana user is an unlawful user federally and that federal law does not exempt medicinal use. Effect of federal Schedule III (28 Apr 2026) on §922(g)(3): Verify — not established by the given baseline. Schedule III status does not by itself remove marijuana users from §922(g)(3)'s "unlawful user" category, because the category turns on unlawful use under federal law, and state-licensed medical use remains unlawful under federal law outside the specific Schedule III carve-outs; whether ATF or a court treats Schedule III medical use differently, and whether any post-April 2026 ATF guidance exists, was not located — Verify before advising any patient to acquire or possess a firearm. The constitutionality of §922(g)(3) as applied to marijuana users is actively litigated nationally (secondary reporting notes conflicting district/circuit outcomes, e.g. Daniels in the Fifth Circuit) — Verify current Eighth Circuit / Supreme Court posture; no SD-specific firearms ruling was located. This report takes no position on constitutionality.

State notice law — SDCL 34-20G-28.1, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-28.1; SL 2024, ch. 136, § 1 — i.e. 2024 HB 1024 as enacted; this resolves Run 1's "candidate" flag for §34-20G-28.1):

"Each application for a registry identification card and each application for a card renewal must include a notice that: (1) The Gun Control Act of 1968, 18 U.S.C. § 922 (January 1, 2024), prohibits any person who is an unlawful user of or addicted to any controlled substance, as defined by the Controlled Substances Act of 1970, 21 U.S.C. § 801, et seq., (January 1, 2024), from shipping, transporting, receiving, or possessing a firearm or ammunition; (2) Until marijuana is legalized under federal law, an individual who is a current user of marijuana is, under federal law, an unlawful user of a controlled substance; and (3) Federal law does not exempt the use of marijuana for medicinal purposes."

The HB 1024 bill text (search-result: https://mylrc.sdlegislature.gov/api/Documents/Amendment/261063.pdf?Year=2024) adds that the notice must be acknowledged by the separate signature of the qualifying patient or representative. Verify the current DOH application form carries that separate-signature acknowledgement; the statute section opened above states the notice requirement but the signature mechanics sit in the bill/ARSD layer.

Dispensary signage — HB 1036: NOT confirmed as law (correction to Run 1's open question). 2024 HB 1036 would have required every dispensary to post at each public entrance and each cash register/point of sale: "WARNING: Federal law prohibits the possession of a firearm by certain individuals who are users of or addicted to marijuana. See 18 U.S.C. § 922(g) (January 1, 2024)," with a $250-per-day civil penalty after notification, ceasing on AG certification that federal law no longer prohibits such possession (introduced text, search-result: https://mylrc.sdlegislature.gov/api/Documents/262165.html; House passage 42–27, 16 Jan 2024, secondary Vote Smart record). The current ch. 34-20G index opened in this run lists §34-20G-28.1 (notice) and then §34-20G-29 — there is no §34-20G-28.2 or dispensary-posting section. That is strong evidence HB 1036 did not become law (it evidently failed in the Senate or was not signed), but the final Senate disposition was not opened — Verify final HB 1036 journal before stating it "failed in the Senate" as fact; what can be stated now is that no dispensary-posting duty appears in current ch. 34-20G. Do not advise dispensaries they must post the HB 1036 sign, and do not cite the $250/day penalty as current law.

No state firearms ban for cardholders was found. South Dakota law does not add its own prohibition on cardholder firearm possession; the exposure is federal (§922(g)(3)), plus the state notice duty. Concealed-carry permitting interaction (whether a cardholder may obtain/retain a SD permit) was not located — Verify SDCL ch. 23-7 / sheriff permitting practice; do not state that a card automatically bars or preserves a permit.

12.9 Veterinary cannabis and CBD — no SD-specific veterinary cannabis law found

Finding: no South Dakota statute, ARSD provision or Board of Veterinary Medical Examiners guidance specifically authorising, prohibiting or regulating veterinary cannabis/CBD was located in this run — Unavailable / Verify. What can be stated from located law: - Human medical programme does not extend to animals. SDCL ch. 34-20G speaks to "qualifying patients" who are humans (practitioner = physician/PA/APRN; debilitating medical conditions are human conditions). A pet is not a cardholder, a veterinarian is not a ch. 34-20G "practitioner," and a human patient's medical cannabis may not lawfully be diverted to an animal. - Veterinary practice. SDCL 36-12-1 (search-result, LawServer compilation: https://www.lawserver.com/law/state/south-dakota/sd-laws/south_dakota_laws_36-12-1) defines practice of veterinary medicine to include, for a fee, diagnosing, prescribing or administering any drug/medicine/treatment for animals. A veterinarian who recommends/prescribes a cannabis product for an animal is therefore practising veterinary medicine and needs a lawful drug to prescribe. No FDA-approved cannabis-derived animal drug exists (the only FDA-approved cannabis-derived drug, Epidiolex/cannabidiol, is a human drug; its veterinary use would be extra-label and is governed by federal extra-label-use law — Verify FDA / AVMA position; do not state that Epidiolex may lawfully be prescribed for dogs in SD). - Hemp CBD for pets. A hemp-derived CBD product that is a compliant hemp product (≤0.3% total delta-9, not chemically derived, not for smoking/inhaling) is lawful as a hemp product under SDCL 38-35-1; that does not make it an approved animal drug, an approved animal feed additive, or a product a veterinarian may lawfully recommend as treatment. Animal-remedy registration (SDCL ch. 39-18) may apply to products marketed as animal remedies — Verify whether DANR / the Board treats pet CBD products as registrable animal remedies; no such determination was located. One low-quality commercial blog lists South Dakota as a "gray area" for pet CBD (Suzie's Pet Treats, secondary, undated: https://suziespettreats.com/blogs/blog/is-cbd-for-dogs-legal) — do not cite a pet-treat vendor as authority; it is noted only to show that no clear SD rule is in general circulation. - THC and animals. Giving a THC-intoxicating product to an animal has no lawful veterinary pathway in SD law located here and risks animal-cruelty / unlicensed-practice exposure depending on facts — Verify SDCL ch. 40-1 (animal cruelty) application; no case was located. Do not advise pet owners to dose pets with human medical cannabis or hemp-THC products.

12.10 Schools — student administration, school property, drug-free zones

Student administration — SDCL 34-20G-95, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-95; IM 26, eff. 1 July 2021):

"The Department of Education and the department shall establish policy to allow students who are medical cannabis cardholders to have their medicine administered in school in accordance with their physician's recommendation. This policy shall be implemented the first day of the new school year following passage of this chapter. The departments shall implement substantively identical provisions to Colorado Revised Statute 22-1-119.3 as of January 1, 2019."

Implementing rules — ARSD art. 24:80 (Medical Cannabis and Schools), 2021 draft as retrieved (search-result PDF: https://boardsandcommissions.sd.gov/bcuploads/24.80%20(Medical%20Cannabis%20and%20Schools)%20FINAL%20Draft%2005.13.2021%20(1).pdf — a 13 May 2021 public-hearing draft, NOT confirmed as the final adopted text; Verify the current art. 24:80 on rules.sd.gov before quoting any section as current). The draft's structure, which district policies mirror (e.g. Lennox School District medical-cannabis policy, secondary: https://files-backend.assets.thrillshare.com/documents/asset/uploaded_file/1427/Lennox/999f9478-976f-49fc-b69f-001e95573b45/3055_Medical_Cannabis.pdf?disposition=inline), is: - §24:80:02:01: on notification, a school district shall allow a registered designated caregiver to administer medical cannabis to a student cardholder on/in school property or at a school-sponsored activity. - Allowable forms: nonsmokable only in district policies (the draft's §24:80:02:02; district policy states "Medical Cannabis is cannabis in a nonsmokable form"). - Required notification/documentation (§24:80:02:03 in draft; district policy): caregiver notifies the district of intent; submits physician's written/signed certification stating recommended dosage, frequency/time and interval; presents student and caregiver registry cards; signs a liability release (district/personnel/volunteers released except for wilful/wanton conduct or reckless disregard of the treatment plan) and an acknowledgement that no school personnel are required to administer. - Restrictions (§24:80:02:05 in draft): no student may possess or self-administer on/in school property or at a school-sponsored activity, regardless of age — even a student 18+ must still have the designated caregiver administer (draft §24:80:02:07; district policy: "Self-Administration Forbidden… A student who has reached the age of 18 must still comply…"). The caregiver must not administer in a manner that disrupts the educational environment or exposes other students, and must remove any remaining cannabis from school property/activity afterwards. - No discipline solely for cardholder status (§24:80:02:09 in draft); school/personnel/volunteers are not required to administer or store (§24:80:02:10 in draft). - Change-in-status notice within 10 calendar days (district policy); possession/use inconsistent with the policy is subject to district discipline. Caveat: the 2021 ASBSD summary (secondary: https://asbsd.org/proposed-changes-med-cannabis/) describes a 2021 subcommittee proposal to flip the default (schools could prohibit administration unless they opt in by policy). That proposal's fate was not confirmed — Verify whether §34-20G-95 / art. 24:80 were later amended to an opt-in model; do not present the 2021 draft's "shall allow" duty as unqualified current law without that check. Federal-funding jeopardy (a district that allows administration risking federal funds) was raised in that proposal — Verify current DOE guidance on federal funding.

School / landlord status protection — §34-20G-19 (opened, §12.7): a school may not refuse enrollment or otherwise penalise a student solely for cardholder status, unless federal law/benefit requires otherwise. That protects status, not possession/use in violation of the administration policy.

School property — general prohibitions. §34-20G-18(2) (correctional facilities) does not mention schools; the school-property ban for students comes from art. 24:80 / district policy (possession/self-administration banned), and for non-students from ordinary possession law. A non-patient adult smoking on school grounds commits the ordinary possession/consumption offences; a cardholder adult employee's workplace rules are §34-20G-24/-28 (Run 1, Part 1.9). Verify whether any SD statute separately criminalises possession on school property for adults; none was located beyond the drug-free-zone enhancement below.

Drug-free zones — SDCL 22-42-19, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/22-42-19; SL 1992, ch. 168, § 2; SL 1998, ch. 140, § 1; SL 2023, ch. 82, § 19):

"Any person who commits a violation of § 22-42-2, 22-42-3, or 22-42-4, or a felony violation of § 22-42-7, if such activity has taken place: (1) In, on, or within one thousand feet of real property comprising a public or private elementary or secondary school or a playground; or (2) In, on, or within five hundred feet of real property comprising a public or private youth center, public swimming pool, or video arcade facility; is guilty of a Class 4 felony. The sentence imposed for a conviction under this section carries a minimum sentence of imprisonment in a state correctional facility of five years. Any sentence imposed under this section shall be consecutive to any other sentence imposed for the principal felony. The court may not place on probation, suspend the execution of the sentence, or suspend the imposition of the sentence of any person convicted of a violation of this section. However, the sentencing court may impose a sentence other than that specified in this section if the court finds that mitigating circumstances exist which require a departure from the mandatory sentence provided for in this section. [Finding must be in writing.] It is not a defense… that the defendant did not know the distance involved. It is not a defense… that school was not in session."

Scope — narrower than Run 1's summary suggested. §22-42-19 applies only to violations of §§22-42-2/-3/-4 (manufacture/distribution/counterfeiting of Schedule I/II substances) or felony violations of §22-42-7 (distribution/possession with intent of marijuana). It does not apply to simple possession (§22-42-6), to ingestion (§22-42-5.1), or to a cardholder's lawful medical use. Run 1's shorthand ("violation within 1,000 feet… as an additional Class 4 felony") is therefore corrected: it is a standalone Class 4 felony with a 5-year mandatory minimum (consecutive, no probation/suspension except on written mitigating-circumstances findings), not a generic enhancement on every cannabis offence near a school. Verify §§22-42-18/-20/-21 definitions (drug-free-zone terms, exceptions) before applying it to a specific site.

Establishment buffers vs drug-free zones — do not conflate. The 1,000-foot school buffer for establishments (SDCL 34-20G-55, Run 1 Part 3) is a licensing location rule. The 1,000-foot / 500-foot drug-free zone (§22-42-19) is a criminal provision for manufacture/distribution. They share a distance but not a function.

12.11 Probationers and parolees — practitioner attestation gate, not a ban, not a free pass

SDCL 34-20G-96, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-96; SL 2024, ch. 140, § 1 — i.e. 2024 SB 191 as enacted; this resolves Run 1's "reported passed House 63–7, Verify enactment" flag: SB 191 was enacted as ch. 140):

"In order for an individual who is serving a probationary sentence under the supervision of the Unified Judicial System or who is on conditional release or parole from a state correctional facility under the legal custody of the Department of Corrections to utilize medical cannabis, the individual's practitioner must attest that the use of medical cannabis is: (1) Consistent with the medical standard of care for the treatment of the individual's documented debilitating medical condition and any symptoms associated with the debilitating medical condition; (2) Reasonable in light of the practitioner's observation and the individual's physical examination, diagnostic test results, medical history, and reported symptoms; and (3) Reasonable in light of the risks and benefits of medical cannabis as compared to the risks and benefits of other treatment options for the individual's debilitating medical condition and any symptoms associated with the debilitating medical condition."

Practical reading. (a) A probationer/parolee is not barred outright, but the ordinary practitioner certification is not sufficient — the practitioner must make the three-part attestation above. (b) The attestation is a precondition to utilise, not a guarantee: a supervising officer / court / Board may still impose supervision conditions (e.g. abstinence, testing) and a positive test can still be a supervision violation if the attestation is absent or the use is outside it — Verify UJS/DOC supervision policy and whether a standard attestation form exists; none was located. (c) The 2025 repeal attempt — 2025 HB 1209 ("An Act to repeal authorization for the use of medical cannabis by a probationer or parolee") — was withdrawn at the prime sponsor's request, 5 Feb 2025 (BillTrack50 record, secondary: https://www.billtrack50.com/billdetail/1814526). §34-20G-96 therefore remains law; do not state that probationers/parolees were barred in 2025. (d) Federal supervision (federal probation/parole) is a separate system — Verify federal conditions; SD §34-20G-96 addresses UJS supervision and DOC custody only. (e) Correctional-facility possession remains unprotected (§34-20G-18(2)); §34-20G-96 does not authorise use inside a facility.

12.12 Child custody and family law — status alone is not neglect; best interests still govern

SDCL 34-20G-21, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-21; IM 26; SL 2022, ch. 123, § 1):

"No person may be denied custody of, visitation rights with, or parenting time with a minor solely because the person is a cardholder. There is no presumption of neglect or child endangerment for conduct allowed under this chapter, unless the person's behavior creates an unreasonable danger to the safety of the minor. Nothing in this chapter supersedes or otherwise affects custody decisions, visitation rights, or parenting time based upon the best interests of the child."

Application. (a) Lawful medical use, by itself, cannot be the sole basis for denying custody/visitation/parenting time and carries no presumption of neglect/endangerment. (b) The protection evaporates on facts: behaviour creating an "unreasonable danger to the safety of the minor" (e.g. impaired care, accessible product, driving impaired with a child, smoking around a child in a way a court finds dangerous) can still support a custody/visitation decision, and the court retains full best-interests discretion (SDCL ch. 25-5 factors, not re-opened here — Verify current best-interests factors if the final dossier lists them). (c) Non-patient / unlawful use has no §34-20G-21 protection at all; a court may treat unlawful use as a best-interests factor like any other substance use. (d) Child-protection / DSS involvement: no SD statute was located stating that lawful medical use alone is or is not "neglect" for DSS purposes beyond §34-20G-21's no-presumption rule — Verify DSS policy / SDCL ch. 26-8A definitions before advising on a DSS investigation. (e) Minor patients: a parent/guardian's consent and caregiver role (§34-20G-33) do not create a custody advantage or disadvantage beyond §34-20G-21; disputes between parents over a minor's medical use are best-interests disputes — Verify whether any SD case has decided a minor-cannabis custody dispute; none was located.

12.13 Health insurance, Medicaid, workers' compensation, medical care — no reimbursement duty; no disqualification from care

No reimbursement duty — SDCL 34-20G-27(1), opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-27): "Nothing in this chapter requires: (1) A government medical assistance program or private health insurer, workers' compensation insurance carrier, or self-insured employer providing workers' compensation benefits, to reimburse a person for costs associated with the medical use of cannabis." "Government medical assistance program" includes Medicaid on any ordinary reading. Consequence: a patient pays out of pocket; Medicaid, private insurance and workers' comp need not pay for cannabis, a card, or related costs. Whether a payer may voluntarily reimburse is not addressed — Verify any payer policy; none was located. The 2022 bill revising cost-reimbursement language (2022 SB 17, enacted per NCCI legislative tracker, secondary: https://www.ncci.com/Articles/Pages/II_LegislativeAnalysisState_SD.aspx) is the source of the current §34-20G-27(1) formulation — Verify SB 17's session-law chapter before citing it.

No disqualification from medical care — SDCL 34-20G-20, opened directly (https://sdlegislature.gov/Statutes/34-20G-20; IM 26, eff. 1 July 2021): "For the purposes of medical care, including organ and tissue transplants, a registered qualifying patient's use of cannabis in accordance with this chapter is considered the equivalent of the authorized use of any other medication used at the discretion of a practitioner and does not constitute the use of an illicit substance or otherwise disqualify a qualifying patient from needed medical care." Scope note: this is a state-law equivalence rule. It does not bind a federal programme, an out-of-state transplant centre, or a private hospital's clinical criteria beyond SD law, and it does not require any provider to recommend cannabis. Verify whether any SD hospital/transplant policy has tested §34-20G-20; none was located. Secondary summaries (Drug Policy Watch) that state "a patient cannot be denied a transplant listing solely for lawful medical cannabis use" are a fair paraphrase of §34-20G-20 within SD law, not a guarantee of listing.

Healthcare facilities. SDCL 34-20G-25.1 (healthcare and accredited prevention/treatment facilities) was not opened — Verify its text; do not state a hospital/hospice administration rule. A 2025/2026 bill to let terminally ill patients use medical cannabis in hospitals/hospices was reported rejected in committee (Drug Policy Watch, secondary) — Verify bill number/status; it is noted only to warn against assuming a hospital-use right exists.

12.14 Employment and drug testing — cross-reference (no contradiction with Run 1, Part 1.9)

Run 1, Part 1.9 is adopted without change: SDCL 34-20G-22 (as amended by 2024 SB 12, SL 2024, ch. 135) affords a registered patient the same rights as if solely prescribed a pharmaceutical for employer interaction and drug testing, except an employer may take adverse action or refuse to hire based solely on a positive metabolite test for a safety-sensitive job; SDCL 34-20G-24 requires no employer to allow cannabis in the workplace or work under the influence, permits compliant drug-free workplace policies, and creates no discrimination/wrongful-termination cause of action for compliant enforcement; SDCL 34-20G-23 preserves federal-law / federal-benefit conflicts; SDCL 34-20G-28 permits discipline for ingestion at work and working under the influence. Additions from this run (consumer-facing): (a) a positive metabolite test alone, for a non-safety-sensitive job, is the core protection — but an employer may still act on impairment at work, on a workplace ingestion, or on a federal obligation; (b) DOT / federal-contract / federal-grant testing is outside the state protection (SDCL 34-20G-23; SD Association of Healthcare Organizations guidance, Mar 2024, secondary: https://sdaho.org/wp-content/uploads/2024/03/Medical-Marijuana-Workplace-Protections_3112024.pdf — noting Medicare payments are not procurement contracts/grants and therefore do not automatically exempt a provider, while other federal grants/contracts may); (c) federal Schedule III does not by itself change SD employment testing — Verify any post-April 2026 DOT or federal-contractor guidance; none was located. Pre-employment testing of cardholders, "reasonable suspicion" standards, and unemployment-benefits consequences of a positive test were not located — Verify SDCL ch. 61-6 / DLR rules before advising.

12.15 Run 1 conflicts and corrections found in this run

  1. SB 45 — upgraded. Run 1: "reported, secondary-only, citation/section/effective date Verify." This run: 2026 SB 45 enacted, 2026 SL ch. 151, signed 16 Mar 2026, amending SDCL 34-20B-117; seller penalty Class 1 misdemeanor (not the introduced Class 6 felony); under-21 purchase/possession/consumption Class 2 misdemeanor; practitioner-prescription exception; parent/guardian giving exception (full lettering Verify). Effective date 1 July 2026 per secondary July-2026 roundup (Verify session-law clause).
  2. SB 191 / §34-20G-96 — upgraded. Run 1: "reported passed House 63–7, Verify enactment." This run: enacted, SL 2024, ch. 140, § 1; §34-20G-96 text opened directly. 2025 HB 1209 repeal attempt withdrawn 5 Feb 2025 — §34-20G-96 remains law.
  3. HB 1024 / §34-20G-28.1 — upgraded. Run 1: "candidate." This run: enacted, SL 2024, ch. 136, § 1; §34-20G-28.1 text opened directly (card/renewal application notice + federal firearms warning).
  4. HB 1036 dispensary signage — NOT confirmed law (correction to Run 1's open question). No §34-20G-28.2 or posting duty appears in the current ch. 34-20G index; the $250/day penalty must not be presented as current law. Final Senate disposition Verify.
  5. Vehicle smoking — enactment resolved. Run 1: "Verify enactment" for HB 1061 / §22-42-24. This run: §22-42-24 exists, SL 2021, ch. 100, § 1, text opened directly (Class 2 misdemeanor for driver/in-control smoking/consuming while vehicle operated on a public highway/right-of-way).
  6. Drug-free zone — narrowed. Run 1's shorthand suggested a general school-zone enhancement. Opened §22-42-19 applies only to §§22-42-2/-3/-4 and felony §22-42-7 violations, as a standalone Class 4 felony with a 5-year mandatory minimum (consecutive; probation/suspension barred except on written mitigating findings). Simple possession, ingestion and lawful medical use are outside it.
  7. Metabolite proviso — location conflict. Run 1 attributed a "not under the influence solely because of metabolites in insufficient concentration" proviso to §34-20G-18. The §34-20G-18 text opened directly in this run (SL 2022, ch. 116, § 2) contains no such proviso. Do not cite the proviso as §34-20G-18 until its true location is found. The under-21 metabolite rule (§32-23-21(2)) and the ingestion offence (§22-42-5.1) are unaffected by this flag.
  8. SB 151 automatic expungement — vetoed bill, not law. Any summary quoting SB 151's Class 1 marijuana automatic-removal text as §23A-3-34 is wrong. Current §23A-3-34 (opened directly) covers only cases where a petty offence, municipal violation or Class 2 misdemeanor was the highest charge, after 5 years.
  9. SB 45 substance list — delta-10 discrepancy. Enrolled SB 45 text reproduced here lists delta-8, delta-9 THCA, THC-O acetate, HHC. Secondary reporting describes delta-10 as already listed pre-SB 45. Verify the full current §34-20B-117 before stating a five-substance list.
  10. No contradiction found on: Run 1 penalty scales, §34-20G-19/-20/-21/-25/-27/-68 substance, §34-20G-7 paraphernalia protection, HB 1124/HB 1065 status, SB 61 failure, or the tribal-card / tribal-medical Schedule III Verify flags. Where Run 1 flagged a gap (DOH nonresident procedure, ARSD school rules final text, UJS expungement forms/fees, DOT testing, transplant-centre practice), this run preserves it below rather than filling it.

Location of the §34-20G metabolite proviso quoted in Run 1 (not in opened §34-20G-18); any DOH rule or separate section carrying it. 3. SDCL 22-42A-4 current text (delivery/manufacture paraphernalia, Class 6 felony per Run 1); SDCL 22-42-25 (passenger vehicle use); SDCL 22-42-18/-20/-21 (drug-free-zone definitions/exceptions). 4. Unavailable (no source found in this run): - A South Dakota statute creating a cannabis-specific expungement programme, a public-consumption licence, a park-specific cannabis rule, a veterinary cannabis authorisation, a state firearms ban for cardholders, or a Medicaid/insurance cannabis reimbursement duty. Each absence is stated as "none found," not as proof of a negative across every rule and local ordinance. - Official SD data on: cannabis DUI arrests/convictions by year; expungement petitions granted for cannabis offences; school medical-cannabis administrations; tribal carry-off charges by county. None was retrieved; do not estimate them. - A DOH/DPS statement reconciling the metabolite proviso (if any) with §32-23-21(2)'s under-21 physical-evidence rule and §22-42-5.1 ingestion.

(22) Interstate reciprocity conflicts: Minnesota medical visiting-patient rule (Leafwell/CannaCon “no recognition” vs Marijuana Herald October 2026 “visiting purchase pathway”); Montana medical visiting-patient rule (SalesTaxHandbook “yes, purchase” vs NuggMD “possession yes, buying no” vs older “critically ill only” framing); ND card-application rules for SD residents; NE dispensary implementation status at 30 October 2026; IA visiting-patient 4.5 g THC possession conditions (Iowa Code §124E); WY low-THC exemption thresholds/registration and full hemp rule; MN retail/non-resident rules and current retail status. (23) SD §34-20G-3 “transporting” scope (into-SD by confirmed nonresident vs out-of-SD by resident); SD cardholder “laundering” of Pine Ridge product (flagged, not stated); §22-42-7 distribution tiers; §22-42-24 vehicle-smoking enactment — note: confirmed in Part 12; §22-42-5.1 window (5 vs 10 years); §34-20G-43 card term; §34-20G-1(8) lettering after (g) for PTSD. (24) National hemp value conflict ($417m vs $445m, 2024) — do not republish both without resolution in the NASS 2024 National Hemp Report (primarily a Part 6 issue; noted here only if hemp economics are mentioned). (25) Noem 2019 hemp “four guardrails” exact list; 2020 State of the State hemp passage; March 2019 AG hemp/CBD statement original text; 2021 IM 26 task force / Rules Review Committee minutes (148 rules, 6–0 vote, 26 October 2021); first state-system dispensary name/city (July 2022); ARSD Art. 44:90 effective dates (5 October vs 15 November 2021). (26) 2024 Ismay repeal initiative petition for circulation — disposition (approval for circulation is not ballot certification; no evidence it qualified).

E. Cross-run corrections and conflicts — prominent summary (do not silently overwrite Run 1)

  • SB 45 now confirmed enacted (2026 SL ch. 151, signed 16 Mar 2026, amending §34-20B-117; seller Class 1 misdemeanor, under-21 Class 2) — was secondary-only in Run 1. (Part 12)
  • SB 191, HB 1024, §22-42-24 now confirmed (SB 191: SL 2024 ch. 140, §34-20G-96; HB 1024: SL 2024 ch. 136, §34-20G-28.1; §22-42-24: SL 2021 ch. 100) — were Verify in Run 1. (Part 12)
  • HB 1036 dispensary signage NOT confirmed in the current ch. 34-20G index (no §28.2) — do not present as law. (Part 12)
  • §34-20G-18 metabolite proviso location Verify: Run 1's proviso was NOT found in the directly opened §34-20G-18 text in Part 12; if it exists, it sits elsewhere — do not cite it as §34-20G-18. (Part 12)
  • Edible limits 50 mg/serving, 500 mg/marketing layer (ARSD 44:90:07:04, amended from 10/100) — was Verify in Run 1. Product-equivalency table resolved (edibles 2,000 mg THC = 1 oz, oil/tincture 15 g = 1 oz, etc.). (Part 11)
  • 2026 rules package filed, eff. 5 Nov 2026, not in force 30 Oct 2026 — “38 proposed” (Run 1) vs “35 sections” (Register) unreconciled. (Parts 9 and 11)
  • Run 1 “polling Unavailable” superseded for pre-2023 Mason-Dixon polls only (Oct 2020, Oct 2021, July 2022); 2024–2026 polling remains Unavailable. (Part 9)
  • HB 1101 bill number found (Ismay repeal, committee kill 7–6, 18 Feb 2025, journal Verify). HB 1160 Senate disposition conflict (committee blocked 4–3, floor disposition unknown). (Part 9)
  • Flandreau 2015 crop-burn reason now confirmed via Lakota Times (consultant/transport error, facility saved by burning ~600 plants) — was “reason not confirmed” in Run 1. (Part 10)
  • Hemp Quarters 605 PI denied 29 June 2024, merits still Verify. (Part 9)
  • Grower minimum acreage conflict unresolved (0.5 acre/300 plants vs 5 contiguous acres excerpt). Camera retention 90 vs 180 days by jurisdiction. (Parts 7–8)
  • 2022 SB 151 automatic marijuana expungement was vetoed and the override failed 17–17, so current §23A-3-34 automatic removal covers only petty/Class 2-misdemeanor-highest cases after 5 years; no cannabis-specific expungement programme is in force. (Part 12)
  • No adult per se THC driving limit (§32-23-1 impairment standard) but a true under-21 zero-tolerance/metabolite rule (§32-23-21). (Part 12)
  • No SD-specific veterinary cannabis law found; schools use caregiver-only, nonsmokable administration under §34-20G-95 / ARSD art. 24:80 (2021 draft only — final text Verify). (Part 12)
